# EMBLEMS
<note>original Latin: "Emblemata." An emblem book is a genre of Renaissance literature that combines allegorical illustrations with explanatory text and moralizing poems.</note>

## BY ANDREA
## ALCIATO
<note>Andrea Alciato (1492–1550) was a famous Italian legal scholar. He is best known today as the "father" of the emblem book, having created the first and most influential collection of its kind.</note>

<summary>This is the decorative front cover of Andrea Alciato's "Emblemata," the foundational work of the emblem book genre. The gold-leaf lettering on dark leather identifies the author and the title, marking the beginning of a work that would be reprinted and imitated throughout Europe for centuries.</summary>

<keywords>Andrea Alciato, Emblems, Renaissance literature, bookbinding, gold tooling, allegory</keywords>

# EMBLEMS
<note>original: "Emblemata". This term refers to a 16th-century genre of "picture books" for adults, where a symbolic illustration is paired with a motto and a moralizing poem.</note>

## OF ANDREA ALCIATI
<note>Andrea Alciati (1492–1550) was an Italian humanist and legal scholar who is considered the "father" of the emblem book.</note>

->The most renowned<-
->legal expert.<-
<note>original: "Iurisconsulti clarissimi". While famous for his poetry, Alciati's primary profession was as a prestigious professor of law.</note>

# EMBLEMS

## BY ANDREA ALCIATO

->The most renowned <term>legal scholar</term>.<-
<note>original: "I. C. Clariss." / "Iurisconsulti Clarissimi." Andrea Alciato (1492–1550) was a premier Italian jurist. His "Emblemata" combined legal precision with humanist poetry, creating a new literary genre where images and text worked together to provide moral lessons.</note>

->Third edition<-
->revised and expanded<-
->by the author himself.<-
<note>In the 16th century, the phrase "by the author himself" (ab ipso Auctore) was a crucial marketing claim, assuring the buyer that this edition was more accurate and complete than the numerous pirated versions circulating at the time.</note>

->With most comprehensive<-
->indexes.<-
<note>original: "Cum indicibus locupletissimis." Literally "most rich" indexes. These were vital tools for Renaissance readers to quickly find specific moral themes, mythological figures, or historical anecdotes within the collection.</note>

<note>The ornate woodcut border described in the image—featuring a canopy, a satyr, cherubs, and a classical ship—frames the title with classical and mythological authority. The ship at the bottom is a common Renaissance symbol for the "Ship of State" or the journey of the soul through the "waves" of life, themes Alciato frequently addressed.</note>

<summary>This page is the formal title page for a revised and expanded "Third Edition" of Andrea Alciato's *Emblemata*. It establishes Alciato's authority as a renowned legal scholar and promises a highly organized, comprehensive collection of his influential moral symbols.</summary>
<keywords>Andrea Alciato, Emblems, legal scholar, third edition, Renaissance printing, Lyon, woodcut border, moral philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page and provides a preface by the editor or publisher. It introduces Andrea Alciato not only as an author but as a preeminent legal scholar, establishing the intellectual weight behind these "playful" emblems. It also explains the new organization of the book into thematic categories (commonplaces), which was a departure from earlier, less organized editions.</meta>

<image-desc>The text is framed by an elaborate architectural woodcut border. At the top center is a winged grotesque head within scrolling foliage. The vertical sides of the frame feature two female herms (female busts atop decorative pedestals) with scrolling bases, and urns resting on the pillars above them. # TO THE READER.

The *Emblems* of **ANDREA ALCIATO**, a Nobleman of Milan—who is the most eloquent of <term>legal scholars</term> and the most legally expert of the eloquent—were produced as a pastime by that man, who never took a holiday from his literary pursuits even when taking a breather from the more demanding study of the laws. While being refreshed by the more pleasant Muses of human literature, he poured these out <note>original: "ἀφορίστως, καὶ ἀνακόλουθως" / "aphoristikōs, kai anakolouthōs"</note> in brief, disconnected parts and without a set order, just as various subjects offered themselves either to his external senses or to his internal reflections. 

These we have arranged under general headings of the principal matters into <term>commonplaces</term>, as if into certain classes, proceeding from the highest things to the lowest. We did this certainly not with the intention of appearing more diligent or more elegant than the author himself in the organization of his work, but rather to bring his "playful diversions" into common use. In doing so, we have kept a twofold account of this work: namely, its pleasure and its utility. In the case of the former, we aimed to ensure that a more beautiful form of the work might stand out to the eyes of the rea- <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("lectoris") at the bottom of the page.</note>

<note>Alciato is described using a clever rhetorical flip: he is the "most eloquent of lawyers" (praising his style) and the "most expert lawyer among the eloquent" (praising his knowledge). This highlights the Renaissance ideal of combining deep professional knowledge with the "humanities" (literature and art).</note>

<note>The term "commonplaces" (locos communes) refers to a Renaissance method of organizing knowledge. Instead of reading a book from start to finish, readers would use these headings (like "Virtue," "Fortune," or "Greed") to find specific moral lessons for use in their own writing or speeches.</note>

<summary>This preface introduces Andrea Alciato as a brilliant lawyer who wrote these emblems as a refined hobby. The editor explains that they have reorganized Alciato’s originally random poems into thematic "commonplaces" to make the book more useful and pleasing for the reader.</summary>
<keywords>Andrea Alciato, preface, legal scholar, commonplaces, organization, eloquence, Renaissance education</keywords>

The bottom of the border contains two reclining male nude figures amidst scrolling strapwork and stylized foliage, flanking a central blank cartouche.</image-desc>

*at things and peoples, each placed in its own location,*
*might be viewed by those looking on. For things composed*
*from what was scattered, and ordered from what was*
*confused, generally tend to appear more beautiful.*
*The second reason, however, was so that discovery*
*might be easier and readier for those searching. For*
*it is more efficient to seek out things of various kinds*
*when they are arranged in their own order and place,*
*than to hunt through a crowd recklessly heaped together,*
*whenever we have a need to use them. Furthermore, the*
*use of Emblems—beyond the grace and pleasure*
*derived from a delightful novelty that relieves boredom, the*
*brief wit of a maxim <note>original: "sententiæ argutiam"</note> that pricks the mind, the*
*rhythmic sweetness of verses that charms the ears, and the*
*meaningful picture of the **images** <note>original: "τῶν εἰκόνων" (tōn eikonōn)</note> which*
*feeds the eyes—has also this use (I say): that whenever*
*someone wishes to provide completion to empty things, ornament*
*to naked things, speech to silent things, or reason to irrational*
*things (or at least to devise them), he might have*
*from this little book of Emblems—as if from a*
*most well-stocked storeroom <note>original: "promptuario"</note>—material for his home’s*
*walls, glass windows, wall-hangings, **tapestries** <note>original: "peristromatis"</note>,*
*paintings, vessels, statues, **signet rings**, garments,*
*the table, the bed-frame, weapons, the sword, and household goods...* <note>The Latin text ends with the prefix "supel-", indicating the start of "supellectilem" (household furniture/goods).</note>

<summary>This page explains the publisher's decision to organize the emblems into thematic categories for easier reference and highlights the "pattern book" utility of emblems for decorating household items.</summary>
<keywords>Emblems, thematic organization, utility of art, interior decoration, Renaissance aesthetics, pattern book, Alciato</keywords>

<image-desc>The Latin text is enclosed within a detailed woodcut border in the grotesque style. At the top, a central decorative mask with foliage is flanked by two reclining winged putti. The vertical sides of the frame feature ornamental pilasters with human faces (mascarons) and scrollwork. ...and furniture; finally, he can engrave and stamp them everywhere, so that the appearance of things intended for common use might be everywhere eloquent and pleasing to the sight. For this reason, **Alciatus** <note>Andrea Alciato (1492–1550), the Italian jurist who created the emblem book genre.</note> himself wished these epigrams of his to be titled by the most fitting name: *Emblemata* <term>Emblems: from the Greek 'emblema', meaning something inlaid or inserted, like a mosaic tile.</term>. For Emblems are **mosaic works** <note>original: "vermiculata opera," literally "worm-like work," referring to the winding patterns of fine mosaic tiles.</note> fitted and composed from inlaid pieces, according to the interpretation of **Budé** <note>Guillaume Budé (1467–1540), a leading French humanist scholar and contemporary of Alciato.</note>, as the very origin of the Greek word shows. 

Whoever, therefore, wishes to decorate his possessions with the sharp wit of a brief maxim and a festive image will find in this little book an abundance from which he can take what seems adaptable to any matter, and that most promptly and easily. This is possible because the commentary has been adapted to the primary categories of things and the individual forms of those categories, each arranged in its own order according to the natural series of things and common use. An **index table** for these has also been placed at the front of the entire work, which has been purged of the many great errors that had corrupted it, and fully restored **to the original manuscript** <note>original: "εις τὸν αὐτόγραφον" (eis ton autographon), meaning the text has been corrected against the author’s own handwriting or original version.</note>.

<meta>catchword: ry</meta>

<keywords>Andrea Alciato, Guillaume Budé, etymology of emblem, mosaic work, thematic organization, manuscript restoration, Renaissance book design</keywords>

## A 3

# TO THE MOST ILLUSTRIOUS MAN
## *ANDREA ALCIATO*
### Preface to the first book of Emblems, to Konrad Peutinger of Augsburg. <note>Konrad Peutinger (1465–1547) was a celebrated German humanist, jurist, and diplomat who served as a counselor to the Holy Roman Emperors Maximilian I and Charles V.</note>

->☩<-

->While the walnut distracts boys, and the die deceives youths,<- <note>Walnuts were used in Roman times for games similar to marbles, symbolizing childhood innocence or triviality.</note>
->And the painted playing card holds the interest of idle men:<-
->We forge these Emblems in our leisure hours,<-
->Signs crafted by the illustrious hands of artists:<-
->So that each may pin borders to garments and badges to hats,<-
->And be able to write using silent marks.<- <note>original: "tacitis notis," referring to the idea that an image can communicate a complex moral or philosophical concept without needing to speak aloud.</note>
->While the supreme Emperor grants you precious coins,<- <note>Peutinger was a famous collector of Roman coins and antiquities, often supported by the Emperor.</note>
->And gifts you the exceptional works of the ancients.<-
->I, a poet, shall give paper gifts to you, a poet,<-
->Which, Konrad, you may keep as a token of my affection.<-

<vocab>Andrea Alciati, Konrad Peutinger, Emblems, Latin poetry, Renaissance humanism, epigram</vocab>

<summary>This page contains the famous dedication poem of Andrea Alciato’s "Emblemata," where he presents his work to the humanist Konrad Peutinger. Alciato frames his collection of "silent marks" as an intellectual pastime for adults, contrasting them with the simple games of children and the idle distractions of gambling.</summary>

<keywords>dedication, Konrad Peutinger, Andrea Alciato, emblems, visual language, Renaissance games, imperial patronage, friendship</keywords>

This page focuses on the myth of Ganymede, interpreted through a theological lens.</meta>

<header>GOD, OR RELIGION.</header>

### GOD, OR RELIGION,

->To rejoice in God.<-

---

->TO REJOICE IN DIVINE COUNSELS,<- <note>original Greek: "ΓΑΝΝΥΣΘΑΙ ΜΗΔΕΣΙ" (*Gannysthai medesi*). This is a wordplay on the name Ganymede, suggesting that the name itself means "to take joy in [divine] counsels."</note>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting the myth of Ganymede. An eagle (symbolizing Jupiter) is shown with its wings spread, carrying the young Ganymede through the sky toward the clouds. In the landscape below, a dog sits on the ground, looking up at the eagle and boy. The background features a coastline with a city, mountains, and ships on the water. The word "GANYMEDES." is printed in the middle of the scene. The entire page, including the text and central image, is enclosed within a highly ornate woodcut border featuring lions, rabbits, scrolling foliage, and decorative swags of fruit.</image-desc>

---

> See how the distinguished painter has represented the Trojan boy <note>Ganymede, a prince of Troy (Ilium), famed for his beauty.</note>
> Being carried through the highest stars by the bird of Jove. <note>The eagle was the traditional animal of Jupiter (Jove).</note>
> Who would not believe that Jove was touched by a boyish love?
> Tell me, from where did the old Maeonian <note>original: "Mæonius senex," referring to the poet Homer, who was said to be from Maeonia (Lydia).</note> devise these things?
> He whose joys are provided by the counsel and mind of God:
> He is believed to have been snatched away to be present with highest Jove. <note>Alciato provides a Christian/Neoplatonic interpretation: Ganymede represents the human soul or intellect being elevated to God through the joy of divine wisdom, rather than a literal physical abduction.</note>

<sig>A 4</sig>

<vocab>Ganymede, Jupiter, Religion, Maeonian (Homer), emblem, To rejoice in divine counsels</vocab>

<summary>This page presents the emblem of Ganymede, transforming the classical myth of his abduction by Jupiter into an allegory for the soul's ascent to God through joy and divine wisdom. It establishes the "Religious" section of the book by moralizing pagan mythology.</summary>

<keywords>Ganymede, Jupiter, allegory, Neoplatonism, divine wisdom, Homer, Trojan boy, eagle, religious emblems</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page follows the discussion of Ganymede on page 7, which Alciato interpreted as the soul finding joy in God. Here, he provides the inverse: a warning against "human wisdom" that remains tethered to the earth. Where Ganymede was carried to the heavens, the figure on this page is physically bound to the ground by serpent coils.</meta>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>GOD, OR RELIGION.</header>

## Human wisdom is foolishness before God.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within an ornate rectangular frame featuring architectural scrolls, clusters of grapes, and grotesque animal masks. The central image shows an "anguiped" creature—a man from the waist up with a long, scaly, serpentine tail. He is situated in a body of water, pointing his right hand toward the sky. The background contains a rugged landscape with ruins and a monkey perched atop a tall pedestal. The scene illustrates the emblem's theme of human wisdom versus divine truth.</image-desc>

> What shall I say? By what name shall I call this two-formed monster,
>     Which is not a man, nor is it a dragon:
> But a man without feet, and a snake without its upper parts?
>     He can be called a snake-footed man, and a human-headed snake.
> The man walks by means of a snake; the snake has discharged <note>original: *eructauit*, literally "belched forth," describing how the human torso emerges from the serpent's body.</note> a man,
>     There is no end to the man, nor a beginning to the beast.
> Thus did <term>Cecrops</term> <note>The mythical first king and founder of Athens, said to have been born from the earth with a human torso and a serpent's tail. He represents ancient, earth-born knowledge.</note> once rule in learned Athens,
>     Thus too did Mother Earth bring forth the <term>giants</term> <note>The *Gigantes*, the monstrous children of Gaia (Earth) who famously waged war against the Olympian gods; they were often depicted with serpentine legs.</note>.
> This is the appearance of a clever man, but one who lacks religion,
>     And who cares only for earthly things.

**Vocabulary:** <term>Cecrops</term> (the first king of Athens), <term>giants</term> (earth-born rebels), <term>snake-footed</term> (having serpents for legs), <term>Human wisdom</term>, <term>foolishness</term>, <term>religion</term>, <term>two-formed</term>, <term>human-headed</term>.

<summary>This page uses the mythological figure of the snake-footed Cecrops to illustrate that worldly intelligence without religious devotion is monstrous and earth-bound. It warns that "human wisdom" is merely a clever form of folly if it neglects the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Cecrops, giants, earthly wisdom, hybridity, secularism vs religion, Athens, monstrous figures</keywords>

## False Religion.

->Sitting on a royal throne, the most beautiful harlot<-
->Wears a robe <note>original: *peplum*, a Greek-style draped garment often associated with high status or ritual.</note> distinguished by the honor of purple.<-
->She offers wine <note>original: *latices*, literally "liquids," but here referring to the intoxicating wine of her fornications.</note> to all from a full chalice,<-
->But a drunken crowd lies sprawled all around her.<-
->In this way they depict <term>Babylon</term> <note>A reference to the "Whore of Babylon" from the Book of Revelation (chapter 17), a symbol of worldly corruption and false religious systems.</note>, who with her enticing beauty<-
->And a false religion captures the foolish.<-

<vocab>**False Religion** <note>original: *Ficta Religio*</note>, **Babylon** <note>original: *Babylona*</note>, **harlot** <note>original: *meretrix*</note>, **robe** <note>original: *peplum*</note>, **chalice** <note>original: *cratere*</note></vocab>

<summary>This page uses the biblical figure of the Whore of Babylon to personify "False Religion," warning that spiritual deception seduces the world through luxury and intoxicating rituals. It serves as a moral counterpoint to true devotion, illustrating how easily the "foolish" are led astray by outward beauty.</summary>

It uses a classic fable to illustrate that the honor given to a religious office or object belongs to God, not to the person (or beast) conveying it.</meta>

<page-num>10</page-num>
<header>GOD, OR RELIGION.</header>

## Not to you, but to religion.

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut illustration set within a large, decorative architectural border. The central image depicts a fable: a donkey (ass) carries a small, canopied shrine containing a statue of a goddess (Isis) on its back. To the right, several people—men, women, and a child—kneel in prayer and adoration toward the animal. Behind the donkey, a man (the driver or agaso) stands with a stick or whip raised. The background shows classical city buildings. The elaborate border surrounding the entire scene features a grotesque mask at the top center, two large hooded or draped figures in niches on the left and right, and another mask at the bottom center supported by a figure with large scrolls.</image-desc>

A slow little donkey was carrying an image of <term>Isis</term> <note>The ancient Egyptian goddess; in this context, she represents any divine or sacred object.</note>,
Bearing the venerable <term>mysteries</term> <note>original: *mysteria*; sacred objects or hidden rites of a religious cult.</note> upon its bowed back.
Therefore, whoever met them on the road reverently adored the Goddess
And offered up pious prayers on bended knees.
But the donkey believes such great honor is being paid
To himself: and he swells up, becoming quite proud,
Until the driver <note>original: *agaso*, a term for a stable-boy or a driver of beasts of burden.</note>, restraining him with whips, said:
"You are not a God, little donkey, but you carry a God."

<summary>This emblem uses a fable about a donkey carrying a statue of Isis to warn religious officials and leaders not to mistake the respect shown to their office for personal merit.</summary>
<keywords>humility, religious office, Isis, vanity, donkey, Alciato, emblems, divine service</keywords>

->One must go where the Gods call.<-

# V I R T U E S .

## F A I T H .

### The symbol of Faith.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration framed by a highly decorative border. The central image depicts three figures in a landscape: on the left, a man symbolizing Honor, dressed in draped classical garments; on the right, a woman symbolizing Truth (Veritas), also in classical robes. They are standing and joining their right hands over the head of a small, nude child standing between them, who represents Love (Amor). The child wears a floral wreath around his head. The surrounding border is filled with ornate Renaissance-style scrollwork, grotesque masks (including a horned satyr-like head at the top and another grotesque face at the bottom), and female busts integrated into the side pillars.</image-desc>

->Let Honor be depicted here, veiled in a Tyrian cloak,<-
<note>original: "tyrio uelatus amictu"; Tyrian purple was a costly dye associated with high status, nobility, and integrity.</note>
->And let naked Truth join her right hand to his.<-
<note>original: "nuda... Veritas"; Truth is traditionally depicted as "naked" because she has nothing to hide and requires no embellishment.</note>
->In their midst let chaste Love stand: around whose temples<-
->The rose entwines, more beautiful than the Cupid of Dione.<-
<note>original: "Diones pulchrior Cupidine"; Dione was the mother of Venus (Aphrodite). The "Cupid of Dione" represents carnal or earthly desire, which this "chaste Love" surpasses.</note>
->These figures represent Faith, which the reverence of Honor<-
->Fosters, Love nourishes, and Truth brings forth.<-

<vocab>**Virtues** <note>moral excellences</note>, **Faith** <note>trust or belief</note>, **Honor** <note>integrity and reputation</note>, **Truth**, **Love**, **Dione** <note>mother of Venus</note>, **Cupid** <note>god of desire</note>, **Symbol** <note>a representative sign</note></vocab>

<summary>This page presents an allegorical emblem of Faith (Fides), personified through the union of Honor, Truth, and Chaste Love. It suggests that true Faith is an offspring of Truth, sustained by the integrity of Honor and the nourishment of Love.</summary>
<keywords>Faith, Honor, Truth, Love, allegory, virtues, Tyrian purple, Cupid, emblems</keywords>

### Treaties.

<image-desc>An emblem set within a detailed architectural frame. The central illustration depicts a stringed instrument (a cithara or lute) and several books resting on a cloth-covered bed or bench in an interior setting with curtains. The frame features fluted columns, an ornate pediment with a mask, and a base decorated with a grotesque face surrounded by scrolls and foliage.</image-desc>

->*This cithara, which from its boat-like shape is called "fishing-style,"*<-
<note>original: "lembi quæ forma halieutica"; the poet compares the rounded back of the lute to the hull of a small boat or skiff (lembus).</note>
->*The Latin Muse claims as her very own,*<-
->*Receive it, Duke; let this gift of ours please you at this time,*<-
<note>original: "Dux"; likely referring to a specific ruler or leader the author is addressing, emphasizing the "gift" of political wisdom.</note>
->*When you prepare to enter into new treaties with your allies.*<-
->*It is difficult, except for a learned man, to stretch so many strings:*<-
->*And if even one string is not well-tuned,*<-
<term>string (fides): In Latin, the word "fides" is a pun; it means both a musical string and the virtue of "faith" or "trust."</term>
->*Or if it breaks (which happens easily), the whole beauty of the instrument perishes,*<-
->*And that excellent song will become discordant.*<-
->*In this way the Italian princes join together in treaties; harmonious,*<-
->*There is nothing for you to fear, if love remains constant for you.*<-
->*But if anyone defects (as we see happen all too often),*<-
->*All that harmony is dissolved into nothing.*<-

**Vocabulary:**
**Treaties** (fœdera), **cithara** (lute/stringed instrument), **The Latin Muse** (Musa Latina), **Duke/Leader** (Dux), **faith/string** (fides), **harmony** (harmonia)

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a lute to illustrate the delicate nature of political alliances. It explains that just as a single broken or out-of-tune string ruins the music, a single unfaithful ally destroys the peace and harmony of a treaty.</summary>
<keywords>treaties, diplomacy, music metaphor, lute, Italian princes, political harmony, faith, Alciato</keywords>

In this context, Faith refers to the trustworthiness of one who can keep a secret—a vital quality for any advisor or statesman mentioned in the previous sections.</meta>

## On Silence.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration shows a scholar in a study, wearing a cap and long robe. He is seated at a table covered with a cloth, appearing to be in a state of contemplation or writing. Behind him are shelves tightly packed with books. To the right, a large wooden chest sits on the floor, and an open doorway leads to another room where a mechanical bell-pull or clock weight system is visible on the wall.</image-desc>

> *When he holds his peace, a madman differs in no way from the wise,*
<note>original: "Cùm tacet, haud quicquam differt sapientibus amens"; the poet suggests that silence is a mask that can hide a lack of intelligence.</note>
> *For his own tongue and his voice are the indicators of his folly.*
<note>original: "Stultitiæ est index"; the author treats the tongue as a physical pointer or "index" that reveals one's internal character.</note>
> *Therefore, let him press his lips tight, and signal silence with a finger,*
> *And let him transform himself into the Egyptian Harpocrates.*
<note>original: "Pharium... Harpocratem"; Harpocrates was the Greek interpretation of the Egyptian god Horus the Child. Because he was often depicted in statues with a finger to his lips (a gesture representing childhood in Egypt), the Greeks and Romans mistakenly adopted him as the god of silence and secrecy.</note>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut ornament (tailpiece) featuring a grotesque ram's head (bucranium) with large curled horns and draped cloth hanging from the sides.</image-desc>

---

**Vocabulary:** silence, to the wise, madman, folly, Harpocrates (god of silence), Egyptian (literally "of Pharos"), Faith/Trust.

<summary>This page explores the virtue of silence as a component of "Faith" or trustworthiness, advising that the foolish can pass for wise only if they keep their mouths shut. It utilizes the classical figure of Harpocrates to symbolize the importance of discretion.</summary>
<keywords>silence, Harpocrates, wisdom vs. folly, discretion, Egyptian mythology, emblem, trustworthiness</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 14's discussion on personal silence and the figure of Harpocrates, this page applies the virtue of "Faith"—in the sense of keeping a trust or a secret—to the world of military and political leadership. While the previous page focused on the individual's wisdom, this page explores the necessity of strategic secrecy in statecraft.</meta>

<header>F A I T H .</header>
<page-num>15</page-num>

<image-desc>The page is encased in an elaborate woodcut border of Mannerist design. The border features a grotesque mask at the top center wearing a crown, flanked by scrolls and floral patterns. On the left and right sides are sculpted male figures (telamones) that serve as architectural supports. At the bottom, there are intricate scrollwork and foliage. The centaur is bearded and carries a large wooden club over its right shoulder. A banner (vexillum) crosses the image diagonally, inscribed with the letters "S P Q R" (Senatus Populusque Romanus).</image-desc>

## Secret plans must not be shared.
<note>original: "Non vulganda consilia"; "vulganda" implies making something common or public. The title emphasizes that a leader's "consilia" (plans or counsels) lose their effectiveness once they are no longer secret.</note>

->The monster that Daedalus shut away in Knossian shadows,<-
<note>original: "Gnosiacis... latebris"; Knossos was the capital of ancient Crete and the site of the Labyrinth. The "monster" is the Minotaur.</note>
->amidst blind light and dark gloom:<-
->the Roman Phalanx carries its image into battle,<-
<note>original: "Romana Phalanx"; although the term phalanx is Greek, the author refers to the Roman military and their tradition of using various animal symbols on their standards before the eagle was standardized.</note>
->and their proud standards shine with the half-man bull.<-
<note>original: "Semiuiróque... boue"; referring to the Minotaur (half-man, half-bull). While the illustration on this page depicts a centaur (half-man, half-horse), the poem explicitly identifies the creature as the Minotaur, using it as a symbol for the "labyrinthine" complexity of military strategy.</note>
->They warn us: the secret plans of leaders must remain hidden;<-
->a scheme once revealed harms its author.<-
<note>original: "autori cognita techna nocet"; the word "techna" suggests a clever trick or ruse. The poet warns that the "Faith" a general places in his advisors must be absolute, for if a stratagem is leaked, it will backfire on the one who devised it.</note>

<summary>This page uses the mythical Minotaur and the Roman military standards to argue that political and military success depends on absolute secrecy. It teaches that "Faith" in a professional context means the ability to guard a leader's hidden strategies as securely as a labyrinth.</summary>
<keywords>secrecy, military strategy, Minotaur, Daedalus, Roman standards, SPQR, discretion, leadership</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page's discussion on the necessity of military secrecy—symbolized by the Minotaur hidden in the Labyrinth—this page moves from the strategic "counsel" of leaders to the personal "faith" and endurance required to keep a secret under duress. It uses the historical/legendary figure of Leæna as the ultimate example of silence.</meta>

<header>F A I T H .</header>
<page-num>16</page-num>

<image-desc>An emblem illustration set within a highly ornate architectural woodcut border. The border features a crowned mask at the top center, flanked by floral scrollwork and four circular ornaments. The vertical sides are composed of herm-like figures: a bearded male on the left and a female on the right, both with garlands. At the bottom, a grotesque mask with horns and foliage is centered. The central woodcut shows a spotted lioness walking toward the right in a landscape containing a deciduous tree, stone ruins with an arch, and a castle atop a hill in the distance.</image-desc>

### One must not yield
### even to torture.
<note>original: "Nec quæstioni quidem cedendum." In a legal and historical context, "quæstio" refers to judicial examination, often involving "questioning by torture."</note>

*The Lioness you see fashioned in the Athenian <note>original: "Cecropia... arce"; Cecropia is a poetic name for Athens, named after its mythical founder-king, Cecrops.</note> citadel,*
*Was—did you not know, stranger?—the companion of Harmodius <note>Harmodius and Aristogeiton were the "Tyrannicides" who assassinated the Athenian tyrant Hipparchus in 514 BCE.</note>.*
*Thus it was decided to show the fierce spirit of that heroine <term>virago|A woman of masculine spirit or great courage; here referring to Leæna, whose name means "Lioness."</term>*
*In the manner of a wild beast: or perhaps because she bore such a name.*
*Because, though twisted by the torture-strings <note>original: "fidibus"; refers to the chords or strings of a rack or similar torture device used to extract information.</note>, she betrayed no one*
*By her testimony: the sculptor Iphicrates <note>While historical sources often credit the sculptor Amphicrates, the text here names Iphicrates. The statue of the lioness was famously cast without a tongue.</note> rendered her without a tongue.*

<note>According to legend, Leæna was a courtesan and friend of the Tyrannicides. After the assassination of Hipparchus, she was tortured by the tyrant Hippias to reveal the names of other conspirators. To ensure she could not speak even if her resolve broke, she supposedly bit off her own tongue. The Athenians later honored her with a bronze statue of a tongueless lioness on the Acropolis.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the virtue of "Fides" (Faith or Loyalty) through the story of Leæna, who endured torture without betraying her allies. The symbol of the tongueless lioness serves as a potent emblem for the absolute preservation of secrets.</summary>
<keywords>Faith, Loyalty, Silence, Leæna, Harmodius, Athens, Torture, Courage, Tyrannicides, Iphicrates</keywords>

### Through wisdom and valor, the Chimera is overcome;
### that is, those who are more powerful
### and deceptive.
<note>original: "Consilio, & virtute Chimæran superari, id est, fortiores & deceptores." The author provides a moral interpretation of the myth: the Chimera represents enemies who are both physically stronger and intellectually dishonest.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration set within an elaborate decorative frame. The central image depicts the hero Bellerophon riding the winged horse Pegasus. Bellerophon, clad in classical armor, brandishes a long spear directed at the Chimera below. The Chimera is rendered as a composite beast with a lion's head and forequarters, a goat's head emerging from its back, and a serpent for a tail. The scene is set against a landscape of hills and clouds. The ornate frame features winged figures at the top, statuesque male and female figures (atlantes and caryatids) along the vertical sides, and a scene at the bottom where two muscular men pull a wheeled platform bearing a large grotesque mask.</image-desc>

> Bellerophon (as a courageous rider) was able to overcome the Chimera,
> And strike down the monsters of the Lycian <note>original: "Licij... soli"; Lycia was an ancient region in Asia Minor, modern-day Turkey, where the Chimera was said to dwell.</note> soil.
> Thus you, carried on the wings of Pegasus <note>The immortal winged horse who assisted Bellerophon.</note>, seek the heavens,
> And by the wisdom of your mind, you tame proud monsters.
<note>original: "Consilióque animi"; this emphasizes that the hero's victory comes from his intellect and strategic planning ("counsel") rather than just brute force.</note>

<vocab>Prudence, Bellerophon, Pegasus, Chimera, Lycia, Counsel, Virtue</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the myth of Bellerophon and the Chimera to illustrate the virtue of Prudence, arguing that intellectual wisdom combined with courage allows one to defeat even the most formidable and deceptive opponents.</summary>

### Vigilance and Guardianship.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration enclosed in a decorative woodcut frame. The frame features ornamental swags and grotesque figures at the top, two terminal figures (human busts on pedestals) on the sides holding palm branches, and a bottom frieze depicting two men pulling a chariot with a central grotesque mask. ->*Because the rooster, singing, gives the signs of the approaching dawn,*<- <note>original: "èòi"; referring to the Greek *Eos*, the personification of the dawn.</note>
->*And recalls the servants' hands to their new tasks,*<-
->*He is fashioned upon sacred towers; just as the bronze bell*<- <note>original: "ærea... peluis"; literally a "bronze basin," but here referring to a resonant vessel or bell that calls the faithful to prayer.</note>
->*Recalls the watchful mind to the heavens above.*<-
->*The lion is there too: but because as a guardian he sleeps with eyes open,*<- <note>A common belief in historical bestiaries was that lions never closed their eyes when sleeping, making them the ultimate symbol of a "watchful" guardian.</note>
->*He is therefore placed before the doors of temples.*<-

<header>P R U D E N C E</header>
<page-num>19</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>

->*Be sober and remember to be skeptical; these are the joints of the mind.*<- <note>original Greek: "Νᾶφε καὶ μέμνασ’ ἀπιστεῖν. ἄρθρα ταῦτα τῶν φρενῶν." This is a famous maxim by the ancient Greek poet-philosopher Epicharmus.</note>
->Live soberly, and do not believe rashly.<- <note>original Latin: "Sobriè viuendum : & non temerè credendum." This is a Latin translation of the Greek quote above.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration contained within an ornate rectangular border featuring classical scrollwork and grotesque masks. The image depicts a large hand reaching out from the clouds over a landscape. In the center of the palm is an open eye (an "oculata manus" or eye-bearing hand). The background consists of rolling hills, a small stone arch bridge over a stream, and several leafy plants in the foreground, specifically identified in the text as pennyroyal (pulegium).</image-desc>

->“Do not be quick to believe,” says Epicharmus, “and do not be a drunkard;”<-
->These shall be the very sinews and limbs of the human mind.<-
->Behold the eye-bearing hand, which believes only what it sees. Behold<- <note>The "eye-bearing hand" (Latin: *oculata manus*) is a visual metaphor for practical wisdom: a prudent person trusts their own observations and evidence rather than relying on hearsay or blind faith.</note>
->The Pennyroyal, the herb of ancient sobriety,<- <note>original: "Pulegium." This aromatic plant was historically associated with clear-headedness and was used in simple, frugal drinks.</note>
->With which Heraclitus, by showing it, calmed the crowd,<- <note>Heraclitus was a Greek philosopher. This refers to an anecdote where he ended a political riot not with a speech, but by publicly drinking a simple mixture of water and herbs to show that peace and satisfaction come from modest needs.</note>
->And soothed a people heavy with the weight of a swelling rebellion.<-

<sig>B 2</sig>

<summary>This page illustrates the intellectual side of Prudence, emphasizing skepticism and sobriety through the symbols of the 'eye-bearing hand' and the pennyroyal plant. It teaches that a wise person should be slow to believe rumors and should maintain a clear, temperate mind to ensure social and personal stability.</summary>

<header>P R U D E N C E</header>
<page-num>20</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>

<image-desc>The text and central illustration are framed by a large, ornate Renaissance-style woodcut border. The border features heavy scrollwork (cartouches), bunches of fruit (grapes and pomegranates) at the top, and various decorative motifs including masks and stylized foliage. Within this large border is a rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a bearded man in a tunic and cloak standing in a rocky, mountainous landscape. He is gesturing with open hands toward a line of cranes flying in formation across the sky. In the far distance, a small building sits atop a cliff.</image-desc>

->Where did I transgress? What have I done? What did I leave undone that was required?<- <note>original Greek: "Πῇ παρέβην; τί δ᾽ ἔρεξα; Τί μοι δέον, οὐκ ἐτελέσθη?" This is a famous passage from the "Golden Verses of Pythagoras," a collection of moral exhortations. It encourages a nightly review of one's daily actions.</note>
->Where did I overstep? What have I committed? What have I omitted?<- <note>original Latin: "Quid excessi? quid admisi? quid omisi?" This is the Latin translation of the Greek verses above.</note>

->*The most famous author of the Italian sect, the Samian,*<- <note>Pythagoras of Samos (the "Samian") founded a philosophical school and community in Crotone, Italy, which the author refers to here as the "Italian sect."</note>
->*Himself enclosed his own teaching in a short poem.*<-
->*Where have you overstepped? What are you doing? What duty do you neglect?*<-
->*Urging each person to render this account to themselves.*<-
->*Which he is said to have learned from the flock of flying cranes,*<- <note><term>Cranes</term> (Latin: *grues*) were a common symbol in Renaissance emblems for vigilance and order. Here, they represent the steadying influence of self-reflection.</note>
->*Which carry a stone seized in their feet,*<- <note>Natural historians like Pliny the Elder claimed that migrating cranes carried stones as ballast to stay steady in high winds or to keep themselves awake while standing guard.</note>
->*Lest they falter, or lest evil winds snatch them off course.*<-
->*By this same principle must the life of men be ruled.*<-

<vocab>Prudence, The Samian (Pythagoras), Italian Sect (Pythagoreanism), Cranes, Golden Verses</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the Pythagorean practice of daily self-examination as a fundamental aspect of Prudence. It uses the metaphor of cranes carrying stones as ballast to illustrate how reflecting on one's actions keeps a person steady against the "winds" of vice and distraction.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, self-reflection, Prudence, cranes, Golden Verses, moral discipline, ballast, Italian sect</keywords>

### The Prudent. A Problem.

<image-desc>A woodcut emblem page within an ornate decorative border consisting of architectural scrolls, fruit, and a grotesque mask at the bottom center. The central rectangular image depicts a two-faced head (Janus) floating in the center of a landscape. The head looks left and right simultaneously. The landscape features rolling hills, rocks, small plants, and a sky with clouds and a distant flock of birds.</image-desc>

> ->Two-faced Janus, you who know well both things past and those to come,<- <note>original Latin: *Iane bifrons, qui iam transacta, futuraq; calles*. Janus was the Roman god of beginnings, gates, and transitions, traditionally depicted with two faces to look at the year ending and the year beginning.</note>
> ->And who sees behind with your eyes (just as you do in front),<- <note>original Latin: *Quiq; retro sannis (sicut & ante) uides*. The poet suggests that a wise person possesses a "backward" gaze of experience and a "forward" gaze of foresight.</note>
> ->Why do they fashion you with so many eyes, so many faces? Is it because<-
> ->this very form teaches that a man should be circumspect?<- <note>original Latin: *Circunspectum hominem forma fuisse docet*. The term "circumspect" literally means "looking around." In the Renaissance, the prudent man was defined by his ability to weigh past lessons against future possibilities.</note>

<sig>B 3</sig>

<vocab>Janus, Prudence, emblem, Latin poetry, two-faced, Alciati</vocab>

<summary>This page presents an emblem of the Roman god Janus to symbolize the virtue of Prudence, emphasizing that wisdom requires a "circumspect" view of both past experiences and future consequences. It uses the physical form of the two-faced god as a visual metaphor for the intellectual breadth required of a wise person.</summary>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of Prudence. ### Wise, rather than wordy.

->**Prudens, magis quàm loquax.**<-
<note>original Latin: *Prudens, magis quàm loquax.* This motto suggests that a prudent person values silence and observation over excessive talking.</note>

<image-desc>An emblem contained within an elaborate woodcut border. The central image depicts an owl perched inside a scrolled, shield-like cartouche. In the background is a seascape featuring two sailing vessels and a sun rising or setting with prominent rays. The surrounding architectural border is decorated with scrolled motifs, and at the bottom center, a grotesque mask or head is integrated into the design.</image-desc>

> The owl provides the emblem for Cecropian Athens, <note>original: *Noctua Cecropijs insignia præstat Athenis*. Athens is frequently called "Cecropian" in classical poetry, named after Cecrops, the legendary first king of the city who was said to be half-man and half-serpent.</note>
> Among the birds, the Owl is the one of sound counsel.
> Rightly is she consecrated to the service of armor-bearing Minerva, <note>Minerva (the Greek Athena) was the goddess of both wisdom and strategic warfare. The owl, capable of seeing in the dark, became her primary symbol to represent the "light" of wisdom piercing the "darkness" of ignorance.</note>
> In the place where the talkative crow had previously given way. <note>original: *Garrula quo cornix cesserat ante loco.* This refers to a myth where the crow was Athena's original companion but was banished for its inability to keep a secret, replaced by the silent and observant owl.</note>

<vocab>Prudence, Owl, Athens, Minerva, Crow, emblem, discretion</vocab>

<summary>This page illustrates the virtue of Prudence through the symbol of the Owl, the sacred bird of Minerva. It emphasizes that true wisdom involves being "sound of counsel" and maintaining silence, contrasting the owl's discretion with the crow's foolish talkativeness.</summary>

### Make Haste Deliberately.
<note>original Latin: *Maturandum*. This title implies that actions should be "ripened" or brought to maturity, neither rushed nor delayed. It is a variation of the classical adage *Festina Lente* (Make haste slowly).</note>

<image-desc>A central woodcut emblem depicts a vertical arrow (telum) with a remora fish (echeneis) coiled tightly around its shaft. The scene is set in a landscape featuring ruins of a stone building with an arch on the right, a distant town nestled at the foot of mountains on the left, and several birds flying in the sky.</image-desc>

->All bid us to hasten quickly, and yet to delay,<-
->Lest we be too headlong, or our delay too long.<-
->Let the arrow bound with a <term>remora</term> reveal this to you;<-
<note>The remora (also called an *echeneis*) is a small fish which, according to ancient natural history, was capable of stopping a great ship in its tracks by clinging to its hull. It represents restraint and delay.</note>
->The fish is slow, while the darts thrown by the hand fly swift.<-
<note>original Latin: *Hæc tarda est, uolitant spicula missa manu.* The "dart" or "arrow" represents speed and the impulse to act, while the fish represents the prudence that holds one back until the moment is right.</note>

<image-desc>A horizontal landscape woodcut at the base of the page shows a path or river winding through a valley with trees and a small house with a peaked roof.</image-desc>

<sig>B 4</sig>

<summary>This page illustrates the virtue of Prudence through the emblem of an arrow entwined with a remora fish, symbolizing the classical concept of "making haste slowly." It warns the reader to find a balance between impulsive rashness and excessive procrastination.</summary>

<keywords>Prudence, deliberation, remora, arrow, Festina Lente, balance, timing, emblem</keywords>

### On the Capture.
<note>original Latin: *In depræhensum*. This title refers to the act of catching someone or something that has previously been elusive or deceptive.</note>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicts a man in 16th-century clothing kneeling beside a winding stream or pool. He is firmly grasping a large, writhing eel, using a leafy branch or fig leaf to maintain his grip. To the left is a stone building with an arched doorway and barred windows. In the background, there is a rural landscape with trees, additional buildings, and birds flying in the sky.</image-desc>

->For a long time I have followed wherever you flee, but now<-
<note>original Latin: *Iamdudum quacunque fugis te persequor...*</note>
->At last, you are held fast in my nets.<-
->No longer will you be able to escape our grasp,<-
->Now that we have gripped the eel with a fig leaf.<-
<note>original Latin: *Ficulno anguillam strinximus in folio*. This refers to an ancient proverb. Because an eel is incredibly slippery, it was said that one could only hold onto it by using the rough surface of a <term>fig leaf</term> to provide friction. In an allegorical sense, this means that a clever or "slippery" person can only be caught by someone using the right equipment or a clever strategy—a hallmark of Prudence.</note>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of catching a slippery eel with a rough fig leaf to illustrate that Prudence involves using the correct methods and tools to secure difficult goals or people. It emphasizes that persistence combined with the right strategy eventually overcomes even the most elusive challenges.</summary>

<keywords>Prudence, strategy, eel, fig leaf, capture, cleverness, persistence, emblem</keywords>

## That virgins must be guarded.
<note>original Latin: *Custodiendas virgines*. The use of the gerundive implies a moral necessity or duty to protect young women.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration enclosed in a large, ornate rectangular border of interlaced strapwork and foliage. The border features two central grotesque masks: a leonine face at the top and a human-like face at the bottom, the latter flanked by scrolling ribbon-like banners. The central woodcut depicts the goddess Pallas Athena (Minerva) standing in a landscape. She is dressed in classical armor, including a crested helmet and a breastplate featuring the aegis with the face of Medusa. She holds a long spear or staff in her left hand and holds a fold of her billowing cloak in her right. At her feet, to the left, lies a dragon (draco) with its mouth open. In the background to the right stands a circular classical temple with a dome and a colonnade. The landscape includes hills, a distant building, and four birds flying in the sky.</image-desc>

This is the true likeness of the unwed **Pallas**:
<note>Pallas is another name for Athena (the Roman Minerva), the goddess of wisdom, weaving, and strategic warfare. She was famously a "parthenos" or virgin goddess.</note>
Here is her **Dragon**, which stands before the feet of its mistress.
<note>original Latin: *Draco*. In emblem literature, the dragon or serpent is a symbol of <term>Vigilantia</term> (Vigilance) because it was believed that snakes never closed their eyes, making them perfect guardians.</note>
Why is this animal a companion to the goddess? The guarding of things
Is entrusted to it; thus it haunts the groves and sacred temples.
It is necessary to preserve unwed girls with **watchful care**:
<note>original Latin: *Peruigili... cura*. The adjective "ever-watchful" emphasizes that Prudence cannot sleep when it comes to protection.</note>
**Love** stretches his snares on every side.
<note>original Latin: *laqueos undiq; tendit amor*. "Love" here refers to Cupid or erotic desire, depicted as a hunter laying traps for the unwary.</note>

<vocab>Prudence, Pallas, Dragon, Minerva, Virgins, Love, Guarding</vocab>

<summary>This emblem uses Pallas Athena and her ever-watchful dragon to symbolize the necessity of constant vigilance in protecting maidenly virtue. It argues that because desire (Love) is a hunter that sets traps everywhere, the prudent mind must remain as alert as a temple guardian.</summary>

<keywords>Pallas Athena, Minerva, vigilance, dragon, virtue, protection, snares of love, Prudence, maidenhood</keywords>

## That Prudence is increased by Wine.
<note>original Latin: *Vino Prudentiam augeri*. This emblem suggests that a complete and wise life requires both intellectual rigor and the social, spirited influence of wine, provided it is taken in moderation.</note>

<image-desc>The entire page content is enclosed within a broad, decorative woodcut border featuring complex interlocking strapwork patterns. Grotesque masks are integrated into the border at the top and bottom centers. In the middle of the page is a rectangular woodcut illustration showing a vaulted interior with classical columns. On a central stone pedestal stand two figures: Pallas Athena on the left, wearing a helmet and holding a spear and shield, and a youthful, nude Bacchus on the right with one arm raised. A hanging lamp or censer is suspended between them from the ceiling.</image-desc>

->Father **Bacchus** and **Pallas** both share these temples,<-
<note>Pallas (Athena) represents wisdom and war; Bacchus (Dionysus) represents wine and ecstasy.</note>
->Each is the true offspring of **Jove**:<-
<note>original Latin: *Iouis* (Jupiter). In mythology, both were children of Jupiter born in unusual ways, marking them as divine extensions of his own power.</note>
->She was released from his head, and he from his thigh:<-
<note>Pallas Athena famously sprang fully formed from the forehead of Zeus (Jupiter). Bacchus was rescued from the womb of his dying mother, Semele, and sewn into Jupiter’s thigh until he was ready to be born.</note>
->To her, the use of the **olive** is owed, but he first discovered **pure wine**.<-
<note>original Latin: *merum*. The olive is a symbol of peace and civilization, while wine represents the joy and vital spirit of life.</note>
->They are joined for good reason; for if anyone, being abstemious, hates<-
<term>Abstemious</term> (original: *abstemius*): One who refrains from drinking wine.
->Wine, he shall feel no aid from the Goddess.<-
<note>The "Goddess" here is Pallas (Wisdom). The poem suggests that a harsh, overly sober person who entirely rejects the gifts of Bacchus lacks the "spirit" or "warmth" necessary for true, practical wisdom.</note>

<vocab>Prudence, Bacchus, Pallas (Athena), Jove (Jupiter), pure wine, abstinent, wine, of the olive</vocab>

<summary>This emblem depicts the gods Pallas Athena and Bacchus sharing a temple, symbolizing the union of intellect and spirit. It argues that true wisdom requires the refinement and "sharpening" that comes from the moderate enjoyment of wine.</summary>
<keywords>Pallas Athena, Bacchus, Jupiter, Prudence, Wine, Balance, Mythology, Moderation</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page presents a counter-argument to the previous page (page 26). While the former suggested that wine can sharpen the mind, this emblem warns that true wisdom (represented by the olive tree of Pallas) is choked or corrupted by the influence of wine (represented by the grapevine of Bacchus). This "pro and contra" style was common in Renaissance emblem books to show the complexity of moral virtues.</meta>

## The Wise Abstain from Wine.
<note>original Latin: *Prudentes vino abstinent*. This title sets the theme of temperance and the preservation of clear judgment through sobriety.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration set within an elaborate, ornate frame. The central scene shows a sturdy olive tree being entwined by grapevines with hanging clusters of grapes. The background features a landscape with hills and architectural ruins. The surrounding border is rich with Renaissance-style ornamentation, including a blank heraldic shield at the top, grotesque masks, and caryatid-like female figures on the sides. At the bottom of the frame is a ram's head mask with flowing drapery, and below it, a face crowned with grape leaves and fruit, personifying Bacchus (Bromius).</image-desc>

->Why do you trouble me, O branches? I am the tree of **Pallas**,<-
<note>original: *Quid me uexatis rami? sum Palladis arbor*. The olive tree was the sacred gift of Pallas Athena to Athens; it represents peace, utility, and sober wisdom.</note>
->Take away the grape clusters from here; the Virgin flees **Bromius**.<-
<note>original: *Auferte hinc botros, uirgo fugit Bromium*. "The Virgin" refers to Pallas Athena, who remained chaste and intellectual. Bromius is a common epithet for Bacchus, the god of wine. The poem suggests that the "intoxication" of the vine is an unwanted intrusion upon the "sobriety" of the olive.</note>

<term>Prudentia</term> (Prudence or Practical Wisdom); <term>Pallas</term> (The Greek goddess Athena, associated with wisdom and crafts); <term>Bromius</term> (A name for Bacchus/Dionysus, meaning "the noisy one," referring to the rowdiness of wine); <term>Olive tree</term> (Symbol of Pallas and peace); <term>Grapevine</term> (Symbol of Bacchus and wine); <term>Temperance</term> (The virtue of moderation); <term>Emblem</term> (A combination of an image and text used to convey a moral lesson).

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a grapevine entwining an olive tree to argue that wine and wisdom are incompatible. It portrays Pallas (Wisdom) as a virgin goddess who must flee from the intoxicating influence of Bacchus (Wine) to maintain her clarity.</summary>
<keywords>Prudence, Pallas Athena, Bacchus, Temperance, Sobriety, Olive Tree, Grapevine, Classical Mythology</keywords>

<header>P R U D E N C E.</header>

### On the Statue of Bacchus.

#### A DIALOGUE.
<note>original: *Dialogismus*. This is a rhetorical term for a poem written as a conversation, often between a traveler and a statue (a common trope in Greek and Latin epigrams).</note>

<image-desc>A detailed woodcut illustration framed by an ornate architectural border in the Renaissance "grotesque" style. Within the central frame, the god Bacchus is depicted as a youthful, nude male reclining on a grassy bank beneath a grapevine laden with large clusters of grapes. He is shown playing a long, slender pipe with his right hand and holding a circular percussion instrument, likely a tympanum, in his left. A shallow bowl or patera sits on the ground in the foreground. The surrounding border is decorated with intricate strapwork, scrolled foliage, two female herms or caryatids flanking the scene, and several expressive masks, including a prominent central mask at the bottom entwined with leaves.</image-desc>

> Father Bacchus, who among mortals has seen you with their own eyes?
> <note>original: *Bacche pater quis te mortali lumine nouit?*</note>
> And who fashioned your limbs with such a skilled hand?
> <note>original: *Et docta effinxit quis tua membra manu?*</note>
> It was **Praxiteles**, who saw me as I was carrying off the **Gnosian** maiden,
> <note>original: *Praxiteles, qui me rapientem Gnoſida uidit*. Praxiteles was one of the most famous sculptors of ancient Greece. The "Gnosian" refers to Ariadne, the daughter of King Minos of Knossos (Gnosos) on Crete, whom Bacchus famously rescued and married.</note>
> And he depicted me exactly as I appeared at that time.
> <note>original: *Atque illo pinxit tempore qualis eram*.</note>
> Why do you bloom as a youth, with a beard of soft down,
> <note>original: *Cur iuuenis, teneráque etiam lanugine uernat / Barba*. This question highlights the paradox of Bacchus: wine is ancient, yet those who drink it often act with the impulsiveness or vigor of youth.</note>
> When you could surpass even the **Pylian** old man in age?
> <note>original: *queas Pylium cum ſuperare ſenem?* The "Pylian old man" is Nestor, the King of Pylos from Homer's *Iliad*, renowned for living through three generations of men and being the personification of ancient wisdom.</note>

<summary>This page presents a poetic dialogue (ekphrasis) with a statue of Bacchus, explaining his youthful appearance despite his ancient status as a god. It references the famous sculptor Praxiteles and the myth of Ariadne to explore the nature of the god of wine.</summary>

<keywords>Bacchus, Praxiteles, Ariadne, Nestor, youth and age, Greek mythology, Dialogismus, Prudence</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue with Bacchus from page 28. Having established Bacchus's identity and his depiction by the sculptor Praxiteles, the poem now transitions into a moral lesson on temperance. The speaker asks Bacchus about his appearance and the proper way to consume wine, using the god’s mythological origins—born of fire and cooled by water—as a metaphor for the necessary dilution of wine.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>

<header>P R U D E N C E.</header>

<image-desc>The text of the page is enclosed within a highly ornate woodcut border. The border features architectural strapwork and scrollwork. At the top center is a grotesque mask with leafy hair and horns. The sides of the border contain repeating oval patterns that resemble links in a chain. At the bottom center of the border, there is a small, bared-teeth grotesque mask in a cartouche. ->*If you learn to be sparing with my gifts:*<-
->*You will stay younger, and your heart will always be strong.*<-
->*Drums are never far from my hands, nor horns from my head,*<-
->*For whom—unless they are mad—do such signs seem fitting?*<-
->*I teach this: that he who abuses our gift takes on*<-
->*The horns; and like a madman, he shakes the soft rattles.*<-
<note>The "horns" and "rattles" (sistra) are traditional attributes of Bacchus's followers, the Maenads and Satyrs, often symbolizing the loss of human reason and the descent into a wild, animalistic state when drunk.</note>
->*What is the meaning of that almost fiery color in your limbs? (May the omen*<-
->*Be far!) Are you being burned by human fires?*<-
->*When my father pulled me from **Semele's** womb with fire-breathing*<-
<note>original: *Semeles*. In mythology, Bacchus's mother Semele died when she beheld Zeus in his full glory; Zeus rescued the unborn Bacchus from her ashes.</note>
->*Lightning: he plunged me, stained with ash, into the water.*<-
->*Hence, he is wise who dilutes me well with flowing waves:*<-
->*Who does not roast his liver with burning fire.*<-
<term>Iecur: The liver. In ancient physiology, the liver was considered the seat of the passions and the organ most affected by the "heat" of undiluted wine.</term>
->*But now, tell me: how do you wish to be mixed? And by what*<-
->*Rule can a sane man enjoy you safely?*<-
->*Let him add a measure of water, he who desires to drink a cup*<-
->*Of **Falernian**: it is best to take your cups in this manner.*<-
<note>original: *Falerni*. Falernian was a famous, high-alcohol wine from Campania, highly prized by the ancient Romans but known for its potency.</note>
->*Stay within the limit of two **heminae**: for he who dares to go*<-
->*Further: is first cheerful, but soon drunk, and finally driven to rage:*<-
<term>Hemina: A Roman unit of liquid measure, roughly equal to half a pint (approx. 270ml). Two heminae was considered a moderate daily allowance.</term>
->*This is a very hard lesson. Our throats are thirsty, and you*<-
->*Flow so sweetly. Alas, no benefits are ever easily won.*<-

<meta>Artist's mark 'P' visible in the lower-left corner of the bottom illustration.</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the dialogue with Bacchus, framing the god as a teacher of moderation. He explains that wine must be tempered with water to avoid madness and physical harm, referencing his own birth from fire and water.</summary>

<header>J U S T I C E .</header>

## J U S T I C E .

->One should harm no one, whether by word or by deed.<-
<note>original: "Nec verbo, nec facto quenquam lædendum." This moral maxim reflects a fundamental principle of Roman law and ethics: the obligation to avoid causing injury to others.</note>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration is enclosed within an elaborate ornamental border. The border features architectural elements, scrollwork, masks, and a central bearded grotesque head at the top. At the bottom of the frame, a winged putto or child is depicted amidst various objects, possibly tools or instruments. The central illustration shows a female figure representing Justice or Nemesis. She is dressed in flowing robes and stands in a landscape by the sea. She holds a large sword or staff with its point resting on the ground. In the background, there is a body of water with a small vessel, and a rugged coastline with buildings and hills under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

> She catches up, and **Nemesis** tracks the footsteps of men,
> And she restrains the elbow, and holds the hard reins in her hand.
<note>original: "cubitum... frena." The "elbow" and "reins" are symbols of restraint. Nemesis is depicted holding these to show how she physically and morally pulls back a person from committing a rash or harmful act.</note>
> Lest you do any evil; or speak wicked words:
> And she commands that moderation be present in all things.
<note>The concept of "measure" or "moderation" (original: *modum*) was the central virtue of Greek and Roman ethics, often expressed by the phrase "nothing in excess."</note>

<term>Justice</term> (Latin: *Iustitia*): The moral virtue consisting in the constant and resolute will to give to each person their due.
<term>Nemesis</term>: In Greek mythology, the goddess of divine retribution and vengeance, particularly against those who succumb to *hubris* (arrogant pride).
<term>Moderation</term> (Latin: *moderatio*): The avoidance of extremes; restraint.
<term>Reins</term> (Latin: *frena*): A common metaphor for the laws or the conscience that guide and control human impulses.
<term>Footsteps</term> (Latin: *uestigia*): Symbolizes the life path or previous actions of an individual which Justice inevitably follows.
<term>Emblem</term>: A symbolic picture accompanied by a motto and a poem, used to teach a moral lesson.
<term>Ethics</term>: The branch of knowledge that deals with moral principles.

<summary>This page illustrates the figure of Nemesis as a guardian of Justice, emphasizing that divine retribution follows every human action. It teaches that true justice requires restraint in both speech and deed, advocating for moderation in all aspects of life.</summary>
<keywords>Justice, Nemesis, morality, restraint, moderation, ethics, retribution, law</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page follows the discussion of Justice as internal restraint (personified by Nemesis) on page 30. While the previous page focused on preventing harm through self-control, this page illustrates how Justice acts as an external force of nature or divine will to correct human errors, specifically focusing on the famous mythological dispute over the armor of Achilles.</meta>

<header>J U S T I C E .</header>

## At last, at last Justice
## prevails.

<image-desc>A detailed woodcut illustration set within a decorative border featuring masks, garlands, and classical figures. The central scene depicts a rough sea washing a circular shield (the arms of Achilles) toward a large, stepped stone monument or tomb on a rocky shore. In the background, a ruined castle stands on a cliff under a cloudy sky with birds. At the bottom of the border, two reclining male figures flank an empty cartouche.</image-desc>

*The shield of <term>Achilles</term><note>original: "Aeacidæ." Achilles was the grandson of Aeacus, hence the patronymic name.</note>, soaked in the blood of <term>Hector</term>,*
*Which an unjust assembly of Greeks gave to the <term>man from Ithaca</term><note>original: "Ithaco." This refers to Odysseus (Ulysses), the king of Ithaca, who won the armor of Achilles through his persuasive oratory rather than physical prowess.</note>:*
*A more just Neptune snatched it up when it was cast into the sea*
*By shipwreck, so that it might return to its rightful master.*
*For the wave bore it to the tomb of <term>Ajax</term> on the shore:*
*The wave bellows, and strikes the grave with such a voice:*
*“You have conquered, <term>son of Telamon</term><note>original: "Telamoniade." This refers to Ajax the Great, who was considered the most deserving warrior to inherit Achilles' armor after Achilles was killed at Troy.</note>; you are the more worthy of these arms.”*
*It is right that personal favors yield to justice.*
<note>This poem references the "Judgment of the Arms." After Achilles died, Ajax and Odysseus both claimed his divinely-forged armor. When the Greek leaders awarded it to Odysseus, Ajax was so overcome by the injustice that he took his own life. The emblem suggests that nature (represented by Neptune, god of the sea) corrected the biased human judgment by washing the shield to Ajax's tomb.</note>

<vocab>Justice, Achilles, Hector, Ajax, Neptune, Son of Telamon, Ithaca, shield</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the mythological story of the contest for Achilles' armor to illustrate that while human judgments can be biased or "iniquitous," true justice eventually prevails through divine or natural intervention. The sea god Neptune restores the shield to the tomb of the rightful warrior, Ajax, signifying that truth will outlast human deception.</summary>

<keywords>Justice, Ajax, Odysseus, Achilles, Neptune, mythology, divine retribution, fairness, Trojan War</keywords>

While page 31 focused on righting a wrong through nature, page 32 examines how a ruler demonstrates authority by taming the wild, using the historical figure of Mark Antony as a central example.</meta>

<header>J U S T I C E .</header>

# Even the most ferocious are tamed.

<image-desc>An emblem illustration set within an ornate decorative border. The central woodcut depicts a Roman figure, identified in the text as Antony (Mark Antony), sitting in a chariot drawn by two lions. He holds a long staff or sceptre. They are traveling through a landscape of hills; in the distance, a small castle or fort sits on a peak, and two small figures walk on a path. The outer frame is architectural and figurative, containing a grotesque mask at the top, female herms on the sides, and two reclining male figures at the base amidst scrollwork.</image-desc>

->*After Roman eloquence was lost, with <term>Cicero</term><note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), Rome's greatest orator, whose death marked the end of the Roman Republic's traditional liberties.</note> destroyed,*<-
->*By that bitter plague<note>original: "pestis acerba." This refers to Mark Antony himself, who was often characterized by his enemies as a "plague" or "pestilence" to the Roman state.</note> upon his own fatherland;*<-
->*The victor mounted his chariot and yoked the lions,*<-
->*Compelling their necks to submit to the heavy yoke;*<-
->*<term>Antony</term><note>Mark Antony (83–30 BCE), the Roman general and member of the Second Triumvirate.</note> desired to signify through this allegory*<-
->*That even the most high-spirited leaders had yielded to his arms.*<-

<note>This emblem refers to a historical anecdote recorded by Pliny the Elder and Plutarch. Mark Antony reportedly drove a chariot pulled by lions through the streets of Rome to demonstrate that even the most "ferocious" or noble-hearted opponents could be tamed and brought under his control. In the context of a book on "Justice," this serves as a stern reminder of the power of the law or the ruler to enforce order upon the unruly.</note>

<vocab>Justice, Cicero, Antony, lions, emblem, Mark Antony, chariot</vocab>

<summary>This emblem uses the historical image of Mark Antony driving a chariot pulled by lions to represent the subduing of fierce or noble rivals. It portrays political authority as a force capable of taming even the most "ferocious" elements of society after the fall of the Roman Republic.</summary>

<keywords>Mark Antony, Cicero, lions, Roman history, political power, subdual, allegory, chariot, tyranny vs justice</keywords>

<header>J U S T I C E .</header>

### Returning Gratitude.

<image-desc>A woodcut emblem surrounded by a highly decorative border of Renaissance-style scrollwork, floral motifs, and a central grotesque mask at the bottom. The inner scene depicts storks nesting atop a high, ruined-looking column or tower. In the background, a bridge with three visible arches crosses a river toward a cityscape with a large building. The storks are an emblem of "pietas" (filial piety), specifically the legend that storks care for their elderly parents in return for the care they received as chicks.</image-desc>

*The Stork, renowned for her <term>piety</term><note>original: "pietate." In the classical world, 'pietas' was not just religious devotion, but the social obligation and natural love one owed to gods, country, and especially parents.</note> in her high-soaring nest,*
*Cherishes her featherless chicks, her dear pledges.*
*And she expects such mutual rewards to be returned to her,*
*Whenever she, now an aged mother, shall need this help.*
*The dutiful offspring does not fail this hope, but the weary*
*Bodies of the parents it carries on its shoulders, and provides food from its own beak.*

<note>This emblem illustrates the ancient concept of "Antipelargosis" (from the Greek 'pelargos' meaning stork), a law of reciprocity. It was a widely held belief in antiquity—cited by authors like Aristotle and Pliny—that young storks would not only feed their aging parents but also carry them on their wings when they became too weak to fly. This made the stork the primary symbol of filial gratitude and the "justice" of the younger generation caring for the older.</note>

<sig>C</sig>

<summary>This page uses the natural history of the stork to illustrate filial piety, presenting the care of elderly parents as a fundamental form of social justice and reciprocal duty.</summary>
<keywords>Justice, Filial Piety, Storks, Gratitude, Antipelargosis, Family Duty, Reciprocity</keywords>

<header>J U S T I C E .</header>

## Integrity.
<note>original: "Abstinentia." In this context, the term refers specifically to "abstinence" from greed, bribery, and the "sordid" lure of illicit gain.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration is framed by an elaborate decorative border featuring renaissance-style scrollwork, floral motifs, and a central grotesque mask with an open mouth at the bottom. In the bottom-left corner of the border's scrollwork, the letter "P" is visible. The central image depicts three men in a stone-paved interior. On the left, a man sits on a raised stone bench. Before him, a second man kneels, holding out a wide, shallow basin. A third, bearded man stands in the background. In the upper right background, a pitcher (urceus) sits atop a stone architectural structure.</image-desc>

->On one side of the marble column upon the tomb stands<-
->A <term>pitcher</term><note>original: "Urceus," a tall, one-handled vessel for pouring water.</note>; on the other, a <term>wash-basin</term><note>original: "malluuium," a basin specifically for washing the hands.</note> is to be seen.<-
->This imagery reminds us that the law was delivered without <term>corruption</term><note>original: "sordibus," literally meaning "filth" or "dirt." In a legal sense, it refers to "sordid" bribes that "soil" the reputation of a judge.</note>,<-
->And that the deceased possessed clean hands.<-

<note>This emblem uses the symbols of a funerary monument to celebrate the character of a deceased judge or magistrate. The pitcher and basin—tools for ritual cleansing—serve as a visual metaphor for the "clean hands" required in a court of law. To have "clean hands" meant that the official never accepted a bribe or allowed his judgment to be stained by personal profit. The placement of these items on a tomb suggests that a person's reputation for honesty is their most lasting monument.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the virtue of Integrity (Abstinentia) through the symbols of a pitcher and wash-basin on a judge's tomb, representing a life free from bribery and "sordid" corruption.</summary>
<keywords>Integrity, Justice, corruption, clean hands, funerary monument, symbolism, legal ethics</keywords>

Marius is joined to me by a shared wall, and so too is
Subbardus—both well-known names in our <term>forum</term> <note>original: *fori*. Here referring to the legal community or the law courts where these men are prominent figures.</note>.
Being well-funded, they are building, and they even strive 
to block the <term>light</term> <note>original: *luminibus*. This refers to a "servitude of light," a common Roman legal dispute where a neighbor builds a structure that obstructs the sunlight or view of an existing house.</note> from my windows on every side.
Wretched me! Two **Harpies** snatch at me just as they did to **Phineus**,
seeking to cast me out from my own home.

shall be my **Zetes** and my **Calais** against them. <note>In Greek mythology, the blind King Phineus was tormented by Harpies who stole his food. He was eventually rescued by the winged brothers Zetes and Calais. The author is using this myth as an allegory: the greedy neighbors are the Harpies, and his own moral integrity acts as the heroes who will defend his rights.</note>

<summary>This page uses the mythological story of Phineus and the Harpies to illustrate a legal struggle between a common citizen and his wealthy neighbors, asserting that moral integrity is the best defense against corruption.</summary>

# F O R T I T U D E.
## Signs of the Brave,
### A DIALOGUE. <note>original: *DIALOGISMVS*. A rhetorical device where the author engages in a conversation with an object or a historical figure.</note>

<image-desc>A detailed woodcut illustration framed by an ornate decorative border. The central image shows an eagle with outspread wings perched on top of a stone tomb or sarcophagus. ->To the Spirits of the Dead<- <note>original: *D - M*, an abbreviation for *Diis Manibus*, a common Roman funerary dedication to the spirits of the departed.</note>
->**OF ARISTOMENES**<- <note>Aristomenes was a legendary Greek hero from the 7th century BCE who led the Messenians in their struggle for independence against Sparta. He was famed for his near-superhuman escapes and bravery.</note>

> What cause moves you, bird of Jove <note>original: *uolucris Saturnia*. Literally "Saturnian bird." The eagle is the bird of Jupiter, who was the son of Saturn.</note>,
> That you sit high upon the tomb of the great Aristomenes?
> I give this reminder: As much as I excel in strength among the birds,
> So much does Aristomenes excel among the demigods.
> Let fearful doves perch upon the funeral pyres of the fearful;
> We eagles grant favorable signs to those who are intrepid.
