### On one who knows not how to flatter.

<image-desc>
A woodcut illustration set within an elaborate decorative border. The central image depicts a rider being thrown from a rearing horse in a landscape with buildings and ruins in the distance. The rider, wearing a plumed hat, appears to be losing control as the horse bucks. The surrounding border is highly ornate: the top features a cartouche flanked by clusters of fruit and foliage; the sides are composed of vertical columns of muscular male figures (atlantes) stacked atop one another; the bottom depicts two reclining, semi-human grotesque figures flanking a central footed bowl filled with fruit.
</image-desc>

*Do you wish to know why the land of Thessaly* <note>Thessaly, a region in northern Greece, was renowned in the ancient world for its superior cavalry and high-spirited horses.</note> *so often*
*Changes its lords, and seeks to have various leaders?*
*It knows not how to flatter, nor to stroke anyone with a fawning touch,* <note>original: *obtrudere palpum* — literally "to thrust a caress," a Latin idiom for acting as a sycophant or flatterer.</note>
*A custom which the court of every prince possesses.*
*But just like a noble steed,* <note>original: *sonipes* — literally "noisy-footed," a poetic Latin term for a spirited horse.</note> *it shakes from its back every*
*Groom* <note>original: *Hippocomon* — a term derived from the Greek *hippokomos*, meaning a horse-tender or stable hand.</note> *who knows not how to manage it.*
*Nor is it right to serve such a master. The only revenge is*
*That he commands the wild beast to bear even harsher jagged bits.* <note>original: *lupata* — literally "wolf-bits," these were sharp, jagged horse bits used to control particularly stubborn or high-spirited animals.</note>

<vocab><term>Fortitudo</term> (Fortitude): The virtue of courage and strength of mind. <term>Thessalis</term> (Thessalian): Relating to the region of Thessaly. <term>Hippocomon</term>: A groom or horse-handler. <term>Lupata</term>: A jagged horse bit. <term>Adulari</term>: To flatter or fawn. <term>Sonipes</term>: A poetic term for a horse.</vocab>

<summary>This emblem uses the metaphor of a spirited Thessalian horse to represent a people or an individual of high character who refuses to engage in courtly flattery. It suggests that a noble spirit would rather suffer harsh discipline than lose its integrity to an unworthy master.</summary>
<keywords>fortitude, flattery, Thessaly, horses, integrity, political allegory, noble spirit, resistance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Fortitude. While the previous page (38) focused on political integrity and the refusal to flatter, this page shifts to personal resilience. It employs the classic emblem of the palm tree—which was anciently believed to grow stronger and arch upwards when weighed down—to symbolize how a steadfast spirit thrives under pressure.</meta>

<page-num>39</page-num>
<header>F O R T I T U D E.</header>
<sig>C 4</sig>

<image-desc>A central woodcut illustration is enclosed within an elaborate ornamental border featuring scrolling cartouches, a grotesque mask at the top, and a burning urn on a pedestal at the base. The illustration depicts a young nude figure, possibly a putto, climbing the fronds of a palm tree that is heavily bowed into an arch. In the background, a landscape unfolds with a winding river and a cluster of buildings or ruins atop a hill. The scene symbolizes resilience and strength, as the palm tree is known to grow stronger under weight.</image-desc>

## One must endure against
## pressing weights.

*The palm tree strives against the weight: and rises into an arch,* <note>The author references a common Renaissance belief, originating in Aristotle and Pliny, that palm wood is unique because it resists downward pressure, curving upward against any load placed upon it.</note>
*The more it is pressed down: the more it lifts the burden.*
*It also bears sweet dates as fragrant delicacies,* <note>original: *bellaria* — a Latin term for dessert, dainties, or sweetmeats served at the end of a meal. The "acorns" (*glandes*) mentioned in the Latin text refer here to the fruit of the palm, or dates.</note>
*Which are held in the highest honor among the banquet tables.*
*Go, boy, and crawling among the branches gather these; he who*
*Possesses a steadfast mind: shall carry away worthy rewards.*

<note>The poem serves as an allegory for the human spirit: the "boy" represents the soul or the seeker of virtue, the "weight" represents life's hardships, and the "sweet dates" represent the wisdom and rewards gained only by those who do not break under pressure.</note>

<summary>This page uses the natural metaphor of the palm tree to illustrate the virtue of resilience. It suggests that just as the palm grows stronger and bears fruit when weighed down, the steadfast individual achieves greatness through adversity.</summary>

<keywords>resilience, palm tree, fortitude, endurance, rewards of virtue, moral strength, steadfastness</keywords>

# C O N C O R D.

## The Symbol of Concord.

<image-desc>A woodcut emblem contained within a large, highly decorative Renaissance border. The central illustration shows four crows (or ravens): two are perched on the edges of a classical stone altar or tomb, and two fly in the sky above. A tall, tapered scepter or monument stands upright from the center of the altar. The altar itself is adorned with a carved face and scrollwork. The surrounding border is rich with architectural details, grotesque masks, winged figures, and elaborate strapwork. At the bottom center, within the frame, is an ornamental vase or urn from which flames emerge.</image-desc>

-> Among crows, there is a marvelous harmony of life, <note>original: *Cornicum* — Crows or ravens were frequently used in Renaissance emblems to represent social unity and long-lasting companionship.</note> <-
-> And their mutual loyalty is never defiled. <note>original: *fides* — This term implies a mixture of faith, trust, and political allegiance.</note> <-
-> For this reason, these birds carry the scepter, for surely all <-
-> Leaders stand and fall by the consensus of the people: <note>The "scepter" topped with a bird is a traditional symbol of authority that is rooted in popular support rather than mere force.</note> <-
-> If you remove this consensus from their midst, headlong discord <-
-> Flies in, and drags the fate of the kingdom with it. <-

| | |
|---|---|
| **Concordia** | Harmony |
| **Symbolum** | Symbol or Emblem |
| **Cornicum** | Of crows |
| **fides** | Loyalty or faith |
| **sceptra** | Scepters (symbols of power) |
| **populi** | Of the people |
| **duces** | Leaders or guides |
| **discordia** | Strife or disagreement |

<summary>This page employs the crow as a symbol of social and political harmony, emphasizing that a ruler's authority depends entirely on the unified consent of their subjects. It warns that without this collective "concord," a kingdom is doomed to fall into ruin through discord.</summary>

<keywords>concord, harmony, crows, political consensus, leadership, loyalty, scepter, discord, social stability</keywords>

### Concord.

<image-desc>The entire page is enclosed within a highly ornate woodcut border featuring architectural scrolls, grotesque masks at the bottom and top corners, and figures. Centered within this frame is a woodcut illustration depicting two military leaders in classical Roman armor, including helmets with plumes and breastplates, shaking right hands (dextrarum iunctio). In the background are military tents and other soldiers, suggesting a scene of reconciliation or treaty in a camp setting.</image-desc>

When Rome’s leaders were readying for civil war,
And the land of Mars <note>original: *Martia terra* — A poetic reference to Rome, named for its legendary founder Romulus, the son of Mars, the god of war.</note> was falling by its own might:
It was the custom, as the ranks gathered into the same cause,
To offer joined right hands <note>original: *dextras* — The *dextrarum iunctio* (joining of right hands) was a sacred Roman gesture signifying friendship, marriage, or military alliance.</note> as mutual gifts.
This is the form of a treaty; Concord <note>Concordia was also the name of the Roman goddess of agreement and understanding.</note> holds this sign,
So that those whom love unites, the hand itself might also unite.

<summary>This page illustrates the Roman tradition of the handshake (dextrarum iunctio) as a symbol of Concord, specifically as a remedy for the destruction of civil war. It emphasizes that physical gestures of peace are the outward signs of an internal, loving commitment to unity.</summary>

## One is nothing; two are most powerful.

<image-desc>A central woodcut is framed by an ornate architectural border. At the top, two reclining male figures lie back-to-back on a plinth. The side borders are designed as decorative columns or herms: the left column features a male figure holding a shield marked with the letter "P", while the right column features a female figure with a shield marked with the letter "V". On the right, a warrior (Diomedes) in classical armor, complete with a plumed helmet and shield, holds a long spear. They appear to be in conversation.</image-desc>

> The son of Laertes <note>Ulysses (Odysseus), known for his cunning and intellect.</note> and the son of Tydeus <note>Diomedes, one of the greatest Greek warriors at Troy.</note> together
> The skilled hand of Zenalis <note>Likely referring to the artist or engraver who created the original image.</note> has expressed here in wax.
> This one [Diomedes] excels in strength, the other [Ulysses] is powerful in sharpness of mind,
> Yet neither can do without the help of the other.
> When two come joined together, victory is certain.
> A man is failed by either his mind alone, or by his right hand <note>original: *dextra* — representing physical force or military action.</note> alone.

<summary>This emblem uses the partnership of the Greek heroes Ulysses and Diomedes to argue that success requires the union of strategic wisdom and physical courage. It emphasizes that neither intellect nor strength is sufficient on its own to ensure victory.</summary>

While page 43 focused on the cooperation between individuals, page 44 focuses on the collective resilience of the Empire under its leader, asserting that a unified state remains firm even against the most violent external forces.</meta>

### The firmest things
### cannot be shaken.
<note>original: *Firmissima convelli non posse* — Literally, "the strongest things cannot be uprooted." This serves as the motto for the emblem below.</note>

<image-desc>A central woodcut illustration depicts a sturdy oak tree on a rocky mound, resisting a storm. The background shows a vast body of water, likely the Danube or the ocean, with a distant coastline featuring a town and mountains.</image-desc>

->Though Father Ocean <note>The personification of the sea.</note> stirs up every wave:<-
->And though you, barbarian Turk, <note>A reference to the Ottoman Empire, specifically Sultan Suleiman the Magnificent, who was the primary rival of Charles V.</note> may drink the whole Danube.<-

->Charles <note>Emperor Charles V (reigned 1519–1556), the ruler of the Holy Roman Empire and the Spanish Empire.</note> gives the battle signals to his people.<-

->Even if the winds shake their dry leaves.<-

<summary>This emblem features a storm-tossed oak tree to symbolize the political and military resilience of the Holy Roman Empire. It asserts that under the leadership of Emperor Charles V, the state remains unshakeable despite the looming threats of the Ottoman Empire and external "storms."</summary>

# H O P E .

->Hope is at hand.<- <note>original: *Spes proxima* — Suggesting that relief or salvation is nearby even in the midst of danger.</note>

<image-desc>
Within a smaller rectangular frame in the center of the page is a woodcut emblem depicting a large, multi-masted sailing ship with full sails, tossed on a stormy sea with high waves. In the top left corner of this frame, two stars (representing Castor and Pollux) are visible in the sky. A faint ray of light or sunburst appears at the top right.
</image-desc>

Our state <note>original: *respub.* (respublica) — The "commonwealth" or "Republic," referring here to the Holy Roman Empire or the collective body of Christendom.</note> is tossed by countless storms,
And the hope of coming safety is all that remains:
Just like a ship in the middle of the sea, which the winds
Seize and carry away; and now it leaks from the salt waters.
But if the shining stars, the brothers of Helen, <note>original: *Helenæ fratres* — A reference to Castor and Pollux, the twin sons of Leda and brothers of Helen of Troy. In classical mythology, they were the protectors of sailors. Their appearance as "stars" (often associated with the electrical phenomenon known as St. Elmo’s Fire) was believed to signal the end of a storm.</note> should appear:
Good hope restores our lost courage.

<summary>This page uses a maritime metaphor to describe the state of the Empire, comparing the political and religious turmoil of the era to a ship leaking in a storm, only saved by the "stars" of hope. It identifies divine or providential intervention (symbolized by Castor and Pollux) as the source of national resilience.</summary>
<keywords>Hope, Republic, ship of state, maritime metaphor, Castor and Pollux, storms, salvation, resilience</keywords>

## On the Image of Hope. <note>original: *In simulachrum Spei*</note>

### A DIALOGUE. <note>original: *Dialogismus* — A rhetorical term for a conversation or a speech written for a specific character.</note>

<image-desc>A central rectangular woodcut illustration depicts an allegorical scene. A female figure representing Hope (Spes) sits on a stool, gazing upward toward stars in the sky. To her left stands a female attendant. In front of her is a small child. To her right stands a muscular man wearing a lion skin and carrying a heavy club (Hercules), holding a shallow vessel aloft. At Hope's feet lie broken arrows and spears. In the background, an arched doorway is visible.</image-desc>

->Which Goddess is this, looking up at the stars with such a joyful expression?<-
->By whose brushes was this image produced?<-
->The hands of Elpidius <note>original: *Elpidij* — A name derived from the Greek *elpis*, meaning "hope." This suggests that Hope is the creator of her own image, or a pun on the artist's name.</note> made me. I am known as she,<-
->Kind Hope, who offers ready aid to the wretched.<-
->Why is your cloak green? Because under my guidance, all things turn to spring. <note>original: *vernent* — To flourish or grow green like springtime; a symbol of rebirth and growth.</note><-
->Why do you carry the broken weapons of death in your hands?<- <note>In the accompanying woodcut, these weapons are shown shattered at her feet, representing Hope’s power to overcome despair and the finality of death.</note>

<summary>This page presents an allegorical dialogue explaining the iconography of Hope, specifically her joyful gaze toward the heavens, her green garments signifying renewal, and her triumph over the "weapons of death."</summary>
<keywords>Hope, Elpidius, allegory, spring, renewal, Hercules, stars, iconography</keywords>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>H O P E <note>original: *SPES*</note></header>

<image-desc>The page is framed by a complex woodcut border featuring various human figures intertwined with vines, clusters of grapes, masks, and architectural elements.</image-desc>

Because it is proper for the living to hope, I cut hope off from the buried.
Why do you sit idle upon the lid of a small jar? <note>original: *dolioli*. A reference to Pandora’s jar (often called a "box" in later traditions), which contained all the world's evils but kept Hope trapped inside.</note>
I alone remained at home while evils flew about everywhere,
As the venerable Muse of the old man of Ascra taught. <note>original: *Ascræi... senis*. Refers to the Greek poet Hesiod, who was born in Ascra and wrote the story of Pandora.</note>
Which bird is present with you? The crow, a most faithful bird of omen; <note>term: *oscen* — a bird whose chirping or song provided omens in Roman divination.</note>
When it cannot say "all is well," it says: "It will be." <note>The crow’s call, *cras*, is the Latin word for "tomorrow." Thus, the crow is the bird of Hope because it always speaks of the future.</note>
Who are your companions? Good Fortune, and headlong Desire.
Who go before you? They are called the vain dreams of those who wake.
Who stands joined to you? The Rhamnusian avenger of crimes, <note>original: *Rhamnusia*. An epithet for the goddess Nemesis, who had a famous sanctuary at Rhamnus. She ensures that Hope remains tempered by justice.</note>
To be sure, so that you may hope for nothing except what is permitted.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut vignette depicting a central reclining figure surrounded by other figures and ornamental scrolls within a framed cartouche.</image-desc>

->Better things every day.<- <note>original: *In dies meliora*.</note>

A client offered me the snout of a bristly boar for the new year,
And said, "Accept these guest-gifts for your belly." <note>term: *xenia* — gifts given to a guest or host, or seasonal presents.</note>
The boar always moves forward, and never looks back,
When, ravenous, it roots up the grass with its curved snout.
Men have the same concern: lest hope, having slipped,
Should fall backward; and that what is further ahead might also be better. <note>The boar's physical habit of rooting only in a forward direction is used here as a metaphor for the human drive to look toward the future rather than dwelling on the past.</note>

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut tailpiece featuring two owls with outspread wings flanking a central structure, with a mask at the very bottom.</image-desc>

<summary>This page explains the symbols of Hope, linking her to Pandora's jar and the crow that promises "tomorrow." It also uses the metaphor of a rooting boar to illustrate the necessity of always looking forward toward progress.</summary>

# V I C E S. <note>original: *VITIA*</note>

## P E R F I D Y. <note>original: *PERFIDIA*</note>

->On a victory obtained through deceit. <note>original: *In victoriam dolo partam*</note><-

<image-desc>The page features an emblem composed of three main parts. At the top, a heading and title. > I should be defeated, and that deceit <note>original: *dolus*</note> should prevail with a stronger case.

### On the fraudulent. <note>original: *In fraudulentos*</note>

A small lizard, its body dappled with dark spots,
Is the Star-Lizard <note>original: *Stellio*; likely a gecko or spotted lizard. In ancient lore, this creature was considered a symbol of envy and fraud because it was believed to "swallow" its own shed skin to prevent humans from using it for medicine.</note>, which dwells in hidden places and hollow tombs.
Painted with these marks, it bears the symbols of envy and wicked craft.
Alas, it is known all too well by jealous daughters-in-law <note>original: *nuribus*; can also refer to young wives or women in a household.</note>.
For whoever drinks wine in which a Star-Lizard has been drowned
Will have their face covered in foul freckles. <note>original: *lentigine*. This reflects a Renaissance-era folk belief that wine tainted by this lizard caused skin disfigurement.</note>
Thus, vengeance is often taken upon a rival <note>original: *pellice*; specifically a mistress or "the other woman."</note> through such wine,
Whom the lover then abandons once the flower of her beauty is lost.

### Deceit against one’s own. <note>original: *Dolus in suos*</note>

A fattened decoy duck, <note>original: *allectator anas*; a bird trained to lure others into a trap.</note> with feathers of leaden blue,
Is accustomed to coming and going at its master’s call.
Seeing flocks of its own kind flying through the air,
It chatters away and joins itself to their group,
Leading the unsuspecting birds into the outspread nets.
The captives cry out in distress, but the traitor herself stays silent.
This treacherous bird defiles herself with the blood of her own kin—
Dutiful to others, but a bringer of destruction to her own.

### Slander. <note>original: *Maledicentia*</note>

They say that wasps are carved from marble upon the tomb
Of Archilochus <note>A Greek poet from the 7th century BCE, famous for his stinging, aggressive satire. Legend says his verses were so sharp that those he mocked, such as Lycambes and his daughters, were driven to hang themselves.</note>—sure seals of a wicked tongue.

<summary>This page uses three emblems—the spotted lizard, the decoy duck, and the wasps of Archilochus—to illustrate different facets of treachery, ranging from the sabotage of beauty to the betrayal of one's own family and the lethal power of a cruel tongue.</summary>
<keywords>treachery, fraud, envy, Stellio, decoy duck, Archilochus, slander, betrayal, Renaissance folklore</keywords>

## Against flatterers. <note>original: *In adulatores*</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular frame, itself set inside a larger ornamental architectural border. The image depicts a chameleon with a textured, scaly body and a long, curled tail, standing on rocky terrain. The background consists of a landscape with several slender trees and distant hills. A small flock of birds is visible in the sky. ->Always gaping, the chameleon draws in and out<-
->the thin air on which it feeds,<-
->And changes its appearance, taking on various colors,<-
->Except for red, or white: <note>In Renaissance natural history, it was believed that chameleons could mimic any color except for white (symbolizing purity) or red (symbolizing the blush of shame or modesty).</note><-
->So also the Flatterer feeds on the popular breeze,<-
->And gaping wide, he devours all things.<-
->He imitates only the dark <note>original: *atros*; implies something black, foul, or wicked.</note> habits of the prince,<-
->Ignorant of what is white <note>original: *albi*; symbolizing honesty and integrity.</note> and virtuous.<-

<term>Treachery</term> <note>original: *Perfidia*; the overarching theme of this section of the book, covering various forms of faithlessness.</note>, <term>Against flatterers</term>, <term>Chameleon</term>, <term>Flatterer</term> <note>original: *Adulator*; specifically one who uses insincere praise to gain advantage.</note>, <term>habits of the prince</term> <note>original: *mores principis*; referring to the moral conduct of a ruler which the flatterer mimics to stay in favor.</note>

<summary>This page employs the chameleon as a natural metaphor for the sycophant, illustrating how flatterers mimic the vices of their superiors while remaining incapable of reflecting true virtue or honesty.</summary>

## One who has once squandered their own wealth should not be trusted with that of others.

<image-desc>An emblem illustration set within an elaborate woodcut architectural frame. The frame consists of two fluted columns supporting a pediment at the top and a decorative base at the bottom. Both the top and bottom sections feature central ornamental masks—the top one appears distressed, while the bottom one is more grotesque and surrounded by foliate scrolls. The central image depicts a bird in flight in the upper left, carrying a snake or twig. To the right is a substantial stone building with a turreted tower. In the background, a road or river passes through a village with several houses, leading toward distant mountains under a cloudy sky. A figure is visible within a doorway or opening in the building on the right.</image-desc>

->Why do you build your nest in the lap of the woman from Colchis? <note>original: *Colchidos*; referring to the mythological sorceress Medea, who hailed from the Kingdom of Colchis.</note> Alas!<-
->O unknowing bird, why do you so foolishly <note>original: *tam malè*; literally "so badly."</note> entrust your chicks to her?<-
->The dreadful Medea, that most cruel parent, destroyed her own children, <note>In Greek mythology, Medea murdered her own sons to take revenge on her husband, Jason, after he abandoned her.</note><-
->And do you hope that she might spare yours?<-

<term>Treachery</term> <note>original: *Perfidia*; in the Renaissance context, this term implies more than just a lie; it suggests a total violation of "fides" (trust or faith) which was considered the foundation of social and legal order.</note>

<summary>This emblem uses the mythological figure of Medea, famous for murdering her own children, to argue that those who have already betrayed their closest kin or wasted their own resources are fundamentally unworthy of trust. It warns that a person's past actions are the best predictors of their future behavior toward others.</summary>

## Recklessness. <note>original: *Temeritas*</note>

> A charioteer <note>original: *Auriga*; in classical literature, the charioteer often symbolizes Reason trying to control the "horses" of passion.</note> is swept headlong, and pulls the reins in vain,
> He whom a horse with an unbridled mouth carries.
> You should not easily trust this man, whom no reason governs,
> And who is rashly led by his own whim.

## Fury and Madness. <note>original: *Furor, & rabies*</note>

> And has a verse inscribed upon its upper rim:
> "This is the terror of men," whose owner, the great-souled Agamemnon,
> The son of Atreus, <note>original: *Atrida*; a title for Agamemnon, the king who led the Greeks during the Trojan War.</note> carried such insignia.

### Against the Rash. <note>original: *In temerarios*; the Latin *temeritas* refers to a dangerous boldness or a lack of foresight.</note>

<image-desc>An elaborate woodcut frame surrounds the page's content, featuring architectural elements, scrolls, and figures. At the top center is a small crowned head with wings. ->In this same way, many Kings are carried toward the stars by the wheels<-

->they finally pay the price for all their wicked deeds.<-

**Vocabulary:** <note>These terms highlight the central themes of the poem.</note>

## Against those who attempt things beyond their strength. <note>original: *In eos qui supra vires quicquam audent*</note>

<image-desc>A central woodcut illustration is framed by an elaborate ornamental border featuring scrolls, foliage, caryatids on the sides, and a central mask at the top. The illustration depicts the giant Hercules (Alcides) sleeping while leaning against a tree, his head resting on his hand. He is wearing the Nemean lion skin. Surrounding his feet and legs are several tiny Pygmies; some are armed with clubs and spears, and one in the lower right foreground is kneeling and preparing to shoot a miniature bow. The background shows a hilly landscape under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

->While he sleeps, and restores his body with sweet slumber<-
->Beneath a spruce tree, still holding his club and other arms,<-
->A band <note>original: *manus*; literally "a hand," often used to describe a small troop or company.</note> of Pygmies thinks it can lay Alcides <note>Alcides is a common epithet for the hero Hercules, referring to his lineage through his grandfather Alceus.</note> low<-
->In death: <note>original: *letho*</note> for they have not rightly learned their own strength.<-
->Once awakened, he crushes the enemy just as if they were fleas,<-
->And carries them off, bundled within the skin of the fierce lion.<-

<note>**Vocabulary:** **Folly** (*Stultitia*); **Alcides** (Hercules); **Pygmy** (*pygmæa*); **Death** (*letho*); **Nemean lion skin** (the impenetrable pelt of the lion Hercules slew as his first labor, often used as his cloak and armor).</note>

<summary>This page uses the mythological encounter between the sleeping Hercules and the tiny Pygmies to satirize those who lack self-awareness and foolishly challenge much greater powers. It serves as a moral warning against overestimating one's own capabilities while underestimating an opponent's strength.</summary>

<keywords>Hercules, Pygmies, Folly, Alcides, Nemean Lion, disproportionate strength, arrogance, Greek mythology in emblems</keywords>

### Cuckoos <note>original: *Cuculi*</note>

Why do many call the country folk—that rustic race—"cuckoos"? What reason for this has been handed down? 
In the early spring, the Cuckoo <note>original: *Coccyx*; the Greek name for the bird</note> sings; at this time, any man who has not yet finished pruning his vines is rightfully branded as lazy <note>This refers to an ancient Roman proverb: a farmer still pruning when the cuckoo returned was considered late and "foolish" for his poor timing.</note>.
The bird carries its eggs into the nests of others, much like a man whose wife betrays their marriage bed through adultery.

### Anger <note>original: *Ira*</note>

The ancients used the word *alcaea* <note>original: *Alcæam*; a term used by classical naturalists for the tuft or "sting" at the end of a lion's tail</note> for the tail of the Lion, by which he whips himself into a great rage.
When the yellow bile <note>original: *luthea... bilis*</note> rises, the pain grows raw with black gall <note>original: *atro felle*</note>; it stirs up an untamable fury. <note>This imagery relies on the "Four Humors" theory of medicine, which taught that an excess of bile or "choler" caused violent outbursts of anger.</note>

<note>**Vocabulary:** **Folly** (*Stultitia*); **Cuckoos** (*Cuculi*); **Cuckoo** (*Coccyx*); **Anger** (*Ira*); **Lion's tail** (*Alcæam Leonis*); **Bile/Gall** (*bilis*).</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the theme of Folly through two lenses: the "Cuckoo," which symbolizes the laziness of the late farmer and the shame of the cuckold, and "Anger," depicted as a biological madness akin to a lion goading itself into a frenzy.</summary>
<keywords>folly, cuckoo, laziness, adultery, anger, lion, humors, yellow bile, four humors, classical proverbs</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---

This page continues the theme of Folly (Stultitia) by moving from individual character flaws to the folly of misplaced mercy and the danger of nurturing one's own enemy.</meta>

### Regarding one who prepares for his own destruction <note>original: *In eum qui sibi ipsi damnum apparat*</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration enclosed within a large, elaborate Renaissance-style border featuring grotesque faces, scrolls, and winged figures. ->I, a goat, nurse a wolf from my own udder—not by choice,<-
->But because the shepherd’s ill-judged care <note>original: *provida cura*; ironically referring to the shepherd's "foresight" which is actually a lack thereof</note> commands it.<-
->As soon as he has grown, he will feast on me, having finished my milk.<-
->Wickedness <note>original: *Improbitas*; refers to an inherent depravity or bad character</note> is swayed by no act of kindness.<-

<note>**Vocabulary:** **Folly** (*Stultitia*); **Goat** (*Capra*); **Wolf** (*Lupus*); **Wickedness** (*Improbitas*); **emblem** <note>An "emblem" is a traditional Renaissance pairing of a title, an image, and a short poem that delivers a moral lesson.</note>.</note>

<summary>This page features an emblem illustrating the folly of misplaced kindness, using the metaphor of a goat forced by a shepherd to nurse a wolf cub that will eventually devour her. It serves as a warning that an inherently evil nature cannot be changed by nurture or mercy.</summary>

### Folly <note>original: *Fatuitas*</note>

You wonder why in our song you are called <term>Otus <note>A type of horned or eared owl (*Asio otus*). In classical lore, this bird was considered easily caught because it would "dance" or mimic the movements of a hunter to its own capture.</note></term>,
Though the name **Otho** has been yours from your ancestors.
The bird is long-eared, and possesses feathers just like an owl,
And the skillful birdcatcher captures the dancing bird.
Hence we call those who are foolish, and easily caught, **oti**,
May you also hold this name, as it is well-suited to you.

## PRIDE <note>original: *S V P E R B I A*</note>

### Pride <note>original: *Superbia*</note>

Behold a statue of a statue, and marble drawn from marble:
The impudent <term>Niobe <note>In Greek mythology, Niobe boasted of her fourteen children to the goddess Leto, who only had two. As punishment, Leto's children (Apollo and Artemis) killed all of Niobe’s children, and Niobe was turned to stone by grief.</note></term> dared to compare herself to the gods.
Pride is a womanly vice, and it reveals a hardness
Of face and of feeling, just like that which dwells within a stone.

### Impudence <note>original: *Impudentia*</note>

A woman down to her loins, girt below by barking
Monster-cubs—such was the two-formed **Scylla**.
These monsters are understood as greed, audacity, and plunder.
But **Scylla** is one who has no shame <note>original: *pudor*</note> in their face.

### Self-Love <note>original: *Φιλαυτία* / *Philautia*</note>

Because your own beauty pleased you too much, **Narcissus**,
It was turned into a flower—the herb of a well-known numbness <note>original: *stuporis*; a play on the word "narcotic," derived from the same root as Narcissus, referring to the numbing effect of self-obsession.</note>.
**Self-love** <note>original: *Philautia*</note> is a decay and a disaster of the mind,
Which brings, and has brought, many learned men to ruin.
These are the ones who, having cast aside the ancient method, seek new dogmas,
And deliver nothing to the world except their own fantasies.

<summary>This page explores various facets of Pride and intellectual vanity through the figures of Niobe, Scylla, and Narcissus. It concludes with a sharp critique of scholars who abandon tradition to promote their own "fantasies," framing self-love as a destructive mental decay.</summary>

<keywords>Pride, Folly, Niobe, Scylla, Narcissus, Self-Love, Philautia, Otho, mythology, intellectual vanity, Renaissance emblems</keywords>

### Garrulity <note>original: *Garrulitas*</note>

> Why, talkative <term>Procne <note>original: *Progne*. In Greek myth, Procne was turned into a swallow, a bird often associated with morning chatter.</note></term>, do you break my morning sleep,
> And sing with your noisy <term>Daulian <note>original: *Daulias*. Referring to Daulis, the city in Phocis where the tragic myth of Procne, Philomela, and Tereus took place.</note></term> mouth?

### E N V Y <note>original: *I N V I D I A*</note>

> A squalid woman eating the flesh of vipers,
> Whose eyes are pained, and who eats her own heart,
> Whom leanness and paleness possess, and who carries 
> Thorny weapons in her hand. Such is the depiction of Envy.
> <note>The iconography of Envy here follows the classical tradition of Ovid: a figure consumed by her own bitterness, unable to look directly at the success of others without pain.</note>

### L U X U R Y <note>original: *L V X V R I A*</note>

### Luxury <note>original: *Luxuria* <note>In this context, *Luxuria* translates more accurately as Lust or Lechery, rather than modern "luxury."</note></note>

> A goat-footed <term>Faunus <note>A rustic Roman god of the forest, often depicted with goat-like features and associated with unbridled sexual nature.</note></term>, his temples crowned with <term>arugula <note>original: *Eruca*. This plant was famously regarded as a powerful aphrodisiac by Roman poets like Ovid and Martial.</note></term>,
> Brings back the certain symbols of immoderate <term>Venus <note>The goddess of love and sexual desire.</note></term>.
> Arugula is provocative, and the goat is a sign of lust,
> And satyrs are always accustomed to loving nymphs.

### The wealth of the luxurious <note>original: *Luxuriosorum opes*</note>

> On airy cliffs, and on the edge of the highest rock,
> The bitter fig tree produces unripe fruits.
> Which ravens eat, which the wicked crow devours,
> Which have no human usefulness.
> So do parasites and harlots enjoy the wealth of fools:
> And they help the righteous with no benefit.
> <note>The author uses the metaphor of a fig tree growing in an inaccessible place; its fruit is wasted on scavengers (representing social parasites) rather than being used for the common good.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the vices of talkativeness, envy, and lust through mythological allegories, using figures like Procne and Faunus to warn against lack of restraint. It concludes with a moralizing epigram on how the wealth of the dissolute is wasted on unworthy companions rather than helping the virtuous.</summary>

# The Courtesan's Tomb. <note>original: *Tumulus meretricis*</note>
->In the form of a dialogue <note>original: *διαλογικῶς* (dialogikōs)</note><-

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration framed by an ornate, interlaced decorative border. The central image depicts a tomb in a landscape of classical ruins. On top of the rectangular sarcophagus lies a reclining female figure, presumably representing Lais of Corinth. The side of the sarcophagus features a relief carving of a lioness attacking a ram, biting into its haunch. Whose tomb is this? Whose urn? It belongs to <term>Ephyraean <note>original: *Ephyrææ*. Ephyra was the ancient name for the city of Corinth.</note></term> <term>Lais <note>Lais of Corinth was a famous courtesan of ancient Greece, renowned for her extraordinary beauty and the high fees she charged her lovers.</note></term>. Alas,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Did the <term>Fate <note>original: *Parca*. One of the three goddesses who spun, measured, and cut the thread of human life.</note></term> not blush to destroy such great beauty?
No beauty remained then, for age had already plucked it away;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As a cautious old woman, she had already dedicated her mirror to <term>Venus <note>The goddess of love. This refers to an ancient epigram (often attributed to Plato) where the aging Lais offers her mirror to Venus because she can no longer see her former self in it and does not wish to see her current reflection.</note></term>.
What does the carved ram signify, which the lioness
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Holds, seized by its hindquarters in her claws?
In just this way she used to hold her captured lovers;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The ram is the leader of the flock, and the lover is held by the haunch.

### Against the lovers of harlots. <note>original: *In amatores meretricum*</note>

> The fisherman, clad in the shaggy hide of a goat,
> Having added two horns to his own head,
> Standing at the very edge of the shore, deceives the amorous <term>Sargus <note>The *Sargus* (White Seabream) was a fish that ancient naturalists, such as Oppian and Pliny, believed was so uncontrollably attracted to goats that it would leap from the water to meet them. Fishermen supposedly exploited this by dressing in goatskins.</note></term>;
> Driven into the nets by his burning desire for the snub-nosed flock <note>A poetic reference to goats, who were often described by their "snub" or "flat" noses (*simus*).</note>.
> The goat represents the harlot; the Sargus becomes like the lover,
> Who, wretchedly captured by a base love, perishes.

Lust, harlot, fisherman, goat, Sargus-fish, lover.

## One Must Beware of Harlots. <note>original: *Cauendum à meretricibus*</note>

## The Amulet of Venus. <note>original: *Amuletum Veneris*. In this context, an "amulet" or charm refers to a natural remedy or preventative measure against the heat of passion.</note>

> As the lustful <term>rocket <note>original: *eruca*. Known today as arugula, this plant was celebrated by ancient poets like Ovid and Martial as a potent aphrodisiac.</note></term> can scarcely stimulate it.

<header>L U X U R Y</header>

### Invulnerable to Cupid’s Arrow. <note>original: *Inuiolabiles telo Cupidinis*</note>

<image-desc>A detailed woodcut emblem within an ornate architectural frame. The border features two terminal figures (caryatids) on the left and right, with grotesque masks positioned at the top and bottom center. The central illustration depicts a bird (identified as a motacilla or wryneck) spread across a four-spoked wheel (an iynx-wheel), with its head, tail, and wings stretched to form a cross shape. The scene is set in a landscape with hills and water under a rising or setting sun.</image-desc>

Lest dreadful love conquer you, or any woman 
Shatter your mind with her magical arts:
Let the Bacchic bird, the <term>wagtail <note>original: *motacilla*. Historically associated with the *iynx* or wryneck, a bird used in ancient Greek love magic to attract lovers or, as seen here, to provide a counter-charm against such spells.</note></term>, be ready at your hand,
Which you shall place within the circle of a four-spoked wheel:
So that it forms a cross with its beak and tail, and with its twin wings;
Such an amulet will serve [as protection] against every magic spell.
It is said that by this sign of Venus, <term>Jason of Pagasae <note>The leader of the Argonauts. Pagasae was the port from which the Argo set sail.</note></term>
Could not be harmed by the <term>Phasian deceits <note>original: *Phasiacis dolis*. This refers to the sorcery of Medea, who lived by the Phasis River in Colchis. In myth, Aphrodite (Venus) gave Jason the iynx-wheel to win Medea's love, but here the emblem interprets it as a protective charm against her magic.</note></term>.

<sig>E</sig>

<vocab>Lust, Cupid, wagtail, Jason, Venus, amulet, Pagasaean, Phasian (Colchian)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes a mythological amulet—a wagtail bird bound to a four-spoked wheel—used to protect the mind from the "magic" of lust and seduction. It cites the success of the hero Jason against the sorcery of Medea as proof of the charm's efficacy.</summary>

### Wantonness. A Riddle. <note>original: *Lasciuia. Problema*</note>

*A white mouse is believed to signify*
*Delights and softness, yet the reason is not quite clear to me.*
*Is it because its nature is lecherous, and its lust is great?*
*Or because it adorns the skin of Roman brides?* <note>The "white mouse" refers to the ermine, whose fur was a prized luxury in garment making. The author questions whether the animal represents lust because of its biological nature or because its fur is a symbol of wealthy, "soft" living.</note>
*Most people call the Sarmatian mouse the <term>Sable <note>original: *Zibellum*</note></term>,*
*And the Arabian musk is famous for its sweet ointment.* <note>Sarmatia was a region of Eastern Europe; the author connects exotic furs and perfumes to the theme of "wantonness" or excessive luxury.</note>

### Those Sinning Against Nature.

*It is a shameful thing to do, and a wicked thing even to say,*
*If anyone should receive the burden of the belly in a <term>quart-measure <note>original: *chœnice*. A Greek unit of dry volume, used here as a metaphor for violating the natural "measure" or order of things.</note></term>.*
*This exceeds the limit of measure and of sacred law,*
*Just as it would be to be defiled by incestuous adultery.*

->S L O T H<-

### Sloth.

*The <term>Essene <note>original: *Essæus*. A member of an ancient Jewish ascetic sect. Here, the author uses the figure to represent a hermit who withdraws so far from society that his wisdom becomes useless.</note></term> sits idle in a bushel; an observer of the stars,*
*He covers his lit torch underneath it.* <note>An allusion to the Gospel of Matthew (5:15): "Neither do men light a candle, and put it under a bushel." The author argues that hiding one's talents or knowledge is a form of Sloth.</note>
*Sloth, veiled in a monk's cowl under the appearance of righteousness,*
*Benefits neither himself nor others with any usefulness.*

<vocab>Wantonness, Sloth, white mouse (ermine), Sable, Arabian musk, Essene, lust, sinners</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the luxuries associated with wantonness (such as fine furs and perfumes) and introduces the vice of Sloth, depicted as a "righteous" withdrawal from the world that fails to benefit society.</summary>

### Sloth Must Be Cast Aside. <note>original: *Deſidiam abijciendam*</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration set within a rectangular frame and surrounded by an expansive, ornate decorative border. <gloss>Author: Hadrianus Iunius of Batavia <note>Hadrianus Iunius (1511–1575) was a Dutch physician and humanist scholar. "Batavia" refers to the Netherlands.</note> <unclear>...</unclear></gloss>

->*Let whoever is idle depart. For to sit upon a <term>quart-measure <note>original: *chœnice*. A Greek dry measure of grain, roughly a daily ration. ## On those who easily fall away from virtue. <note>original: *In facilè à virtute desciscentes*</note>

->Just as the small <term>Remora <note>A suckerfish. In classical antiquity and medieval bestiaries, the Remora was a legendary creature believed to possess the miraculous power to stop a massive sailing ship in its tracks simply by clinging to the hull, regardless of the wind or the strength of the rowers.</note></term>, like a snail, despite the force of the wind,<-
->and the oars, is able alone to halt a ship.<-
->So some, who were carried toward the stars <note>original: *sydera*. Metaphorically referring to the heights of fame, divinity, or intellectual achievement.</note> by talent and virtue,<-
->are held back in the middle of their path by a trivial cause.<-
->It may be a troubling lawsuit; or that lustful passion <note>original: *meretricius ardor*</note><-
->which calls young men away from their noble studies.<-

**Vocabulary:** Sloth, Remora (suckerfish), virtue, stars/the heavens, lustful passion, studies. <note>original: *Desidia, Remora, virtus, sydera, meretricius ardor, studia*</note>

<summary>This page employs the emblem of the Remora fish to illustrate how minor distractions or moral lapses can stop a person's advancement in virtue and education. It warns that even those destined for greatness can be derailed by "trivial causes" like legal disputes or carnal desires.</summary>
<keywords>Remora, Sloth, Virtue, Education, Metaphor, Temptation, Moral Failure</keywords>

### The Idle. <note>original: *Ignaui*. Referring here to those who are sluggish or busy with useless things.</note>

*An ancient fable tells that idle servants represent*
*The starry bittern, both in their pursuits and habits.*
*It claims that a servant took on the form of the bird <term>Asteria <note>In Greek mythology, Asteria was a goddess who transformed into a quail to escape the advances of Zeus. Here, the poet uses it to describe a specific type of bird associated with a "busybody" servant.</note></term>:*
*This is a fiction: let the historians decide the truth of it.*
*Just like a hawk that flutters in the air, this base man*
*Was called a "meddler" <term>ardelio <note>A Latin term for a person who busies themselves with trifles, a "busybody" or "fussbudget" who appears busy but accomplishes nothing of value.</note></term> by the poets of old.*

### Greed. <note>original: *Auaritia*</note>

*Alas, the wretched <term>Tantalus <note>A mythological figure punished in the underworld by being forced to stand in a pool of water beneath a fruit tree. Whenever he bent to drink, the water receded; whenever he reached for fruit, the branches moved away.</note></term> stands thirsty in the midst of the waves,*
*And, starving, is unable to grasp the fruit hanging close by.*
*Change the name, and the story is told of you, O greedy man:*
*You who, as if you did not possess it, do not enjoy what you have.*

## Against the Greedy. <note>original: *In auaros*</note>

Septitius is the wealthiest man among all peoples;
    No old man holds broader fields than he.
Yet, defrauding his own spirit <note>original: *geniumque suum*. In Roman belief, one's "Genius" was a personal guardian spirit that enjoyed the pleasures of life; to "cheat your genius" was a common expression for being a miser who denies himself basic comforts.</note> and ignoring fine tables,
    He devours nothing but beets and hard turnips.
To whom shall I say he is similar—this man whom plenty makes poor?
    Perhaps a donkey? It is so: he is the very image of one.
For the donkey carries precious delicacies <term>obsonia <note>A Latin term referring to rich foods, side dishes, or provisions that accompany bread.</note></term> on its back,
    Yet the poor creature feeds itself on brambles or tough weeds.

<vocab>Greed, Septitius <note>A name used in Latin poetry to represent a typical miser.</note>, donkey, needy, plenty, delicacies, the greedy</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a donkey—which carries rich food for others while eating thistles itself—to mock the miser Septitius, who lives like a beggar despite his vast landholdings.</summary>
<keywords>Avarice, greed, Septitius, donkey metaphor, irony of wealth, Roman genius, frugality vs misery</keywords>

### Against Courtiers. <note>original: *In aulicos*</note>

<image-desc>An emblem is framed by a complex, ornate woodcut border. The border's top edge features a large goat's head with prominent horns and hanging swags of fabric. The vertical columns of the frame are decorated with grotesque masks, anatomical muscular forms, and decorative tassels. The bottom edge includes a central grotesque mask and two horizontal panels, each containing a depiction of a bird (possibly a swan or stork). 

The central woodcut image shows a bearded man wearing a cap and ruffled collar, leaning against a low stone wall or balcony in an urban setting. The background consists of Renaissance-style architecture, including a building with an arched colonnade. Birds are visible in the sky. Below the main image and above the verse text is a smaller decorative woodcut of a horned, grinning mask with drapery.</image-desc>

The vain court, which nurtures its palace followers, <note>original: *Palatinos... clientes*. In the Roman "client" system, a person of lower status provided services to a patron in exchange for protection and money; here, it refers to the attendants of a royal household.</note>
    Is said to bind them with golden shackles. <note>The "golden shackles" (*auratis compedibus*) is a classic metaphor for the high price of ambition: though a courtier may be wealthy and powerful, they have lost their independence and are "chained" to the whims of their ruler.</note>

<vocab>Greed, Against courtiers, Palace followers, Court, Clients, Golden, Shackles</vocab>

It uses the visual of a finely dressed man in an urban setting to illustrate the hidden burdens of high society.</summary>

<header>AVARICE. <note>original: *A V A R I T I A*</note></header>

### Against the Sordid. <note>original: *In sordidos*. The term "sordid" here implies both physical filth and moral baseness, often associated with misers who neglect their own well-being to save money.</note>

The Ibis, well-known on the banks of the Nile, 
Who washes out her own belly with her beak (as if using a syringe), <note>Renaissance natural history, following Pliny the Elder, popularly claimed the Ibis invented the clyster (enema) by using its long beak to inject water into its own bowels. This made the bird a symbol of "sordid" or "unclean" habits.</note>
Has become a name of reproach, which Publius Naso <note>Publius Ovidius Naso, the Roman poet Ovid, wrote a famous invective poem titled *Ibis*.</note>
Used to label his enemy, as did Battiades before him. <note>Battiades is a name for the Greek poet Callimachus, who wrote the original (now lost) poem *Ibis* attacking a rival.</note>

### Against those who grow wealthy through public misfortune. <note>original: *In diuites publico malo*.</note>

Whoever tries to catch eels: if he sweeps through clear 
Rivers, or dares to approach lakes free of mud, 
He will come away empty-handed and waste his effort. 
But if his hand stirs up plenty of silt and disturbs the glassy waters, 
He will be rich. So it is for those who profit from a troubled State— 
Those who, in times of peace, go hungry because they are constrained by laws. <note>The author uses a fishing metaphor ("fishing in troubled waters") to describe war profiteers or corrupt officials who can only thrive and steal when the law is weakened by social chaos.</note>

<vocab>Greed, Ibis, Ovid, Callimachus, Eels, The State (Res Publica), Sordid, The wealthy</vocab>

<summary>This page presents two emblems of greed: the first uses the Ibis bird to symbolize the "filthy" nature of the miser, while the second uses the metaphor of catching eels in muddy water to critique those who profit from political instability.</summary>
<keywords>Avarice, Ovid, Ibis, corruption, war profiteering, social unrest, emblems, Renaissance ethics</keywords>

## GLUTTONY <note>original: *GVLA*</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration depicting an allegory of Gula (Gluttony). A man is depicted with an exceptionally long, thin neck, similar to that of a crane, and a large, protruding belly. He is dressed in a simple tunic with buttons and a soft cap. In his right hand, he holds a small bird, and in his left hand, a larger bird (likely a goose or onocrotalus). In the background, there is a landscape with hills, two small human figures, and a flock of birds flying in the sky. The entire scene is enclosed within a rectangular frame, which is further surrounded by a large, ornate decorative woodcut border featuring scrollwork, foliage, and a grotesque mask at the bottom center.</image-desc>

> A man is depicted with the neck <note>The "neck of a crane" is a classical reference to the glutton Philoxenus, who wished for a long neck so he could enjoy the sensation of swallowing for a longer duration.</note> of a crane and a swollen belly,
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;One who carries in his hands a gull, or perhaps a pelican. <note>original: *Onocrotalum*. This refers to a large water bird, often identified as a pelican or a shoveler, considered a delicacy or a symbol of excess here.</note>
> Such was the physical form of Dionysius, and such was Apicius, <note>Dionysius of Heraclea was a tyrant so famously obese he supposedly had to be pricked with needles to be woken; Apicius was a legendary Roman gourmet who spent a fortune on food.</note>
> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And all those whom a love of gluttony makes infamous.

<summary>This page illustrates the vice of Gluttony through the grotesque image of a man with a crane's neck and a bloated stomach, citing historical figures like Apicius to warn against the physical and moral deformity caused by excess.</summary>
<keywords>Gluttony, Gula, Dionysius of Heraclea, Apicius, Crane's neck, Allegory, Vice, Roman history</keywords>

<header>GLUTTONY <note>original: *GVLA*</note></header>

### The image of Ocnus. Concerning those who give to prostitutes: that wealth ought to be turned to good uses.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration framed by a simple black line, set within a much larger and more elaborate decorative border. The scene depicts the mythological figure Ocnus on the right, standing and diligently braiding a long rope from a bundle of straw. On the left, a donkey sits and consumes the rope as quickly as Ocnus can weave it. In the background is a large stone fortress or city with several towers. A small flock of birds flies in the sky above. The large outer border is architectural in style, featuring scrolls, foliage, grotesque masks at the midpoints and bottom, and stylized female figures on the vertical columns.</image-desc>

Unwearied, he does not cease to weave a rope from broom-grass, <note>original: *sparto*. Spanish broom was a common material for rope-making in the ancient world.</note>
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And to join the damp fibers with a skilled hand.
But as much as he vigorously twists through many long hours,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;A she-donkey with a lazy belly <note>The "lazy belly" (*ignaui uentris*) links this image to the theme of gluttony; the donkey represents the consumer who produces nothing but devours everything.</note> devours it immediately.
So a woman, an idle creature, snatches the riches gathered
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;By an easy-going husband, and squanders them on her finery. <note>original: *mundum*. While this usually means "world," in this context it refers to the *mundus muliebris*—a woman's collection of jewelry, clothing, and cosmetics.</note>

<note>In Greek mythology, Ocnus was a man condemned in the underworld to eternally weave a rope of straw which is eaten by a donkey as fast as he can make it. This was a common moral emblem for the futility of working hard only to have one's earnings wasted by others, particularly through the vice of gluttony or the "insatiable belly" of a mistress.</note>

<vocab>Gluttony, Ocnus, prostitute, she-donkey, rope, broom-grass, uses, of the belly, woman</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the myth of Ocnus and his rope-eating donkey to allegorically represent how wealth earned through hard labor is consumed by the "gluttony" of idle and vain companions.</summary>
<keywords>Ocnus, allegory of waste, futility, financial gluttony, gendered vice, mythological punishment, labor and consumption</keywords>

### Against parasites.

<image-desc>A central woodcut illustration is framed by an elaborate, decorative border. The border is filled with Renaissance-style ornamentation, including scrolls, floral swags, and several grotesque masks: one at the top, one on each side, and a large, foliage-crowned mask at the bottom flanked by seated human figures. The central scene depicts a man in a short tunic and hose running through a perspective-rendered city street. He holds out a large, flat basket or platter overflowing with crayfish or crabs. The street is paved with stones, and buildings with arched doorways and windows line the path.</image-desc>

Accept the river crayfish <note>original: *cancros*. While often translated as "crabs," the term *fluuiales* (river-dwelling) indicates crayfish, which were a common delicacy and a symbol used in emblems to represent backward or erratic behavior.</note> we give to you,
->These gifts are well-suited to your character.<-
They have watchful eyes, and many rows of pincers
->Armed with claws, and they possess a massive belly.<-
Just so, your own belly hangs heavy with its fat gut,
->With nimble feet and sharp spikes fitted to your pace.<-
As you wander aimlessly through the crossroads and the dinner benches,
->Hurling biting and false taunts at others.<- <note>original: *scommata*. From the Greek *skōmma*, referring to a teasing or scurrilous jest. In the social world of the Renaissance, "parasites" were uninvited guests who provided entertainment or flattery in exchange for food, often at the expense of other guests.</note>

<vocab>Gluttony, parasites, crayfish, river-dwelling, crossroads, taunts <note>original: *Gula, paraſitos, cancros, fluuiales, triuijs, scommata*</note></vocab>

<summary>This page uses the anatomy of a crayfish—its large belly, many legs, and nipping claws—as a metaphor for the social "parasite" who wanders from table to table, eating excessively and attacking others with biting wit.</summary>
<keywords>gluttony, social parasites, satire, crayfish metaphor, Renaissance humor, vice</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the theme of Gluttony from the previous page, shifting from the social parasite to the inherent conflict between those who compete for the same meager resources. While page 76 mocked the individual "crab-like" nature of the parasite, this page uses an avian metaphor to show that two such gluttons cannot coexist in one "kitchen."</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>GLUTTONY <note>original: *G V L A*</note></header>

## A small kitchen does not suffice for two gluttons. <note>original: *ganeonibus*. A *ganeo* specifically refers to a person who squanders their life and money in taverns or "eating houses."</note>

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut frame featuring architectural scrollwork and grotesque masks at the top, bottom, and sides surrounds a central emblem. The illustration inside the frame shows a rural landscape. On the left, a large, gnarled tree stands on a riverbank. Two birds are depicted in a territorial display or fight near the tree's base. In the background to the right, a small thatched-roof cottage is enclosed by a wooden fence. A flock of birds flies in the sky above the distant hills.</image-desc>

*In small things there is nothing for anyone to gain:*
*And a single grove does not nourish two robins.* <note>original: *Erythacos*. This refers to a Greek proverb recorded by Aristotle, suggesting that certain birds are too solitary and territorial to share a single bush.</note>

->ANOTHER VERSION,<-

*In meager means there is no hope of profit:*
*And two fig-peckers do not settle well in a single grove.* <note>original: *Ficedulæ*. The "fig-pecker" or garden warbler was considered a great culinary delicacy in the ancient world; here, they represent those with expensive tastes competing for limited food.</note>

<vocab>Gluttony <note>original: *Gula*</note>, glutton <note>original: *ganeo*</note>, robin <note>original: *Erythacus*</note>, fig-pecker <note>original: *Ficedula*</note>, grove <note>original: *Arbustum*</note>, kitchen <note>original: *culina*</note></vocab>

<summary>This page employs the metaphor of territorial birds—the robin and the fig-pecker—to argue that a single household or source of wealth cannot sustain more than one person devoted to gluttony and excess. It serves as a warning against the instability and inevitable conflict caused by competing vices in close quarters.</summary>

<header>GLUTTONY <note>original: *G V L A*</note></header>

## Trapped by greed. <note>original: *Captiuus ob gulam*. This refers to being made a prisoner by one's own appetite.</note>

->The ruler of the pantry, and gnawer of the master's table,<-
->A mouse, spied an oyster gaping with open lips.<-
->Applying his soft whiskers to them, he bit into those "deceptive bones,"<- <note>original: *falsa ossa*. The hard shells of the oyster are poetically called "false bones" because they appear to be something to gnaw on, but are actually a trap.</note>
->But they, once touched, suddenly snapped shut their house.<-
->They caught the thief and held him in a grim prison—<-
->He who had delivered himself into a dark and lightless tomb.<-

<note>This emblem is taken from Andrea Alciato's *Emblemata*, the most influential book of its kind in the Renaissance. The image serves as a moral warning: the mouse, looking for an easy meal, finds only its own death. It suggests that the "glutton" is often the architect of his own destruction.</note>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the specific vice of gluttony—concluding with a final avian metaphor—into a broader philosophical exploration of Nature. It examines the dual essence of humanity (reason versus instinct) through the figure of Pan and the stability of learned skill versus the fickleness of luck through Hermes and Fortune.</meta>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>N A T V R E.</header>

<image-desc>The text is framed by an elaborate woodcut border. The top section features two composite monsters with leonine bodies and avian heads, facing outward and intertwined with thick foliage and fruit. The side panels consist of scrolling floral and foliate motifs, including roses. The bottom section depicts two grotesque or gargoyle-like faces with leafy hair and beards, surrounded by heavy acanthus scrolls.</image-desc>

### Against the talkative and the gluttonous.
He roars with a grim voice, possesses an immense throat, and has a beak like a nose or a many-holed trumpet. <note>original: *multiforis tubae*. This likely refers to a wind instrument with many finger-holes, suggesting the bird makes a great deal of noise.</note> When the bird called a truo <note>original: *truo*. This likely refers to a pelican or a shoveler bird, known for its large beak and throat sac.</note> is depicted, it signifies a misshapen brawler, someone enslaved to both his belly and his appetite.

->N A T V R E.<-

### The Power of Nature.
The nations worship Pan—which is to say, the Nature of things—as a being who is half-goat and half-man, a half-human God. He is a man as far as the navel, representing the virtue proper to human beings; arising from the heart, this virtue sits within the high fortress of the head. <note>In Renaissance thought, the "fortress of the head" is the seat of reason and the soul.</note> From the waist down he is a goat, because nature propagates our kind through the ages by mating: just as she does for birds, scaled fish, beasts, and wild animals. Because this drive is common to all other living creatures, the goat serves as an indicator of lust <note>original: *luxuriae*</note> and bears the visible signs of Venus <note>The goddess of love and physical desire.</note>. Some attribute wisdom <note>original: *sophian*</note> to the heart, others to the brain. However, no moderation or reason governs the lower parts.

### Art assisting Nature.
Just as Fortune sits upon a ball, so Hermes <note>The god of commerce and communication, often representing "Art" or technical skill.</note> sits upon a cube: he presides over the arts, while she governs the various accidents of life. Art was created to counter the force of Fortune, yet when Fortune turns sour, she often requires the help of art. Therefore, studious youth, learn the good arts, which bring with them the rewards of a stable destiny. <note>The contrast between the rolling ball of Fortune and the stable cube of Hermes (Mercury) was a common visual metaphor for how education provides a foundation that luck cannot shake.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the section on gluttony with a bird metaphor before exploring the dual nature of man through the allegory of Pan and the stability of "Art" (skill) over "Fortune" (luck).</summary>
<keywords>Pan, Hermes, Nature, Fortune, gluttony, allegory, reason, instinct, Mercury, stability, education</keywords>

->N A T V R E.<-

<image-desc>The text is framed by an elaborate four-part woodcut border. The top and bottom sections feature ornate horizontal scrollwork intertwined with stylized foliage and grotesque figures. ### On youth.

Both sons of Jove <note>original: *Iouis*. Jupiter, the king of the gods.</note> are young, and both are beardless—
The one whom Latona bore <note>Apollo, the god of light, music, and healing.</note>, and the one whom Semele bore <note>Bacchus, the god of wine and festivities.</note>.
Hail to you both; flourish with eternal youth,
And by your divine power, may mine be long-lasting.
You [Bacchus] dissolve cares with wine; you [Apollo] wash away diseases with a healthy life,
So that late old age may approach with a slow step.

# A S T R O L O G Y.

### The Cup of Nestor.

Accept this mixing-bowl of Nestor with its double base,
A work of art cast from a heavy mass of silver.
The small studs are made of gold: four handles stand around it,
And upon each one, a golden dove is perched.
Only the aged Nestor had the strength to lift it.
O Muse of Maeonides <note>original: *Meonidæ*. A name for the poet Homer, often called the "Maeonian Bard."</note>, will you teach what this means?
The cup itself is the Heavens. Its color is silver;
The golden studs of the sky are the stars.
They believe the "doves" he spoke of are the Pleiades <note>The Greek name for the star cluster *Pleiades* sounds similar to the word for "doves," *peleiades*.</note>.
The twin "navels" <note>original: *vmblici*. This refers to the central points or pivots of the bowl.</note> are the Great and Lesser Bear <note>original: *magna minórque fera*. Literally "the great and lesser beast," referring to the constellations Ursa Major and Ursa Minor.</note>.
Wise Nestor understands these things through long experience;
The strong wage wars, but the clever man understands the stars.

<summary>This page presents two poems: one invoking Apollo and Bacchus to grant lasting youth and health, and another providing an astrological allegory for the legendary Cup of Nestor from Homer’s Iliad. The cup is interpreted as a map of the cosmos, where silver represents the sky and golden studs represent the stars.</summary>

<keywords>Apollo, Bacchus, youth, Astrology, Nestor's Cup, Homer, Pleiades, Ursa Major, Ursa Minor, allegory, constellations</keywords>

# A S T R O L O G Y.

->What is above us, does not concern us.<-
<note>original: *Quæ supra nos, nihil ad nos.* A Latin proverb often attributed to Socrates, suggesting that humans should focus on earthly ethics rather than speculative or divine mysteries that are beyond human comprehension.</note>

<image-desc>The page is encased in a large, ornate woodcut border featuring elaborate scrollwork, architectural motifs, swags of fruit at the top, and grotesque masks at the sides and bottom. Within this border, a central rectangular woodcut illustrates the myth of Prometheus. He is shown bound in chains to the rocky cliffs of the Caucasus Mountains. A large eagle is perched upon his body, tearing into his side with its beak to reach his liver. In the background, a desolate landscape of rolling hills and a distant structure is visible under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

Prometheus, hanging forever on a Caucasian cliff,
Has his liver torn by the talon of the sacred bird <note>The eagle, a symbol of Jupiter, who punished Prometheus for stealing divine fire.</note>.
He wishes he had never created man; and hating the potter's craft <note>In mythology, Prometheus was said to have sculpted the first humans out of clay.</note>,
He curses the torch lit by the stolen fire.
So the hearts of the learned are gnawed by various anxieties—
Those who strive to know the heavens and the shifting fates of the gods.

<summary>This page presents an emblem of Prometheus as a warning against the "gnawing" anxieties that plague scholars and astrologers who seek to uncover divine secrets. It uses the punishment of Prometheus to allegorically represent the mental burden of those obsessed with celestial predictions.</summary>

# ASTROLOGY.

### Against astrologers.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration set within a rectangular frame. It depicts the myth of Icarus falling from the sky into the sea. Icarus is shown mid-air, tumbling headfirst with feathers shedding from his failing wings. In the upper left corner, the sun is illustrated with a face and radiating rays, representing the heat that melted the wax of his wings. The sea below is choppy, and in the distance, a small sailing vessel and a rocky coastline are visible. The entire central area is framed by an elaborate woodcut border featuring architectural elements, scrolls, bunches of fruit at the top, and a grotesque winged mask at the base.</image-desc>

> O Icarus, you were carried through the heavens and the air,
> Until the melted wax threw you headlong into the sea.
> Now that same wax and the burning fire bring you back to mind,
> So that you might teach certain lessons <note>original: *dogmata*. This refers to established principles or moral truths that the reader should learn from the story.</note> by your own example.
> Let the astrologer beware of predicting anything headlong <note>original: *praeceps*. The author uses a play on words: Icarus fell "headlong" (physically), and the astrologer makes "headlong" (rash or reckless) predictions.</note>:
> For the impostor will fall while he flies above the stars.

<summary>This page uses the fall of Icarus to warn astrologers against the pride and danger of "flying too high" with their predictions. It serves as a moral lesson that those who claim to master the heavens through false expertise will eventually face a sudden and disastrous downfall.</summary>

## Those who gaze at high things fall.

<image-desc>A central woodcut illustration shows a fowler in a landscape. He stands in the foreground, aiming a longbow at a flock of birds flying in the sky above. In the background to the left, there is a rustic house with a thatched roof and a fence. On the ground near the fowler's feet, a small snake (a dipsas) is coiled, representing the hidden danger he ignores while looking upward.</image-desc>

While with birdlime <note>A sticky substance spread on branches to catch small birds.</note> he snares thrushes, while with a trap he deceives larks,
And the shot arrow <note>original: *harundo*. Literally a "reed," frequently used in Latin poetry to mean an arrow shaft.</note> pierces the high-flying crane,
The unwary fowler <term>fowler: a hunter who specializes in catching birds, often using nets, lime, or bows.</term> strikes a dipsas snake <note>original: *Dipsada*. A legendary venomous snake whose bite was said to cause an unquenchable, fatal thirst.</note> with his foot;
Avenging the injury, it hurls the venom released from its mouth.
Thus dies the man who, with drawn bow, gazes at the stars,
Heedless of the fate <note>original: *fati*. This refers to the immediate, deadly reality of the snakebite as a "doom" or "destiny."</note> that lies right before his feet.

## *L O V E.*

# Love, the most powerful emotion. <term>emotion: original "affectus," referring to a passion or state of mind that moves a person to action.</term>

> Behold how, as the unconquered charioteer of the lion's strength,
> Love, portrayed as a little boy <term>little boy: original "pusio," a term often used to describe Cupid as a small child.</term> on a gemstone <note>original: "gemma." > And how much grace there is in the boy’s face.

> Such a wild beast: will he withhold his hand from us? <note>A rhetorical question warning the reader: if Love can tame the king of beasts, human beings have no hope of resisting his power.</note>

## On a scholar captured
# By Love.

<image-desc>An ornate woodcut emblem within a rectangular frame, itself surrounded by elaborate borders. <header>L O V E.</header>

### Anteros <note>original: "Αντέρως" — The god of reciprocal or virtuous love, often depicted as the brother or rival of the common Cupid.</note>, that is, the love of virtue.
### In the manner of a dialogue <note>original: "Διαλογισικῶς"</note>.

<image-desc>The page is encased in a decorative woodcut border depicting dense foliage, intertwining tree trunks, and figures at the base. Centrally, a rectangular illustration shows a nude youth (Anteros) seated beneath a large tree in a vast landscape. He holds three floral wreaths in his right hand and wears a fourth upon his head. In the background, a winding river leads toward a distant city or castle nestled among rolling hills under a streaked sky.</image-desc>

*Tell me, where are the curved bows? Where are your arrows, Cupid,*
    *With which you are accustomed to pierce the soft hearts of youths?*
*Where is the mournful torch? Where are your wings? Why does your hand carry*
    *Three wreaths? Why does another encircle your brow?*
*Stranger, I have nothing in common with the common Venus <term>Cypris: An epithet for Venus, here used to represent vulgar or physical desire.</term>,*
    *Neither did any shape of pleasure bring us forth.*
*But I kindle the fires of learning <term>discipline: "disciplinae," referring here to the rigorous pursuit of knowledge and moral training.</term> in the pure minds of men,*
    *And I draw their souls toward the high stars.*
*And from virtue itself, I weave four wreaths,*
    *Of which the one belonging to Wisdom <note>original: "Sophiæ" — The Greek word for wisdom, often personified as the highest virtue.</note> first covers my temples.*

<summary>This page introduces Anteros, the personification of virtuous love, who rejects the traditional bow and arrows of Cupid in favor of wreaths representing the four cardinal virtues. He is presented not as a source of romantic distraction, but as the divine impulse toward learning and wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Anteros, Cupid, Virtue, Wisdom, Sophia, Allegory of Love, Education, Platonic Love</keywords>

### Anteros <note>original: "Αντερως"</note>, the love of virtue <note>original: "Amor virtutis"</note>, overcoming the other Cupid.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration set within an elaborate ornamental border featuring scrolls, floral swags, and architectural elements. The central scene depicts two winged infants (Erotes/Cupids) in a landscape. One Cupid is tied by his hands to a tree, appearing distressed. Standing next to him is another winged figure, representing Anteros or Nemesis, who holds a bow. To the left, a large fire burns on the ground. The background shows a valley with buildings and birds flying in the sky. At the bottom corners of the decorative border are the initials "P" and "V".</image-desc>

->Nemesis <note>original: "Nemesis" — Here personifying divine retribution, or perhaps Anteros himself acting as an agent of justice to punish the common Cupid for his mischief.</note> has portrayed a winged boy, an enemy to the winged Love,<-
->Subduing bow with bow, and quenching fires with fire.<-
->So that he might suffer what he inflicted on others; but this boy, who once<-
->Carried his weapons so boldly, now weeps in his wretchedness.<-
->Thrice he spits <note>original: "Ter spuit" — In antiquity, spitting three times was a ritual practice intended to avert a curse, ward off the "evil eye," or break the power of a magical spell.</note>; and in his deepest heart—a wondrous thing—<-
->Fire is consumed by fire: the Love of virtue loathes the madness <term>furies: "furias," referring to the destructive, irrational rage of romantic obsession or lust.</term> of desire.<-

<meta>Artist initials 'P' and 'V' (likely Pierre Vase) visible at the base of the border frame.</meta>

<summary>This page depicts Anteros (Virtuous Love) punishing the common Cupid, symbolizing the triumph of reason and moral virtue over irrational passion. It uses the metaphor of "fire fighting fire" to show how higher love can extinguish lower desires.</summary>
<keywords>Anteros, Cupid, Nemesis, virtue, retribution, fire metaphor, Pierre Vase, divine justice</keywords>
