### Sweet things sometimes become bitter <note>original: "Dulcia quandoque amara fieri"</note>

<image-desc>An engraving within an ornate, wide woodcut border featuring scrollwork, foliage, and masks. The central image depicts a scene from classical mythology: on the left, a woman (Venus) reaches out toward a winged infant (Cupid) who is fleeing from a swarm of bees. On the right, four straw beehives (skeps) are arranged on a low wooden bench. The background shows a landscape with rolling hills and a distant building.</image-desc>

Leaving his mother far behind, the Lydian <note>original: "Lydius" — an epithet for Cupid. Lydia was an ancient kingdom in Asia Minor, and the term here evokes the exotic and luxury-loving origins of the gods of love.</note> infant had wandered off a little way:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;But you, dreadful bees, have attacked him.
He had come to you thinking you were gentle birds,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Though even a savage viper would not be so merciless as you.
Ah! You give stings instead of the gift of sweet honey.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Oh, what pain! Alas, no pleasure <note>original: "gratia" — meaning grace, charm, or the "sweetness" of a gift.</note> is given without you <note>Referring to the "stings" or the "honey"—the poet suggests that in the experience of love, sweetness and suffering are inseparable companions.</note>.

<sig>P V <note>These initials belong to the artist Pierre Vase (also known as Pierre Eskrich), a prolific 16th-century engraver and illustrator.</note></sig>

<term>Amor</term>: Love.
<term>Cupid</term>: The god of desire, often depicted as a winged child.
<term>Venus</term>: The goddess of love and mother of Cupid.
<term>Lydius infans</term>: The Lydian infant (Cupid).
<term>apes</term>: Bees.
<term>mel</term>: Honey.
<term>Dulcia quandoque amara fieri</term>: Sweet things sometimes become bitter.

<summary>This page features a popular Renaissance allegory based on the poems of Theocritus and Anacreon, where Cupid is stung by bees while trying to steal honey. It serves as a moral warning that the pursuit of fleeting pleasure (the honey) inevitably brings sharp pain (the sting).</summary>
<keywords>Cupid, Venus, bees, honey, pleasure and pain, allegory, Lydia, Pierre Vase, emblems</keywords>

### A very similar theme from Theocritus <note>original: "Ferè ſimile ex Theocrito." Theocritus was a Greek poet of the 3rd century BCE. This poem is an adaptation of his "Idyll 19," a popular subject in Renaissance emblem books.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration set within an ornate decorative frame. The frame contains architectural elements, scrolls, floral swags, and several figures, including caryatids on the vertical pillars and masks at the top and bottom centers. The central image depicts a scene from Greek mythology and poetry: a young, naked Cupid (Amor) has been stung by bees while attempting to steal honey from a hive in a tree on the left. He reaches out in distress toward his mother, Venus, who stands on the right dressed in classical drapery. The background shows a rugged landscape with a castle perched on a distant hill.</image-desc>

While he was gathering honey from the hives, a wicked bee 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Stung the thieving Cupid <note>original: "Amorem"</note>, and left its sting in the tips 
Of his fingers: then the boy groans in distress over his swollen nail. 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;He stamps on the ground, wandering in pain, and shows his wound 
To Venus; he complains bitterly that such a tiny creature 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;As a little bee <note>original: "apicula" — a diminutive form of the Latin word for bee, "apis," used here to emphasize the creature's small size.</note> could inflict such harmful hurts. 
Venus laughed and said: "You also imitate this wild creature, 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;My son; you who are so small, yet deal so many painful wounds."

<term>Amor</term>: Love personified; another name for Cupid, the god of desire.
<term>Venus</term>: The Roman goddess of love and beauty, often depicted as Cupid's mother.
<term>Theocritus</term>: An ancient Greek poet whose pastoral works often explored the bittersweet nature of romance.
<term>Apicula</term>: A "little bee." The poet uses this term to contrast the bee’s small size with the great pain it causes, mirroring Cupid's own nature.
<term>Emblem</term>: A Renaissance art form combining a motto, an image, and a poem to deliver a moral lesson.

<summary>This page adapts a poem by Theocritus where Cupid is stung by a bee while stealing honey. His mother, Venus, uses the moment to teach him that he is just like the bee: small but capable of inflicting great pain through his arrows of desire.</summary>
<keywords>Cupid, Venus, Theocritus, bees, honey, pleasure and pain, Greek mythology, Latin poetry, Renaissance emblems</keywords>

### On a statue of Love <note>original: "In ſtatuam Amoris." This emblem explores the contradictions in how the god Cupid (Amor) is represented in art and literature.</note>

<image-desc>A central woodcut illustration depicts a youthful, athletic Cupid (Amor) standing naked in a landscape. He holds a large, oval shield in his left hand, resting it on the ground. The background features a river valley with a fortified city on a hill to the left and another settlement in the distance to the right. The entire composition is framed by an elaborate Renaissance woodcut border. The border includes a grotesque mask at the top center, decorative herms (female figures tapering into pedestals) on the left and right sides, and two reclining nude male figures at the bottom flanking a central blank cartouche.</image-desc>

*Many poets of old have sung of who Love might be,*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Relating his deeds under various names.*
*They agree on this: that he lacks clothing, that his body is small,*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*That he bears weapons and wings, and possesses no eyes <note>original: "lumina nulla tenet"—literally "holds no lights." In Latin poetry, "lights" (lumina) is a common metaphor for eyes, referencing the tradition that Love is blind.</note>.*
*This is the face, this is the appearance of the God. But if I may*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Speak against such great poets, I believe there is falsehood beneath.*
*For why should he go about naked? As if a cloak were lacking for the god*
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Who possesses all the riches of the conquered world <note>original: "domiti... orbis opes." This refers to the conceit that because Love conquers everyone—kings and commoners alike—he should logically be the wealthiest of the gods, making his nakedness a poetic paradox.</note>.*

<term>Amor<Love, specifically the Roman god Cupid, often depicted as a winged child.></term>
<term>Statua<Statue or image; in the Renaissance, "statue" often referred broadly to the visual representation or "icon" of a mythological figure.></term>
<term>Poetae<Poets; the author is engaging in a "paragone" or competition between the visual arts and the literary traditions of ancient poets.></term>

<summary>This page critiques the traditional artistic representation of Cupid as a naked child, arguing that a god who conquers the whole world should be depicted with great wealth rather than in poverty.</summary>
<keywords>Cupid, Amor, iconography, poetic tradition, wealth and poverty, mythology, Alciati, Renaissance emblems</keywords>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical critique of the traditional artistic depiction of Cupid (Love) started on page 92. While the previous page questioned Love's nudity and vulnerability, this page systematically dismantles other iconographic attributes—his youth, his weapons, his wings, his blindness, and his fiery nature—concluding that these poetic tropes are logically inconsistent.</meta>

<page-num>93</page-num>
<header>->L O V E.<-</header>

<image-desc>The text is framed by an elaborate woodcut border in the Renaissance style. At the top, a grotesque, bearded mask with foliage and scrolls is flanked by decorative swags of fruit. The sides of the border feature complex strapwork and architectural elements. At the bottom, an infant or Cupid-like figure reclines among various items, including a shield and architectural fragments. Two smaller figures appear in the bottom corners of the ornamental frame.</image-desc>

*Or how, I ask, could he, being naked, escape the snows and the North Wind?*
    *How could he endure the Alps and meadows stiff with frost?*
*If he is a boy, do you call him a boy who conquers Nestor <note>original: "Nestora"—the legendary King of Pylos from the Iliad, famous for his extreme age and wisdom. The author implies Love's power exceeds even the most seasoned experience.</note>?*
    *Or are you familiar with the learned songs of the old man of Ascra <note>original: "Ascraei... senis"—referring to the Greek poet Hesiod, who was born in Ascra. In his "Theogony," Hesiod describes Love (Eros) as one of the oldest primordial forces of the universe.</note>?*
*A fickle boy? Yet this one is persistent; once he has entered*
    *The heart, he can never leave of his own accord.*
*But he carries a quiver and weapons; why this useless weight?*
    *Can an infant bend the stiff horns of a bow?*
*And why does he have wings, which he does not know how to carry into the sky?*
    *He is ignorant of how to aim his arrows at the hearts <note>original: "iecur"—literally "liver." In ancient and medieval physiology, the liver was considered the seat of passion and desire, much like we use the heart today.</note> of birds.*
*He crawls upon the ground and always wounds the mortal hearts*
    *Of men; and like a statue of stone, he never moves his wings from there.*
*If he is blind and wears a band, of what use is a blindfold to one*
    *Already blind? Does he see any less because of it?*
*Who would believe a blind man is an archer?*
    *This god strikes a certain mark, whereas a blind man's arrows fly in vain.*
*They say he is made of fire and stirs up flames within the breast;*
    *But tell me, why is he still alive? For flame devours all things.*
*Furthermore, why is he not extinguished by the swelling waves*
    *Whenever he enters the soft hearts of the Naiads <note>original: "Naiadum"—water nymphs who inhabit springs and rivers.</note>?*
*But listen, so that you are not caught by such great errors;*
    *Our songs will reveal what true Love is.*
*It is pleasant labor born of playful leisure. His emblem*
    *Is a pomegranate <note>original: "punica glans"—literally "Punic nut," usually referring to a pomegranate. In this context, it symbolizes the internal "seeds" of effort and the hard work required to maintain affection.</note> upon a black shield.*

<summary>This page concludes a skeptical critique of Cupid’s traditional appearance, arguing that the classic image of a winged, blind, fiery child is a logical impossibility. The author proposes a new emblem for Love: a pomegranate on a shield, representing "pleasant labor."</summary>
<keywords>Cupid, Amor, iconography, Nestor, Hesiod, Naiads, pomegranate, emblem, poetic critique</keywords>

### On forgetting one’s homeland.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration is enclosed within a very elaborate decorative border. The border features architectural scrollwork, strapwork, and a grotesque mask at the top center. At the bottom, a reclining figure rests amidst more scrolls. The central illustration depicts the Lotophagi: a central figure has arms that have transformed into the branches of a lotus tree. Several men surround the tree; some are picking fruit while others are seated or reclining on the ground, eating the fruit in a state of lethargy. A mountainous landscape with a small town is visible in the background on the left.</image-desc>

->Having long since left your homeland, and forgotten your kin,<-
->Whom either blood or love had joined to you,<-
->You reside in Rome; nor does any desire to return home enter your mind.<-
->Only the glory of the Eternal City <note>original: "Aeternæ... urbis"—This is a classic honorific for Rome. The author suggests that the city's prestige acts as a sort of intoxicating drug.</note> captivates you.<-
->Just so, the scouts sent ahead by the Ithacan <note>original: "Ithacum"—Referring to Odysseus (Ulysses), the legendary King of Ithaca and hero of Homer's *Odyssey*.</note> were swayed by the sweetness of the Lotus,<-
->And had forsaken their country, and forsaken their leader <note>original: "ducem"—The poem references the Book 9 of the *Odyssey*, where those who ate the lotus fruit lost all memory of their journey and their desire to return home.</note>.<-

<summary>This emblem uses the Homeric myth of the Lotus-eaters to criticize those who move to Rome and become so enamored with its prestige that they abandon their families and duties at home.</summary>
<keywords>Rome, homeland, nostalgia, Lotus-eaters, Odysseus, Alciato, Eternal City, loyalty</keywords>

### Sirens.

> Birds without wings, and girls without legs,
> Fish without a snout, who yet sing with their mouths—
> Who would believe such creatures exist? Nature refused to join these forms,
> But they teach us that Sirens could be created.
> A woman is a lure who ends in a black fish-tail,
> Because lust <note>original: "libido"—Often used to mean unrestrained desire or passion.</note> carries many monsters in its wake.
> Men are drawn by the appearance, the words, and the [feigned] purity of soul
> Of Parthenope, Ligeia, and Leucosia. <note>original: "Parthenope, Ligia, Leucosiáque"—The traditional names of the three Sirens in Greek mythology, who lured sailors to their doom with their music.</note>
> The Muses pluck their feathers: and Ulysses mocks them. <note>original: "Vlysses"—The Greek hero Odysseus, who famously survived the Sirens' song by tying himself to the mast of his ship.</note>
> To be sure, the learned man has nothing to do with a harlot. <note>original: "meretrix"—The poem uses the Sirens as an allegory for the dangers of prostitution and superficial attraction, contrasting them with the wisdom of the Muses and the "learned" man.</note>

### An old man in love with a girl.

> While Sophocles (though of advanced age) draws the girl
> Archippe from her trade <note>original: "A' quæstu"—Literally "from her gain," referring to her work as a prostitute.</note> to his own desires,
> And entices her with a price, the reckless youth took it poorly
> Out of jealousy, and mocks them both with such a poem:
> "Like a screech-owl on tombs, or like a horned owl over corpses,
> Just so does our girl sit by Sophocles' side." <note>This biting epigram compares the aging playwright to a corpse and the young woman to a bird of ill omen (the owl) scavenging near the dead, highlighting the perceived indecency of their relationship.</note>

<image-desc>Below the text is a decorative woodcut tailpiece consisting of scrolling strapwork and stylized floral elements. Further down, at the base within the main border frame, is a large, elaborate woodcut of a winged grotesque mask with scaly features and floral extensions.</image-desc>

<summary>This page presents two moralizing emblems on the theme of desire: the first uses Sirens to warn against the monstrous nature of lust, while the second uses a historical anecdote about the elderly Sophocles to mock the undignified pursuit of young women by old men.</summary>
<keywords>Sirens, Sophocles, Archippe, Ulysses, lust, morality, aging, Sirenes, Muses, birds of ill omen</keywords>

It categorizes various hues according to the emotions, social roles, and states of mind they represent, which was a common theme in Renaissance emblem books and courtly literature.</meta>

### On Colors.

Dark gray <note>original: "pullus"—a dark, dusky color, often the natural color of black sheep's wool, used specifically for mourning.</note> is a sign of sorrow. We all use 
This attire when we offer funeral rites at the tombs. 
But a white <note>original: "candida"—shining or pure white.</note> robe belongs to a sincere soul and a pure mind. 
For this reason, fine linen <note>original: "syndon"—a Greek term for fine fabric or a shroud.</note> is pleasing to holy men. 
Green <note>original: "uiridis"</note> teaches us to hope. Hope is said to be 
"In the green," whenever it fails and falls back fruitlessly. <note>The author references the Latin proverb *spes in uiridi*, suggesting that green represents a hope that is fresh but perhaps unripened or easily disappointed.</note>
Yellow <note>original: "flauus"</note> is the color for the greedy, and is also fit for lovers, 
For harlots, and for those whose hope has been made certain. 
But let red <note>original: "ruber"</note> adorn the cloaks of armed knights, 
And let it show when modesty <note>original: "pudor"</note> makes boys blush. 
Deep blue <note>original: "caeruleus"</note> is for sailors, and for those prophets 
Who, awestruck by deep devotion, seek heavenly things <note>original: "cœlestia"</note>. 
Natural and cheap fleeces are for pale-yellow <note>original: "giluis"—a dull, honey-yellow or dun color.</note> cloaks, 
The sort of coverings that those who wear wooden clogs <note>original: "lignipedes"—literally "wooden-footed," referring to the poor or certain monastic orders who wore clogs.</note> are accustomed to have. 
He who is tormented by great cares or the jealousy of love 
Is rightly believed to be dressed in a tawny <note>original: "fulua"—a reddish-yellow or brownish-orange, like a lion's mane.</note> garment. 
Whoever is content with his lot should wear violet <note>original: "ianthina"—from the Greek word for the color of violets.</note>, 
As should those who bear the burdens of fortune with a steady mind. 
Just as nature is varied in the colors it produces, 
So different things please different people. But each is pleased by his own choice.

<term>Colors (colores)</term>: The author assigns specific moral or social meanings to various hues: <term>dark gray (pullus)</term> for grief, <term>white (candidus)</term> for purity, <term>green (uiridis)</term> for hope, <term>yellow (flauus)</term> for desire or greed, <term>red (ruber)</term> for courage or shame, <term>blue (caeruleus)</term> for the sea or heavens, <term>pale-yellow (giluus)</term> for poverty, <term>tawny (fuluus)</term> for anxiety, and <term>violet (ianthina)</term> for contentment.

<summary>This page provides a poetic guide to color symbolism in the Renaissance, linking specific hues to emotional states, social classes, and spiritual qualities. It reflects the era's interest in "emblems" where visual elements served as a shorthand for complex moral ideas.</summary>
<keywords>color symbolism, mourning, purity, hope, yellow, red, blue, violet, Renaissance fashion, social status</keywords>

# F O R T V N E.

->Fortune is the companion to Virtue.<- 
<note>original: "Virtuti, Fortuna comes." This was a popular Renaissance motto suggesting that success is not merely a matter of luck, but a natural result of one's own merit and moral excellence.</note>

<image-desc>The page is encased in an elaborate woodcut architectural border. At the top center, a lion's head mask is flanked by garlands and scrolls. The side borders are formed by two ornate caryatid columns; the top portions are female figures with arms folded across their chests, while the bottom halves transition into spiraling, floral pedestals. The bottom of the frame consists of a decorative base with large scroll ornaments and a small central cartouche containing the letters "PV".

Within the main frame is a smaller rectangular woodcut illustration. It depicts a winged caduceus (the staff of Mercury) with two entwined snakes. The staff is positioned vertically between two large cornucopias (horns of plenty) that are brimming with fruits and foliage. The entire arrangement is set against a landscape background featuring rocky ground and distant hills.</image-desc>

->The caduceus, with its entwined snakes and twin wings,<-
<note>The <term>caduceus</term> is the traditional staff carried by Mercury (Hermes), the messenger of the gods. In this context, it represents eloquence, reason, and the peaceful resolution of conflict.</note>
->Stands upright between the horns of Amalthea.<-
<note>original: "Amalthææ cornua." In Greek mythology, Amalthea was the goat who nursed the infant Jupiter. One of her horns was broken off and became the "Cornucopia" or "Horn of Plenty," which provides an endless supply of food and flowers.</note>
->Thus it signifies men powerful in mind and skilled in speech,<-
->So that a great abundance of things may bless them.<-
<note>The author is explaining the visual metaphor: when wisdom and eloquence (the staff) are combined with virtue, they lead to material and spiritual prosperity (the cornucopias).</note>

## Fortune, overcoming Virtue.
<note>original: "Fortuna, Virtutem superans." This emblem reflects a moment of deep disillusionment, contrasting with the previous page’s more optimistic view of success.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut emblem illustration contained within an elaborate architectural frame. The central scene depicts a bearded man in classical Roman armor, likely Marcus Junius Brutus, in a dramatic pose of despair amidst a landscape of trees and rolling hills. He gestures with his arms while his sword stands upright, hilt-down, on the ground before him. The framing border is highly decorative: it features two caryatid-like figures on the sides whose lower halves transition into ornate, twisted columns. The top of the frame is decorated with a lion's head mask and draped fabric swags flanked by globes. The bottom pedestal includes a small central cartouche bearing the initials "PV".</image-desc>

->After he was defeated by Caesar’s soldiers, and saw<-
<note>The poem refers to the Roman Civil Wars. Although the text mentions <term>Pharsalia</term> (the site of Caesar's victory over Pompey), Brutus famously met his end after the later Battle of Philippi.</note>
->Pharsalia overflowing with the blood of fellow citizens:<-
->Just as he was about to draw the blade into his dying breast,<-
->Brutus brought forth these cries from his bold mouth:<-
->“O wretched Virtue, a provider of mere words!<-
<note>original: "Infelix Virtus & solis prouida uerbis." This famous lament, attributed to Brutus in his final moments, suggests that living a moral and virtuous life is meaningless if luck or "Fortune" does not favor you.</note>
->Why do you follow Fortune as your mistress in worldly affairs?”<-

<vocab>Fortune, Virtue, Brutus, Pharsalia, Caesar's, Latin emblem, Roman armor</vocab>

<summary>This page depicts the suicide of the Roman senator Brutus, using his tragic end to illustrate the Renaissance fear that blind luck (Fortune) can overpower even the most virtuous of men. It serves as a grim warning that moral excellence does not always guarantee success in the face of political upheaval.</summary>

<keywords>Fortune, Virtue, Marcus Junius Brutus, Roman Civil War, Pharsalia, tragedy, stoicism, ethics vs success</keywords>

## Poverty hinders the greatest talents from advancing.
<note>original: "Paupertatem summis ingenijs obesse ne prouehantur." This title addresses a common grievance of Renaissance scholars: that intellectual or artistic brilliance is often rendered useless without financial patronage.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut emblem set within a rectangular frame. It depicts a man seen from behind, dressed in a simple tunic. His right arm is raised toward the sky, where a small winged figure or hand emerges from the clouds; a wing is attached to his right wrist, symbolizing aspiration or genius. His left arm hangs down, and his left hand holds a large, heavy stone that rests on the ground, symbolizing the weight of poverty. The background shows a rocky landscape with sparse trees.</image-desc>

->The right hand holds a stone, the other hand bears wings;<-
<note>The image of the "wings" and the "stone" provides a stark visual metaphor for the internal conflict between ambition and necessity.</note>
->As much as the feathers lift me, so the heavy weight sinks me down.<-
->By my talent I could fly through the high citadels,<-
<note><term>talent</term> (original: *ingenio*): This refers to innate brilliance, inspiration, or "genius." In this period, it was believed that such gifts came from the heavens but required earthly means to flourish.</note>
->If only envious poverty did not weigh me down.<-

---

<image-desc>A horizontal woodcut landscape at the bottom of the page, showing a detailed vista of a city with various buildings, towers, and a multi-arched bridge crossing a river. The foreground and sides are filled with rocky terrain and vegetation.</image-desc>

<sig>G 2</sig>

**Vocabulary:** Poverty, Talent, Fortune, stone, wings, burden.

<summary>This emblem illustrates the "poverty of genius," a classic theme depicting how material hardship prevents individuals of great intellectual or artistic ability from achieving their potential. The visual metaphor of a man pulled between wings and a heavy stone captures the frustration of being "grounded" by economic necessity.</summary>
<keywords>poverty, talent, genius, Fortune, social mobility, ambition, wings and stone, Alciato</keywords>

This page (100) shifts from the general concept of Fortune to a specific personification: "Opportunity" (Occasio), exploring how timing and readiness are essential to success.</meta>

# On Opportunity.
## In the form of a dialogue.
<note>original: "Διαλογισικῶς" (Dialogistikōs). This Greek term indicates a poem written as a conversation, usually between a traveler and a statue, designed to explain the statue’s symbolic attributes.</note>

<image-desc>An emblem woodcut enclosed in a dense ornamental border of foliage and twisted branches. The central image depicts the personification of Occasio (Opportunity) as a nude female figure balancing on a wheel in the sea. She has long hair that drapes forward over her face, while the back of her head is bald. Her heels have wings. She holds a razor in her right hand and a large, flowing sail or cloth in her left. The background features a seascape with rocks and birds. Below the central frame is a smaller horizontal woodcut panel showing a landscape with a river, a bridge, and buildings, with a rabbit on the left and a stag on the right nestled in the decorative border.</image-desc>

"This is the work of Lysippus, whose homeland was Sicyon. Who are you?"
<note>Lysippus was a celebrated Greek sculptor of the 4th century BCE. Sicyon was an ancient Greek city famous as a center for painting and bronze sculpture.</note>
"I am the moment of seized time that subdues all things."
"Why do you stand on wings?" "I am constantly spinning." "Why do you keep winged sandals on your feet?"
<term>winged sandals</term> (original: *talaria*): These are the same magical footwear associated with the messenger god Mercury, symbolizing swift movement and the fleeting nature of time.
"A light breeze carries me everywhere."
"Tell me, why is there a thin razor in your right hand?" "This sign teaches me to be keener than any blade."
"Why is there hair on your forehead?" "So that I may be grabbed as I approach." "But wait, tell me, why is the back of your head bald?"
"If someone lets me fly away on my winged feet, I cannot afterward be caught by the hair."
<note>This is the origin of the phrase "to take time by the forelock." Once Opportunity passes you by, there is nothing left to grab onto from behind.</note>
"The craftsman created me with such skill for your sake, stranger, and so that I may warn everyone, this open gallery holds me."
<note>original: *pergula*. This refers to an open-fronted room or porch, likely where the statue of Opportunity was famously displayed for public view and contemplation.</note>

<summary>This page describes the personification of Opportunity (Occasio) through a dialogue, explaining her symbolic attributes like her forelock and bald head to emphasize that one must seize the moment before it passes. It adapts a famous Greek epigram about a lost statue by the sculptor Lysippus.</summary>
<keywords>Opportunity, Occasio, Lysippus, timing, allegory, talaria, razor, Sicyon, Greek sculpture</keywords>

### On sudden terror.
<note>The Latin title "In subitum terrorem" refers to the concept of "Panic" fear. In Greek and Roman mythology, sudden, groundless fear—especially in forests or wilderness—was attributed to the god Pan (Roman Faunus).</note>

->*Seeing the crowds fleeing in a scattered line,*<-
->*“Who is blowing into my horns now?” asks Faunus.*<-
<note>original: "cornua." This is a clever play on words. In Latin, *cornua* refers both to the physical horns on Faunus's head and the musical horns or trumpets used to sound an alarm. Faunus is surprised to see people fleeing in "panic" even when he himself has not sounded his horn to frighten them.</note>

<vocab>Fortune, Faunus, terror, sudden terror, horns, emblem</vocab>

<summary>This page illustrates the concept of sudden terror (the origin of the word "panic") through the figure of Faunus, who observes people fleeing in fear even without his intervention. It highlights the unpredictable and often irrational nature of human fear within the broader theme of Fortune's whims.</summary>
<keywords>Panic, Faunus, sudden fear, mythology, Roman gods, psychological terror, Fortune</keywords>

### Praising things that bring no glory.
<note>original: "In illaudata laudantes." This title refers to the act of celebrating a victory or a tool of war that, while successful, reflects poorly on the character or skill of the victor.</note>

The massive throngs of Galatians <note>The Galatians were Celtic tribes who invaded Asia Minor in the 3rd century BCE.</note>, by a half-armed soldier,
The trembling Antiochus <note>Antiochus I Soter (reigned 281–261 BCE), a Hellenistic king of the Seleucid Empire.</note> had routed beyond all hope,
When the savage force of Lucanian oxen <term>Lucanian oxen: a Roman nickname for elephants, who first encountered them in the region of Lucania during the war against Pyrrhus</term>, and their dreadful trunks,
Then for the first time had seized the enemy’s horses.
Therefore, when setting up trophies, he decorated them with the image of an Elephant,
And moreover, he said to his companions: "We would have perished,
Had that most foul beast, the elephant <term>barrus: an ancient, rare term for an elephant</term>, not saved us."
Just as it is a joy to have conquered: so is it a shame to have conquered in such a way.

<vocab>Antiochus, Galatians, Elephant, Lucanian oxen, elephant (barrus), trophies</vocab>

<summary>This page recounts the "Elephant Victory" of King Antiochus I over the Galatians, where the king felt more shame than pride because his victory relied on the terrifying novelty of elephants rather than the bravery of his men.</summary>
<keywords>Antiochus, Galatians, elephants, military ethics, victory, shame, Fortune, Battle of the Elephants</keywords>

It contrasts the slow-growing, enduring pine tree with the fast-growing, but short-lived, gourd vine.</meta>

->On fleeting prosperity.<-
<note>original: "In momentaneam felicitatem." This emblem warns that success achieved too quickly is often precarious and temporary.</note>

A gourd <term>Cucurbita: A plant of the squash or pumpkin family, known in classical literature for its rapid, climbing growth and its watery, fragile nature.</term> is said to have grown beside a lofty pine,
And to have flourished with a great abundance of leaves.
When it had entwined the branches and even overtopped the very peak,
It believed itself superior to all other trees.
To which the pine replied: "This glory of yours is far too brief;
For winter will soon arrive to wretchedly destroy you."
<note>original: "malè perdet." This implies a ruinous or miserable end, contrasting the gourd's current arrogance with its inevitable decay when the season turns.</note>

<sig>G 4</sig>

<summary>This emblem uses a dialogue between a fast-growing gourd vine and a steady pine tree to illustrate the dangers of sudden, arrogant success. It serves as a warning that those who rise quickly without a solid foundation are easily destroyed by a change in fortune.</summary>
<keywords>Fortune, pride, transience, nature fables, Alciato, Pine tree, Gourd, Winter, humility</keywords>

These move from the opportunistic nature of loss to the warnings of bad omens, the devastation of natural disasters, and the transience of stolen wealth.</meta>

<header>F O R T U N E</header>

## One man’s loss is another’s gain.
<note>original: "Ex damno alterius, alterius vtilitas." This common proverb suggests that Fortune often redistributes resources through conflict.</note>

*While a fierce lioness and a boar with fearsome tusks,*
*Rushed at each other with savage weapons to inflict mutual wounds,*
*A vulture flew near to watch the sight, and to catch its lunch.*
*The glory of the victor shall become the vulture's future prey.*
<note>This emblem warns that while two powerful rivals exhaust themselves in combat, a lowlier opportunist—the vulture—waits to benefit from the downfall of both.</note>

## One must begin with good omens.

*A matter begun with bad omens knows not how to end well.*
*Those things done under a happy sign bring joy.*
*Whatever you are doing, if a weasel crosses your path, stop:*
*This wicked beast carries the signs of a bad fate.*
<term>mustella: A weasel. In ancient Greek and Roman folklore, seeing a weasel was often considered a "monstrum" or an unlucky omen that signaled a need to halt one's current business.</term>

## Nothing remains.

*Indeed, this was all that was lacking: that after so many evils,*
*Locusts should finally snatch away whatever was in our fields.*
*We saw innumerable swarms arrive, led by the east wind,*
*Such as the camps of Attila or Xerxes never were.*
*These have consumed the hay, the millet, and every grain;*
*Hope is now in narrow straits, unless our prayers alone remain.*
<note>Attila the Hun and the Persian King Xerxes were historical figures synonymous with unstoppable, massive invading armies. Here, the poet argues that the "army" of locusts is even more destructive to the common person's survival.</note>

## Ill-gotten gains slip away badly.
<note>original: "Malè parta, malè dilabuntur." A variation of the sentiment "easy come, easy go," specifically regarding wealth obtained through dishonest means.</note>

*A greedy kite, whom the nausea of too much food had twisted,*
*Cried, "Woe is me, mother! My very guts are flowing from my mouth!"*
*But she replied: "Why do you weep? Why believe these guts are yours,*
*When you, who live on plunder, vomit only what belongs to others?"*
<note>The "kite" (miluus) is a bird of prey often used in emblems to represent thievery. The mother’s sharp reply points out that the bird hasn't actually lost its own substance, but merely failed to keep what it stole.</note>

<summary>This page presents four moral emblems regarding Fortune, illustrating how fate rewards opportunists, warns through omens, destroys through plague, and punishes the greedy.</summary>
<keywords>Fortune, omens, weasel, locusts, Attila, Xerxes, greed, opportunism, Aesopic fables, Latin poetry</keywords>

## Misfortune is always at hand.
<note>original: "Semper præsto esse infortunia." This heading serves as a grim reminder of the unpredictability of life's end.</note>

<image-desc>An elaborate woodcut border surrounds the page content, featuring heavy garlands of fruit and flowers, ribbons, and grotesque masks. The side borders include standing male figures. At the bottom, two owls flank a central decorative basin above another mask. Within the frame is a woodcut illustration depicting three women in Renaissance attire seated at a round table. They appear to be playing a game of chance with dice. One woman sits centrally, facing the viewer, while the other two sit on the left and right in profile. The table's pedestal is decorated with a lion's head.</image-desc>

Once, three girls of equal age were playing
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;With lots: <note>The "lots" here refer to a game of chance, used to simulate a dark prophecy.</note> [to see] who would be the first to go to the Stygian waters. <note>original: "ad Stygias... aquas." The River Styx in Greek mythology, across which souls are ferried to the Underworld.</note>
But she for whom the dice had fallen poorly upon the throw,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;That girl, blind <note>original: "cæca." Here meaning blind to her own destiny or reckless, rather than sightless.</note> to her own fate, laughed at her luck.
When suddenly, struck on the head by a falling roof, she died,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And paid the debt of fate for her daring joke.
In times of adversity, bad luck does not miss its mark; but in
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Times of prosperity, there is no place for prayers, nor for the hand of man to help. <note>This concludes the moral: misfortune is unerring, and human intervention—whether through prayer or physical effort—cannot stop fate once it is set in motion.</note>

<vocab><term>Fortune</term> (Fortuna), <term>misfortunes</term> (infortunia), <term>girls</term> (puellæ), <term>lot or fate</term> (sors), <term>dice</term> (alea), <term>the fates</term> (fata)</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the story of three girls playing a game about death to illustrate that misfortune is always present and fate cannot be mocked. It highlights the irony of a girl "winning" a game about who dies first, only to be killed instantly by a structural collapse.</summary>
<keywords>Fortune, Styx, death, irony, dice, gaming, fate, misfortune</keywords>

<page-num>106</page-num>
<header>F O R T U N E.</header>

<image-desc>The page is framed by an elaborate woodcut border depicting clusters of fruit, leaves, and human figures. In the bottom section of the border, two owls with outstretched wings flank a central decorative basin. A grotesque mask is visible at the very bottom center. ->Remedies are found on the uphill path; evils lie on the downward slope.<-
<note>original: "Remedia in arduo, mala in prono esse." This proverb suggests that while it takes great effort to fix a problem, falling into ruin is easy and effortless.</note>

After Jupiter cast down **Ate** <note>In Greek mythology, Ate is the goddess of mischief, delusion, and ruin who leads men toward reckless actions.</note> from her heavenly home:
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Alas, how much wretched harm does she inflict upon men?
She flies forth, swift of foot and with nimble wings,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And allows nothing to remain untouched by disaster.
Therefore, the **Litae** <note>term: Litae (the "Prayers"). In Homer's Iliad, these are the daughters of Jupiter who follow after Ate to heal the wounds she causes. They represent the slow process of prayer, repentance, and restitution.</note>, the offspring of Jupiter, follow her as she goes,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;To mend whatever evil she has committed.
But because they are slow-footed, squint-eyed, and weary with age,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;They restore nothing except after a long passing of time.

<vocab>Fortune, Ate, Jupiter, Litae (Prayers), remedies, evils, harm</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the mythological figures of Ate (Ruin) and the Litae (Prayers/Repentance) to explain the human experience of misfortune: ruin arrives with lightning speed, while the process of healing and restoration is slow and difficult.</summary>
<keywords>Ate, Litae, Jupiter, misfortune, recovery, mythology, moral philosophy</keywords>

## HONOR.

->From difficult struggles, an everlasting name.<-
<note>original: "Ex arduis perpetuum nomen." This motto suggests that true, lasting glory is only achieved through great effort and hardship.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut emblem set within a highly decorative rectangular border. The border is filled with grotesque figures, including swine at the top, satyr-like figures on the sides, and winged beasts at the bottom. The central illustration shows a rocky landscape with a large plane tree. On the ground, a dragon-like serpent crawls toward several small birds it is devouring. In the sky above, an adult bird (a sparrow) flies away. This scene illustrates the omen at Aulis described in the Iliad, where a serpent devours a sparrow and her eight young.</image-desc>

> A sparrow had entrusted her dear <term>brood</term> <note>original: "pignora." Literally "pledges," but used poetically here to mean children or offspring—the "pledges" of a parent's love.</note> to the branches of a plane tree,
> And it would have been well, had they not been seen by a cruel serpent.
> This beast swallowed all the chicks, and the wretched mother too,
> Before turning to stone—a death it well deserved.
> Unless **Calchas** <note>In Greek mythology, Calchas was the powerful seer or prophet for the Greeks during the Trojan War. He interpreted this omen of the serpent and the nine birds to mean that the Greeks would labor for nine years at Troy before finally winning in the tenth.</note> lies, these are the monuments
> Of a long labor, the fame of which shall endure forever.

**Vocabulary**
**Honor** (Honor), **Serpent** (Draco), **Calchas** (The prophet Calchas), **Sparrow** (Passer), **Monuments** (Monumenta), **Of the plane tree** (Platani), **Pledges/Offspring** (Pignora)

<summary>This page illustrates the concept of Honor through the story of Calchas’s omen at Aulis, where a serpent eating nine birds predicted the long years of the Trojan War. It teaches that lasting fame is a "monument" built from enduring great difficulty.</summary>

## That immortality is acquired through the study of literature.
<note>original: "Ex literarum studijs immortalitatem acquiri." In the Renaissance, "letters" (literarum) referred to all forms of scholarship and creative writing that preserve human memory.</note>

<image-desc>
A central woodcut illustration depicts Triton, a mythological merman with a human torso and a fish tail, blowing into a large conch shell while emerging from a turbulent sea. He is entirely enclosed within an Ouroboros—a serpent biting its own tail—representing eternity. The sky above the horizon is filled with stylized clouds. The image is framed by an ornate decorative border containing reclining figures at the top and bottom, herm-like figures at the sides, and various architectural scrolls and floral garlands.
</image-desc>

->The trumpeter of **Neptune**, whose lower half is a sea-creature,<-
<note>original: "Neptuni tubicen." Neptune was the Roman god of the sea; his son, Triton, used a conch shell to command the waves.</note>
->His face reveals him to be a god of the ocean:<-
->**Triton** is enclosed within the circle of a serpent,<-
->Which holds its tail fast in its mouth.<-
<note>This serpent is the **Ouroboros**, an ancient symbol of the infinite cycle of time and eternity. By placing Triton—the messenger of fame—inside it, the author suggests that the message will never end.</note>
->**Fama** <note>The personification of Fame or Renown.</note> follows men of noble spirit and their illustrious deeds:<-
->She commands that they be read throughout the entire world.<-
<note>The poet argues that even a god's herald (Triton) needs the "circle" of eternal literature to ensure that a hero's deeds are "read" (legi) forever.</note>

<vocab>Triton (sea messenger), Ouroboros (eternal serpent), Neptune (sea god), Fame, honor, immortality, the study of literature</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the mythological figure of Triton encircled by an Ouroboros to symbolize how the "study of letters" grants eternal life to human achievements. It argues that writing is the vessel through which noble deeds are published and preserved across the globe forever.</summary>
<keywords>immortality, Triton, Ouroboros, Fame, literature, Neptune, eternity, noble deeds</keywords>

Here, the "tomb" of the Duke of Milan is described not as a simple stone monument, but as the entire land of Italy itself, symbolizing his immense power and the lasting impact of his reign.</meta>

## The Tomb of Gian Galeazzo Visconti,
## first Duke of Milan.
<note>Gian Galeazzo Visconti (1351–1402) was the first Duke of Milan and a massive figure in Italian history. He transformed Milan into a major European power and was known for commissioning the Milan Cathedral.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut map depicting central Italy, featuring coastal and inland cities labeled as CESENADEGO, RIMANO, ANCONA, RAVENA, PORTODERAVENA, FIRENZA, PISA, SIENA, BIBIENA, MOTESCIO, PERVRA, TVRNI, BOLSENA, CITAVECHIA, CAPOLINARE, MARTAFIVME, and the island of GIGLIO. In the upper right of the map, a large crowned serpent (the Visconti biscione) is shown swallowing a human figure. The entire scene is enclosed in a grand woodcut border featuring two reclining figures at the top, decorative twisted columns on the left and right, and a large ornate basin flanked by winged griffins at the bottom.</image-desc>

->For a tomb, set out Italy; set out weapons and leaders,<-
->And the sea, which bellows all the way to the twin bays.<-
<note>The "twin bays" (original: *geminos sinus*) refer to the Adriatic and Tyrrhenian seas that frame the Italian peninsula.</note>
->Add to these the barbarians trying to break in without success,<-
->And the hands of mercenaries hired for brutal wars.<-
<note>During this era, Italy was frequently the site of conflict involving foreign "barbarian" powers and paid mercenary soldiers known as *condottieri*.</note>
->But let the Serpent-bearer, standing at the very peak, ask:<-
<note>The "Serpent-bearer" (original: *Anguiger*) refers to the **Biscione**, the famous heraldic emblem of the Visconti family—a giant serpent swallowing a human. It remains a symbol of Milan today.</note>
->"Who has placed me, so great, atop such small things?"<-
<note>This final line is a boast: the Duke's legacy is so vast that even the entire land of Italy seems like a "small thing" to serve as his monument.</note>

<summary>This page commemorates Gian Galeazzo Visconti, the first Duke of Milan, by metaphorically claiming the entire Italian peninsula as his tomb. It highlights the Visconti family's power through their iconic serpent emblem, the Biscione, which is depicted dominating a map of central Italy.</summary>

<keywords>Gian Galeazzo Visconti, Milan, Italy, Biscione, heraldry, monument, Renaissance politics, Duchy of Milan, mercenaries</keywords>

<header>H O N O R .</header>

<image-desc>An elaborate Renaissance woodcut frame surrounds the text and central illustration. At the top, two winged male figures recline toward the center. The sides are formed by spiraled columns topped by terminal busts; the bust on the left has a cartouche marked with the letter "P", while the one on the right is marked with "V". A group of followers or citizens is visible in the background to the left.</image-desc>

### The Best Citizen.
<note>The "Best Citizen" (original: *Optimus ciuis*) is a classical ideal of a leader who puts the health of the state above his own power or revenge.</note>

->While Thrasybulus liberates his homeland with just weapons,<-
<note>Thrasybulus was an Athenian general who led the resistance against the "Thirty Tyrants," a pro-Spartan oligarchy that ruled Athens after the Peloponnesian War.</note>
->And while he commands everyone to set aside their grudges,<-
<note>original: *simultates*. After his victory, Thrasybulus famously declared an "amnesty," meaning he forbade the prosecution of citizens for their actions during the reign of the tyrants, effectively ending a cycle of civil violence.</note>
->The whole citizenry, in one accord, as a great gift for him,<-
->Granted that he should wear a wreath of Pallas’s leaves.<-
<note>original: *Palladiæ sertum frondis*. These are olive leaves, sacred to Pallas Athena, the patron goddess of Athens. Unlike a crown of gold, this simple wreath represented peace, wisdom, and the gratitude of a free people.</note>
->Wreathe your hair, Thrasybulus; may you alone bear this honor,<-
->For in this great city, no one is a rival to you.<-

<term>Thrasybulus</term> <note>The Athenian leader (c. 440–388 BC) who restored democracy.</note>
<term>The Best Citizen</term> <note>original: *Optimus ciuis*; a title for a leader who values civic peace.</note>
<term>Honor</term> <note>In this context, honor is a reward granted by the people, not seized by force.</note>
<term>Palladian wreath</term> <note>original: *Palladiæ sertum*; a crown of olive branches signifying Athena’s favor and civic peace.</note>
<term>Grudges</term> <note>original: *simultates*; the political rivalries and personal vendettas that follow a civil war.</note>

<summary>This page celebrates the Athenian general Thrasybulus as the model of civic virtue for his role in liberating Athens from tyranny and establishing an amnesty to heal the city's internal divisions. He is honored not with gold or monuments, but with a simple olive wreath, symbolizing a peace achieved through wisdom and reconciliation.</summary>
<keywords>Thrasybulus, Athens, Democracy, Amnesty, Olive Wreath, Pallas Athena, Civic Virtue, Honor, Peace</keywords>

## The immortal name of the valiant.

->You see the tomb of the descendant of Aeacus on the Rhoetean shore,<-
<note>The "descendant of Aeacus" (original: *Aeacidæ*) refers to the Greek hero Achilles. Rhoeteum was a promontory near Troy where he was said to be buried.</note>
->Which white-footed Thetis visits quite often.<-
<note>Thetis was a sea goddess and the mother of Achilles; she is "white-footed" because of the sea foam.</note>
->This stone is always covered with green amaranth,<-
<note>The amaranth (original: *amarantho*) is a flower named in Greek for being "unfading," making it a perfect symbol for eternal fame.</note>
->Because the honor of the hero will never die.<-
->Here lies the wall of the Greeks. The death of great Hector owes no more<-
<note>Achilles was described by Homer as the "wall" or "bulwark" of the Achaean (Greek) army.</note>
->To the Maeonian, than the Maeonian owes to him.<-
<note>The "Maeonian" (original: *Mæonides*) refers to the poet Homer. This passage argues that while Homer made Achilles famous, Achilles provided the great deeds that gave Homer his subject matter.</note>

## The noble and high-born.

->A golden brooch used to bind Athenian garments:<-
<note>original: *Cecropias*. Cecrops was the legendary first king of Athens, so "Cecropian" is a poetic term for Athenian.</note>
->To which a cicada was joined, clinging with its prong.<-
<note>In antiquity, Athenian nobles wore golden grasshopper (cicada) pins to show they were "autochthonous"—meaning their ancestors were born from the soil of Athens itself, rather than being immigrants.</note>
->The shoe, beneath which was a small moon in the Arcadian fashion,<-
<note>The "small moon" (original: *lunula*) was a crescent-shaped ornament worn on the footwear of Roman senators.</note>
->Is worn by the fathers of the race of Romulus.<-
<note>The "race of Romulus" (original: *Romulidis*) refers to the Romans.</note>
->Because they claimed to be indigenous: these signs were carried<-
->By men illustrious for their ancient nobility.<-

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut tailpiece sits below the second poem. It depicts a central rectangular panel with figures in a landscape (possibly mythological), framed by elaborate scrollwork, floral motifs, and a central grotesque face at the bottom.</image-desc>

<summary>This page discusses the immortality of heroic fame through the example of Achilles and describes the specific historical ornaments—such as the Athenian cicada and the Roman crescent—used by ancient elites to signal their noble and native-born status.</summary>
<keywords>Achilles, Homer, immortality, nobility, Athens, Rome, symbols of status, amaranth, ancestry</keywords>

## The twelve labors of Hercules.
->Allegorically<-
<note>original Greek: *ἀλληγορικῶς*. This section interprets the famous mythological labors of Hercules as metaphors for intellectual and moral virtues rather than just physical feats.</note>

Eloquence surpasses the praises of invincible strength.
It unravels the empty snares and sayings of the Sophists.
<note>The Sophists were ancient Greek teachers often accused of using clever but fallacious arguments. Here, Hercules’ "strength" is equated to the power of truth over deception.</note>
No fury or madness is more powerful than virtue.
Wealth yields to the wise man because of his steady course.
He spurns greed, and takes no joy in plunder or usury.
He conquers and strips feminine deceits of their distinctions.
<note>This likely refers to the labor of obtaining the belt of Hippolyta, Queen of the Amazons, here interpreted as overcoming trickery.</note>
He cleanses filth and adds refinement to the mind.
<note>A reference to the cleaning of the Augean Stables, interpreted as the purification of the soul or intellect.</note>
He hates illicit unions and drives away the harmful.
Barbary and impious savagery finally pay the penalty.
The virtue of one man scatters gathered enemies.
He brings into his homeland many goods from foreign shores.
It flies through the learned mouths of men and never perishes.

## On those of illegitimate birth.

You who are base-born should always celebrate the honors of Hercules:
For he was the leader of your rank.
<note>original: *nothos* and *spurij*. Hercules was the son of Zeus and a mortal woman, Alcmene, making him "illegitimate" in the context of Hera’s (Juno’s) marriage.</note>
Nor could he become a god before he, as an infant, sucked
The milk that Juno gave him, unaware of the trickery.
<note>According to myth, Zeus tricked a sleeping Juno into nursing the infant Hercules. When she woke and pushed him away, the spilled milk created the Milky Way. This "divine" milk was said to have granted him immortality.</note>

## Inequality.

Just as the falcon flying on high cuts through the thin air,
While the jackdaw, the goose, and the duck feed on the ground:
So does the great Pindar climb above the highest heavens,
So does Bacchylides know only how to crawl upon the ground.
<note>Pindar and Bacchylides were rival Greek lyric poets. Historically, Pindar has been viewed as the "eagle" of poets—sublime and difficult—while Bacchylides was often seen as more pedestrian or simple by comparison.</note>

<summary>This page provides an allegorical interpretation of the Labors of Hercules as moral and intellectual victories, explains his divine status as a "noble bastard," and uses a bird metaphor to contrast the poetic genius of Pindar with his rival Bacchylides.</summary>
<keywords>Hercules, Labors of Hercules, Allegory, Virtue, Eloquence, Juno, Pindar, Bacchylides, Illegitimacy, Poetic Excellence</keywords>

### On those who fall away.
<note>original: *In desciscentes*. This refers to people who deviate from a virtuous path or fail to live up to their early promise.</note>

> Because you have stained an excellent beginning with a shameful end,
> and have turned your duty into a crime,
> You have done just as the goat, who, when she strikes
> her own <term>milk-pail</term><note>original: *mulctraria*. A vessel used during milking.</note>, pours out her own wealth with her hoof.

<image-desc>An ornamental woodcut headpiece separates the two poems, consisting of a symmetrical design with a central leaf motif and decorative tassels hanging from the ends.</image-desc>

### Unequal rivalry.
<note>original: *Aemulatio impar*. This poem criticizes those who attempt to gain status by following greater men, or who hold positions they are unqualified for simply because no one else is available.</note>

> The degenerate kite accompanies the high-flying hawk,
> and often takes a share of the falling prey.
> The <term>sea bream</term><note>original: *sargus*. A common fish in the Mediterranean, here contrasted with the more prized mullet.</note> follows the mullet, and greedily devours
> with its mouth the dainties that the other has rejected and passed by.
> So Oenocrates acts with me: but the assembly of students, now deserted,
> uses this bleary-eyed man as if he were an eye.
<note>Oenocrates (a name meaning "Wine-ruler") is used here as a satirical figure. The author suggests that Oenocrates is only seen as an "eye" (a leader or visionary) because the school is empty of better men—a variation of the proverb "in the kingdom of the blind, the one-eyed man is king."</note>

<image-desc>A small decorative woodcut ornament follows the second poem, depicting a winged mask with small horns, reminiscent of a satyr.</image-desc>

<summary>This page features two moralizing epigrams regarding honor: the first uses the image of a goat spilling its own milk to criticize those who ruin a good reputation, while the second uses animal scavengers to mock mediocre people who thrive only in the absence of their superiors.</summary>

## Albutius to Alciato, persuading him to withdraw from the Italian turmoils and to teach in France.
<note>Gaspare Albutio, a fellow legal scholar, writes this to Andrea Alciato. At the time, Italy was ravaged by the Italian Wars. Albutio suggests that Alciato—the "father" of the emblem book—should accept a prestigious teaching position in Bourges, France.</note>

->The tree that gave these fruits, a stranger to our climate,<-
->Came first from the Eastern lands of <term>Persia</term> before;<- <note>original: *Persidis*. The Latin name for the peach, *malum Persicum* or "Persian apple," reflects the ancient belief that it originated there.</note>
->It was made better by being transplanted: that which was once harmful<-
->In its own country, now bears sweet fruit for us here.<-
<note>The author draws on an ancient myth, recorded by the Roman naturalist Pliny, which claimed that peaches were poisonous in Persia but became edible and sweet when moved to the soil of Egypt or Europe.</note>
->It bears a leaf shaped like a tongue; it bears fruit very like a heart,<-
<note>In Renaissance symbolism, the peach leaf represented the eloquent tongue of a teacher, while the fruit represented the sincerity of the heart.</note>
->Alciato, learn from this how to lead your own life.<-
->Far from your fatherland, you will be held in higher esteem;<-
->You are greatly wise of heart, and no less powerful in speech.<-
<note>original: *Multùm corde sapis, nec minus ore uales*. This concludes the metaphor: Alciato’s "heart" (intellect) and "tongue" (eloquence) will flourish more in the safety of France than in his war-torn home.</note>

<summary>A poetic appeal to the scholar Andrea Alciato, using the metaphor of a transplanted peach tree to argue that his talents will be better appreciated and safer in France than in his native Italy.</summary>
<keywords>Andrea Alciato, Gaspare Albutio, peach tree, exile, Italy, France, eloquence, wisdom, transplantation</keywords>

<header>THE PRINCE.</header>
<sig>H 2</sig>

# THE PRINCE.

## THE PRINCE ENSURING THE
## SAFETY OF HIS SUBJECTS.
<note>original: *Princeps subditorum incolumitatem procurans*. The term *incolumitas* implies not just safety, but wholeness and preservation from harm.</note>

<image-desc>An emblem set within a highly decorative woodcut architectural frame. The frame features two muscular caryatids flanking the central image and a grotesque mask at the top center. The emblem itself depicts a large dolphin coiled around the shank of a maritime anchor. The scene takes place at sea, with waves in the foreground, a ship in the distance, and a mountainous coastline with a small town to the right. A radiant sun is partially obscured by clouds on the left, and several birds fly in the sky.</image-desc>

->Whenever the <term>Titanic brothers</term> disturb the seas,<- <note>original: *Titanij fratres*. This refers to the winds, who in mythology were the sons of the Titan Astraeus and Eos (the Dawn).</note>
->Then the cast anchor helps the wretched sailors.<-
->A dolphin, kind toward men, embraces this anchor: so that<-
->It may be fixed more safely in the deepest shoals.<-
->How fitting it is for mindful Kings to bear these insignia?<-
->To be to their own people what an anchor is to sailors?<-
<note>The dolphin and anchor is one of the most famous symbols of the Renaissance. While often meaning "Make haste slowly" (*Festina Lente*), Alciato here interprets the dolphin—traditionally seen as a friend to man—as a force that stabilizes the anchor of the state during the "storms" of war or crisis.</note>

**Vocabulary:** <term>Prince</term> (Ruler), <term>safety</term> (preservation), <term>Titanic</term> (relating to the winds), <term>anchor</term> (symbol of stability), <term>Dolphin</term> (symbol of the helpful ruler), <term>Kings</term>, <term>sailors</term> (the subjects).

<summary>This page introduces the qualities of an ideal ruler, using the emblem of a dolphin wrapped around an anchor to symbolize a prince's duty to provide stability and protection to his subjects during turbulent times.</summary>
<keywords>Prince, sovereignty, protection, anchor and dolphin, stability, nautical metaphor, leadership</keywords>

<header>THE PRINCE.</header>

## On the Council of a good prince.
### A DIALOGUE.

<image-desc>A detailed woodcut illustration is set within a heavy ornamental frame. The central scene shows a crowned king seated on a throne under a draped canopy, holding a scepter. He is flanked by two groups of three seated councilors or senators wearing robes and caps. The floor is tiled with a patterned carpet leading to the throne. The surrounding decorative frame features a crowned head at the top center, architectural herms on the sides, and a grimacing mask at the base amidst scrollwork and foliage.</image-desc>

*Images with severed hands sit here before the altars of the Gods:*
*The first of these is deprived of sight.*
*These symbols of supreme Power and of the Holy <term>Senate</term>* <note>original: *Senatus*. In this context, it refers to the ruler's high council or supreme judicial body rather than a modern legislative parliament.</note>
*Were once devised by the men of <term>Thebes</term>.* <note>The author Alciato is referencing a passage from the Greek writer Plutarch, who described statues of judges in the Egyptian city of Thebes that lacked hands to symbolize their incorruptibility.</note>
*Why do they sit? Because it is fitting for <term>judges</term>* <note>original: *Iuridicos*. Those who interpret or administer the law.</note> *to be of a*
*Quiet and serious mind, and not to waver with a fickle spirit.*
*Why are they without hands? Lest they take <term>bribes</term>:* <note>original: *xenia*. Originally meaning "guest-gifts," it here refers to payments meant to influence a legal decision.</note> *nor should they*
*Allow themselves to be swayed by promises or gifts.*
*But the ruler is blind? [Yes,] because the Council, steadfast and*
*Without personal bias, carries out its decrees using only its ears.*
<note>The "blindness" of the chief judge or prince signifies impartiality; he decides based on the evidence heard (*solis auribus*—with ears alone) rather than being prejudiced by the appearance or status of the people before him.</note>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of ancient handless and blind statues from Thebes to illustrate the ideal qualities of a ruler's judicial council: stability, resistance to bribery, and absolute impartiality.</summary>
<keywords>Justice, Senate, Impartiality, Corruption, Thebes, Bribes, Judicial Ethics</keywords>

<header>THE PRINCE.</header>
<sig>H 3</sig>

### What Christ does not take,
### the treasury seizes.
<note>original: *Quod non capit Christus, rapit fiscus.* This was a common legal proverb. In a literal sense, it meant that property not legally inherited by the Church or private individuals would be confiscated by the state. Here, it takes on a more cynical tone regarding a ruler's greed.</note>

<image-desc>The page features a large, ornate woodcut border consisting of architectural elements, scrolls, and grotesque figures. At the top center of the border is a bearded, foliate mask. The side panels depict figures emerging from pedestal-like structures. At the bottom center, another grotesque mask with pointed ears is framed by scrollwork. Inside this border, a central woodcut illustration depicts a man in a feathered cap and a cloak standing beside a fluted column base. He holds a long staff and squeezes a sponge, with liquid dripping onto the floor. In the distant background, a landscape reveals a gallows with three hanged bodies and a small group of figures standing nearby.</image-desc>

->The greedy hand of the <term>Prince</term> squeezes out<-
<note>original: *Princeps*. While we often think of a "Prince" as a son of a king, in this Renaissance context, it refers to any sovereign ruler or head of state.</note>
->the little sponges which he had already soaked before.<-
<note>The "sponge" is a famous metaphor for corrupt officials. The Roman historian Suetonius claimed the Emperor Vespasian used to promote the most rapacious officials to high office so that they would grow "wet" with bribes, only to "squeeze" them later through fines and confiscations.</note>
->He promotes thieves to the highest ranks: whom he then constrains,<-
->so that he may turn their <term>ill-gotten gains</term> into his own treasury.<-
<note>original: *malè parta*—literally "badly produced" or "wrongfully acquired." This refers to the wealth the officials stole from the people, which the Prince now takes for himself rather than returning it to the victims.</note>

<vocab>Prince, Christ, Treasury, little sponges, thieves, ill-gotten gains</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the "sponge" metaphor to criticize rulers who intentionally appoint corrupt officials, allowing them to plunder the populace only so the ruler can later seize their riches for the state treasury.</summary>
<keywords>corruption, Prince, treasury, greed, sponge metaphor, justice, fiscal policy</keywords>

<header>T H E  C O M M O N W E A L T H.</header>

<image-desc>The page is framed by an ornate Renaissance-style woodcut border. At the top center is a winged grotesque head with a crown of foliage, flanked by cornucopias. ## Counselors of Princes.

->It is said that the Centaur <term>Chiron</term> educated<-
<note>In Greek mythology, Chiron was a wise centaur who tutored several great heroes. Unlike other centaurs, he was known for his knowledge of medicine, music, and hunting.</note>
->the sons of heroes and great Achilles in his stables.<-
->He teaches that whoever sits as an advisor to kings<-
->must be a half-beast teacher and a half-man centaur.<-
->He is a beast when he harms allies and crushes enemies;<-
->and he is a man when he pretends to be dutiful to the people.<-
<note>This passage reflects the Renaissance political idea—famously echoed by Machiavelli—that a ruler or his advisor must know how to use both the "beast" (force) and the "man" (law/reason).</note>

## The Prince’s Mercy.

->Because the King of the wasps never uses his sting,<-
<note>Natural historians in the Renaissance, following ancient writers like Pliny, incorrectly believed that the "king" of bees or wasps (what we now know is the queen) possessed no sting, symbolizing a ruler's inherent restraint.</note>
->and because he is twice the size of all the others,<-
->this proves a merciful rule and moderate kingdoms,<-
->with sacred laws entrusted to good judges.<-

## T H E  C O M M O N W E A L T H.

## Public Welfare.

->The Epidaurian son of <term>Phoebus</term> sits upon the raised altars,<-
<note>Phoebigena, or "Son of Phoebus," refers to Asclepius, the god of healing. His most famous sanctuary was at Epidaurus.</note>
->and the mild God is hidden within a massive snake.<-
<note>Asclepius was often represented by a serpent, which was a symbol of rejuvenation and healing in the ancient world.</note>
->The sick run to him and pray that he comes as a bringer of health;<-
->he nods his head, and makes their prayers come true.<-

## The Republic Set Free.

->As if liberty were recovered by the death of <term>Caesar</term>,<-
<note>Refers to the assassination of Julius Caesar on the Ides of March, 44 BCE, by a group of senators who called themselves the "Liberators."</note>
->this money was coined under the leaders named Brutus.<-
<note>Brutus, one of the assassins, famously minted a coin (the EID MAR denarius) featuring daggers and a freedom cap.</note>
->Daggers are shown first, over which stands a <term>pileus</term>,<-
<note>The *pileus* was a felt cap given to Roman slaves upon their manumission (legal freeing) to signify their new status as citizens.</note>
->such as slaves receive when they are set free.<-

<summary>This page presents four emblems concerning the state: the dual nature of political advisors, the biological metaphor for a ruler's mercy, the divine protection of public health, and the numismatic symbols of political liberty.</summary>

<keywords>Chiron, Achilles, political advice, royal mercy, Asclepius, Epidaurus, public health, Julius Caesar, Brutus, liberty, pileus, coinage</keywords>

<page-num>119</page-num>
<header>L I F E.</header>

## L I F E.

### On human life.

<image-desc>A central woodcut illustration is enclosed within an ornate architectural border. The border features a goat's head (bucranium) at the top center, flanked by flaming torches and swags. The side columns are decorated with scrolling foliage and female busts. The base of the frame features a large grotesque mask surrounded by elaborate scrollwork, with small lizard-like figures in the bottom corners. The central illustration depicts the two "weeping and laughing" philosophers in a landscape. On the left, Heraclitus stands in long robes with his hands on his head in a gesture of despair; a globe sits on the ground beside him. On the right, Democritus sits on a rock, looking toward Heraclitus or the viewer. Books are visible on the ground around them.</image-desc>

->Now, more than ever, weep for the sorrows of human life,<-
<note>original: "Plus solito humanæ nunc defle incommoda uitæ"</note>
-><term>Heraclitus</term>: for it overflows with even greater misfortunes.<-
<note>Heraclitus of Ephesus (c. 535–475 BC) was known as the "Weeping Philosopher" because of his melancholy view of the world’s constant change and human suffering.</note>
->And you, <term>Democritus</term>, raise your loud laughter once again<-
<note>Democritus (c. 460–370 BC), the "Laughing Philosopher," chose to respond to the vanity and folly of mankind with mockery and mirth.</note>
->(if ever there was a time); for life has become even more of a farce.<-
->Meanwhile, as I observe these scenes, I ponder to what end<-
->I should join you in weeping, or how I might joke with you from the <term>spleen</term>.<-
<note>In the humoral theory of medicine, the spleen was believed to be the organ responsible for laughter and mirth, though it was also linked to melancholy.</note>

<sig>H 4</sig>

<vocab>Heraclitus, Democritus, life, human, weeping philosopher, laughing philosopher</vocab>

<header>L I F E.</header>

## L I F E.

### That life must sometimes be redeemed with wealth.
<note>original: "Aëre quandoque falutem redimendam." The Latin "aere" refers to bronze or copper coins, symbolizing money or material wealth.</note>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration set within an ornate, architectural border. The illustration depicts a beaver (fiber) on a riverbank, crouching and biting at its own groin—a classical motif from bestiaries and emblem books. In the background to the right, a hunter with a staff and two hunting dogs approach. The scene is framed by a highly decorative border featuring elaborate scrollwork, floral motifs, and grotesque masks: a horned satyr face at the top center and a lion-like face at the bottom center. The sides of the frame include profile busts of human figures integrated into architectural pedestals.</image-desc>

> Though slow of foot, with a heavy and hanging belly,
>   the <term>beaver</term> nonetheless escapes the snares by this artifice.
<note>original: "fiber." The beaver was often called "fiber" in classical Latin, though "castor" was also common.</note>
> With his own teeth, he tears away his <term>medicinal parts</term>
<note>original: "medicata uirilia." This refers to the beaver's testicles or scent glands. In antiquity and the Middle Ages, it was widely believed that beavers were hunted for "castoreum," a substance found in their glands used for medicine, and that the animal would castrate itself to satisfy the hunter and escape with its life.</note>
>   and casts them aside, knowing well that he is hunted for their sake.
> From this example, you will learn not to be stingy with your possessions,
>   and to give money to your enemies, so that you may redeem your life.
<note>original: "Et uitam ut redimas, hoftibus æra dare." This moral suggests that material wealth is a small price to pay for one's physical safety or "salutem" (health/salvation/safety).</note>

<summary>This page presents an emblem of a beaver self-mutilating to escape a hunter, serving as a metaphor for the wisdom of sacrificing wealth to preserve one's life.</summary>
<keywords>beaver, fiber, ransom, sacrifice, survival, castoreum, Alciato, emblem, wealth vs life</keywords>

## D E A T H.

### One must not wrestle with the dead.
<note>original: "Cum laruis non luctandum." The Latin "larva" refers to a ghost, a specter, or the spirit of the deceased. The proverb warns against attacking those who can no longer defend themselves.</note>

<image-desc>
An ornate woodcut border frames the text and central illustration. At the top, a winged cherub head is set within a decorative scrollwork pediment. The sides are composed of architectural columns integrated with male figures (herms) that have bare torsos and wear turbans. The bottom of the border features elaborate scrollwork with three distinct grotesque or classical masks.

The central woodcut illustration depicts a dead lion lying on its side in a rocky, mountainous landscape. In the background, a ruined castle or fortification sits atop a hill. Several hares or rabbits are shown swarming the lion's carcass; one bites at its hind leg, while another appears to be pulling at the lion's mane or beard.
</image-desc>

->Hector, dying from the blow of <term>Achilles'</term> spear,<-
<note>original: "Æacidæ." This is a patronymic for Achilles, referring to his grandfather, Aeacus.</note>
->Who had so many times before conquered his enemies:<-
->Could not keep silent while his enemies insulted him,<-
->While they prepared to bind the chains to his feet and the chariot.<-
<note>This refers to the scene in the *Iliad* where the Greeks gathered around Hector's body to mock and stab it before Achilles dragged the corpse around the city of Troy.</note>
->“Tear me apart as you please. For thus even timid hares<-
->Pluck at the beard of a lion once <term>deprived of the light</term>.”<-
<note>original: "cassi luce leonis." In classical poetry, "light" is often used as a metaphor for life; to be "deprived of light" is to be dead.</note>

### On Death and Love.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting the mythological exchange of arrows between Death and Cupid. In the clouds, a winged skeleton (Death) and a winged youth (Cupid) are seen together, both holding bows. Below them on the ground, an old bearded man in long robes stands with a staff/scythe amidst several dead bodies. One figure on the right appears to be reclining or in a state of distress/love.</image-desc>

Death wandered joined with her companion Cupid.
Death carried quivers with her; little Love carried his weapons.
They turned aside together, and together they slept for one night;
Love is always blind, and at this time, Death was blind too.
For each one, failing to look ahead, took the other’s arrows:
Death holds the golden ones, and the boy holds the bony shafts.
From this, the old man who ought now to belong to <term>Acheron</term>:
<note>original: "Acheronticus." Acheron is one of the rivers of the Greek underworld; to be "Acherontic" means to be near death or belonging to the realm of the dead.</note>
Behold, he falls in love, and prepares floral wreaths for his head.
But I, because Love has struck me with the swapped bow,
I waste away; and the <term>Fates</term> lay their hand upon me.
<note>original: "fata." In Roman mythology, the Parcae or Fates controlled the metaphorical thread of life for every mortal and god.</note>
Spare me, boy; spare me, Death, holding your victorious banners!
Grant that I may love; grant that the old man may go to the underworld.
<note>original: "subeat fac Acheronta senex." The poet pleads for the natural order to be restored: that the young should feel love and the old should face their end.</note>

<summary>This page recounts a mythological fable where Death and Cupid accidentally swap their arrows at an inn. This cosmic error explains why some elderly people foolishly fall in love while the young and vibrant are suddenly struck down by death.</summary>
<keywords>Death, Cupid, arrows, Acheron, Fates, mythology, aging, love and death</keywords>

--- Page 127 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative from page 122, where Death and Cupid accidentally exchanged their quivers of arrows while sharing a room at an inn. This entry explains the tragic consequence of that mistake: when Love (Cupid) intends to strike the young with desire, he unknowingly uses the lethal arrows of Death, leading to their untimely demise.</meta>

### On a beautiful woman prematurely snatched away by fate.

<image-desc>A central rectangular woodcut shows a landscape with a wild, long-haired figure—representing either Death or one of the Fates—striding across two prone bodies. On the left, a bearded man lies on the ground; on the right, another figure is slumped. The background contains rocky hills and a castle or fortification. Below this main illustration is a smaller horizontal decorative vignette depicting a central panel with diminutive figures, bracketed by two horned satyr masks.</image-desc>

->Why, O Death, have you dared to seize the boy <term>Love</term> through such trickery?<-
<note>original: "puerum... Amorem." Cupid, the boy-god of love. The "trickery" refers to the accidental swapping of their arrows described on the previous page.</note>

->So that he might hurl your weapons, while he thinks they are his own.<-
<note>In this mythological conceit, the poet explains the tragedy of young people dying in their prime. Because Cupid and Death mixed up their quivers, Cupid now shoots the "weapons" of Death (arrows of mortality) at the young and beautiful, believing he is shooting them with the arrows of love.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the emblem of the swapped arrows, providing a poetic explanation for why the young and beautiful are often "snatched away" by fate too soon. It suggests that Cupid unknowingly uses Death’s arrows when he intends to inspire love.</summary>

<keywords>Death, Cupid, Love, fate, arrows, mythology, mortality, tragedy</keywords>
