### On an untimely death.
<note>original: "In mortem præproperam."</note>

The boy who once enticed and tormented the hearts of young girls with his beauty—a youth more handsome and noble than any other in the entire city—has died before his time. To no one is his loss more to be mourned, O **Arestus**, than to you, to whom he was joined by a chaste and devoted love.

Therefore, you build for him this tomb as a monument to such profound grief, and you strike the heavens with your mournful cries:

"Do you depart without me, my beloved? And shall we no longer walk together? Will you no longer spend pleasant hours of leisure with me in our shared studies? Instead, the earth shall cover you; but the face of the <term>Gorgon</term><note>The Medusa, often placed on tombs to "ward off" evil or to represent the cold, petrifying stillness of death.</note> and the dolphins—the signs of your <term>fate</term><note>original: "fati." In Renaissance emblem art, dolphins often represented speed or the swift passage of time and souls.</note>—will bear witness to your sorrowful end."

<vocab>Death, tomb, Gorgon, Arestus, monument, of fate</vocab>

<summary>This page presents a funeral elegy for a beautiful youth who died prematurely, focusing on the grief of his companion Arestus. It uses the imagery of a classical tomb adorned with a Gorgon and dolphins to symbolize the tragic and sudden nature of his passing.</summary>
<keywords>untimely death, mourning, Arestus, Gorgon, Medusa, funerary monument, male friendship, epitaph</keywords>

### Terminus.
<note>original: "Terminus." Terminus was the Roman god of boundaries and landmarks, often depicted as a stone pillar or a bust without arms to show he never moves from his spot.</note>

->A square stone is planted deep, a most solid block,<-
->Upon it stands a bust, hair curled, visible to the chest;<-
->It declares that it yields to no one—such<-
->Is **Terminus**, the one goal that drives all men.<-
->The day is fixed, the times prefixed by the Fates,<-
->And the end brings judgment upon the beginning.<- <note>original: "Deq; ferunt primis ultima iudicium." A philosophical sentiment that a person's life can only be fully judged once it has reached its conclusion.</note>

<image-desc>A central woodcut ornament of a grotesque, horned ram-like mask with decorative fabric wings draped to either side.</image-desc>

### The inheritance of a wealthy man.
<note>original: "Opulenti hæreditas."</note>

->From one side the Trojans snatch <term>Patroclus</term><note>In the Iliad, Patroclus went into battle wearing the armor of Achilles; when he was killed, a fierce battle broke out over his body and the valuable armor.</note> in his borrowed armor;<-
->From the other, his comrades and the whole <term>Pelasgian</term><note>An archaic name for the Greeks.</note> crowd resist.<-
->Hector seizes the spoils, the Greeks take the corpse:<-
->This little story is reenacted whenever a wealthy man dies.<-
->A massive quarrel erupts, until at last the heir settles it,<-
->Leaving a little something for the crows and the vultures.<- <note>A cynical observation that while the heir takes the fortune, the "vultures" (perhaps greedy lawyers or distant relatives) pick over the remains.</note>

<summary>This page features two emblems regarding the finality of life: the first depicts Death as the unyielding boundary-god Terminus, and the second compares the squabbling over a rich man's estate to the Homeric battle over Patroclus's body.</summary>

## *F R I E N D S H I P.*

->Friendship enduring even<-
->after death.<-

<image-desc>A central woodcut illustration depicts a withered, leafless elm tree. A lush grapevine, heavy with bunches of grapes, winds its way up the dead trunk and spreads across its dry branches. The tree stands on a small mound of earth. In the distance to the left, the sun is seen low on the horizon. The image is enclosed within an elaborate woodcut border: at the top center is a horned satyr-like head; on the left is a bearded male caryatid figure; on the right is a female caryatid figure; at the bottom center is a grotesque mask flanked by two birds sitting in their nests.</image-desc>

*The elm, withered by age and stripped of its leaves,*
*Is embraced by the shady foliage of a green vine.*
*She acknowledges the cycles of nature; and grateful to her parent-support,* <note>original: "parenti." The vine treats the elm as a parent or protector because the tree provided the structure for it to grow when the tree was young and strong.</note>
*She repays the mutual duties of service through her own growth.*
*By this example she warns us to seek such friends,*
*Whom even the final day may not sever from their bond.* <note>original: "summa dies." A poetic Latin expression for the day of one's death.</note>

<vocab>Friendship, Alciato, emblem, after death, elm, grapevine</vocab>

<summary>This emblem uses the botanical metaphor of a grapevine growing over a dead elm tree to illustrate the concept of loyalty, suggesting that true friends support one another even after one has passed away.</summary>

->The man deprived of sight <note>original: "lumine captus." A poetic Latin idiom for a blind person.</note> carries the lame man <note>original: "loripedem." Literally "thong-footed," referring to someone whose legs are weak or twisted.</note> on his shoulders:<-

->This one lends his eyes, while that one lends his feet.<-

->The Graces.<-

> *Three Graces <note>original: "Charites." The Greek name for the Gratiae.</note> assist Venus, and follow their mistress;*
> *And from them they provide pleasures and nourishment.*
> *Euphrosyne <note>The Grace of Mirth or Joy.</note> brings joy, Aglaia <note>The Grace of Splendor or Beauty.</note> a beautiful radiance,*
> *Persuasion <note>original: "Pithus," the Greek Peitho.</note> is their counsel, and a charming wit of speech.*
> *Why are they naked? Because beauty consists of the brightness of the mind,*
> *And it pleases through an extraordinary simplicity.*
> *Or is it because the ungrateful return nothing, and the casket of the Graces*
> *Is empty? He who gives gifts is left naked and in need.* <note>This suggests that a true benefactor gives so freely that they keep nothing for themselves, or that ungrateful recipients leave the giver with nothing.</note>
> *Why were winged sandals <term>talaria: Winged sandals, most famously worn by the messenger god Mercury, symbolizing speed and swift action.</term> recently added to their feet? He gives twice*
> *Who gives quickly: a slow favor is of the smallest value.* <note>original: "bis dat qui cito dat." A famous Latin proverb meaning that a prompt response doubles the value of the help provided.</note>

---

# HOSTILITY.

## Against detractors.

->*Shameful fools* <note>original: "matulæ," literally "chamber pots." A Roman insult used for worthless or empty-headed people.</note> *and stupid teachers dare*<-
->*To vomit the bile of their impure hearts upon me:*<-
->*What shall I do? Shall I retaliate? But will I not be said*<-
->*To have caught a noisy cicada* <note>A metaphor for a loud but insignificant pest.</note> *by a single wing?*<-
->*What use is it to drive away flies with laborious fans?*<-
->*It is better to ignore what you cannot destroy.*<-

## Vain attack.

->*A dog gazes at the lunar orb at night (as if into a mirror):*<-
->*And seeing himself, he believes another dog is within,*<-
->*And he barks: but his useless voice is scattered by the winds,*<-
->*And deaf Diana* <note>The goddess of the hunt and the moon. She is "deaf" because she remains indifferent to the dog's barking.</note> *continues her course.*<-

<header>HOSTILITY.</header>
<sig>I 3</sig>

### Some harm, on account of a bad neighbor.
<note>original: "Aliquid mali, propter vicinum malum." This is a proverbial warning suggesting that proximity to something dangerous or incompatible inevitably leads to injury.</note>

->Personification.<-
<note>original Greek: "Προσσωποποιΐα" (Prosopopoeia). This is a rhetorical term for the technique used here, where inanimate objects—the two pots—are given human voices and characters.</note>

<image-desc>A central rectangular woodcut illustration is set within a large, ornate ornamental border. The illustration depicts two pots floating in a turbulent, rushing river. The pot on the left is made of metal and has a large circular handle, while the pot on the right is made of clay. In the background, there is a landscape of rolling hills and trees. The wide border surrounding the central image consists of fruit and floral garlands at the top with a central blank cartouche; the sides are flanked by vertical arrangements of nude male figures (atlantes) in dynamic poses; the bottom features two reclining nude figures flanking a central pedestal bowl filled with fruit.</image-desc>

A rushing torrent swept away two pots; one was made of metal,
The other was fashioned from earth by a potter's hand. <note>original: "figuli... manu." This emphasizes the humble, man-made origin of the clay pot compared to the harder metal one.</note>
The metal one asks the other to be carried close to her,
So that, joined together, they both might withstand the plunging waters.
To which the clay pot replied: "I have no desire for dealings with you,
Lest this closeness bring me many misfortunes.
For whether the wave brings you to me, or me to you:
I alone, being fragile, will be shattered, while you remain unharmed." <note>original: "terar," which can mean to be rubbed, ground down, or crushed. In this context, it describes the clay pot being smashed by the impact of the harder metal pot.</note>

<summary>This page presents the famous Aesopic fable of the "Two Pots" (one of bronze, one of clay) to illustrate the danger of unequal social or political alliances. It warns that when the weak associate with the powerful, the weak are the ones who suffer in times of shared trouble.</summary>

<keywords>Hostility, Aesop's fables, personification, fragility, social hierarchy, caution, allegory</keywords>

## On him who perished by the cruelty of his own kind.
<note>original: "In eum qui truculentia suorum perierit." This title suggests that the dolphin's death is a result of the "cruelty" or harshness of the sea, its own home.</note>

### P E R S O N I F I C A T I O N.
<note>original Greek: "Προσσωποποιΐα" (Prosopopoeia). This is a rhetorical device where an inanimate object or animal is given a voice to speak its own story.</note>

<image-desc>The page is framed by an elaborate woodcut border. The top section consists of a central cartouche surrounded by swags of fruit and foliage. The side panels feature stacked, muscular male figures (atlantes) in dynamic, contorted poses. The bottom panel shows two reclining nude figures flanking a central footed bowl overflowing with fruit. ->The tide drove me, an unwilling dolphin, onto the shore,<-
->A witness to how many dangers are in the faithless sea.<-
->For if Neptune does not spare even his own children,<-
<note>original: "alumnis." In Roman mythology, Neptune is the god of the sea; his "children" or "nurslings" are the creatures that live within it.</note>
->Who would think that men in ships could be safe?<-

<summary>A poem and illustration of a beached dolphin used as a metaphor for the inherent dangers of the world. It suggests that if the sea is hostile even to its own creatures, humans are in constant peril when traveling upon it.</summary>

<header>H O S T I L I T A S.</header>
<sig>I 3</sig>

<image-desc>An emblem page set within a decorative architectural woodcut frame. The frame features two Corinthian columns supporting a pediment with a central grotesque mask and garlands. The base of the frame contains another grotesque mask flanked by acanthus leaf scrolls. Within the frame, at the top, is a Greek and Latin title. ->The gifts of enemies are no gifts,<-
<note>original Greek: "Εχθρῶν ἄδωρα δῶρα" (Echthrōn adōra dōra). This is a famous proverb from Sophocles’ play *Ajax*, suggesting that a gift from a foe always carries a hidden curse.</note>
->On the gifts of enemies.<-
<note>original Latin: "In dona hostium."</note>

They say that shield-bearing Ajax and Hector of Troy
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Exchanged memorials of their wars.
<term>Hector</term> <note>referred to as "Priamides," the son of King Priam of Troy</note> received a belt; <term>Ajax</term> <note>referred to as "Telamonius," the son of Telamon</note> received a stiff sword.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Each took the very instrument of his own death.
For the sword finished off Ajax <note>Ajax later committed suicide using the very sword Hector gave him</note>, while the belt,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Fastened to <term>Thessalian wheels</term>, dragged the deceased Hector.
<note>original: "Aemonijs rotis." After Achilles killed Hector, he tied the body to his chariot using the belt Hector had received from Ajax and dragged him around the walls of Troy. "Aemonian" refers to Thessaly, the homeland of Achilles.</note>
Thus, the gifts which enemies send to enemies under the title of courtesy
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Carry within them the foreknowledge of a coming doom.

<summary>This page uses the legendary gift exchange between Ajax and Hector to illustrate that kindness from an enemy is deceptive. Both heroes were eventually killed or desecrated by the very items they received as "honorable" gifts.</summary>

### One must fear even the smallest creatures.
<note>original Latin: "A minimis quoque timendum." This title serves as a moral warning that no enemy is too small to be ignored or insulted.</note>

---

->The Beetle <note>original: "Scarabæus"</note> wages war, and provokes the enemy of its own accord,<-
->And though inferior in strength, it overcomes through strategy <term>consilio</term>.<-
<note>The term "consilio" here implies a clever plan or cunning, rather than brute force.</note>
->For it hides itself secretly and unnoticed within the eagle’s feathers,<-
->So that it may reach the hostile nest high among the stars.<-
->And by piercing the eggs, it prevents the hope of offspring from growing.<-
->In this way, having avenged the disgrace <note>original: "dedecus"</note> inflicted upon it, it departs.<-

<note>This poem refers to a famous Aesopian fable. After the eagle crushed the beetle's young or ignored its plea for mercy, the beetle pursued the eagle relentlessly. It discovered that even though the eagle is the queen of birds and can fly to the heavens, the beetle can hide in its plumage to reach its nest and destroy its eggs, eventually forcing the eagle to seek the protection of the god Jupiter—though even there, the beetle found a way to win its revenge.</note>

**Vocabulary:** Hostility, Beetle, eagle, strategy, offspring, disgrace, emblem.

<summary>This page uses the fable of the Eagle and the Beetle to illustrate that power is no protection against a clever and determined enemy, warning that even the smallest person can exact a devastating revenge if insulted.</summary>

# R E V E N G E.

## Just revenge.
<note>original Latin: "Iusta vindicta." This refers to the legal and moral concept of "lex talionis" or "the law of retaliation"—punishment that matches the crime.</note>

<image-desc>An emblem illustration set within a highly ornate woodcut border. The border is decorated with Renaissance-style scrollwork, cherubs holding a garland at the top, and various grotesque masks along the vertical columns and base. The central woodcut depicts the mythological scene of Odysseus (Itachus) blinding the Cyclops Polyphemus. The massive Cyclops is shown reclining against a rocky cave entrance. Odysseus stands over him, thrusting a long wooden stake into the giant's single eye. In the foreground, several sheep from the Cyclops's flock are huddled together. The landscape includes a sparse tree and rugged terrain.</image-desc>

->While the Cyclops <term>Cyclops</term> <note>A one-eyed giant from Greek mythology; here specifically Polyphemus.</note> reclines in the mouth of his winding cave,<-
->He sings these words to himself among his tender sheep:<-
->"You graze on the grass; I shall feed on the Achaean <note>original: "Achiuis." A term for the Greeks.</note> companions,<-
->And my belly <note>original: "uiscera nostra," literally "our guts."</note> shall carry 'Nobody' <note>original: "Vtin." This is a Latinization of the Greek "Outis," the name Odysseus used to trick the giant. Polyphemus promised to eat "Nobody" last as a "gift" for the wine Odysseus gave him.</note> last of all."<-
->The Ithacan <note>original: "Itachus." Referring to Odysseus, the King of Ithaca.</note> heard these things, and rendered the Cyclops<-
->Deprived of his sight. See how the author of a crime bears his own punishment?<-

<note>This poem recounts the famous episode from Homer’s Odyssey. Polyphemus has imprisoned Odysseus and his men, eating them two by two. Odysseus blinds the giant with a sharpened stake after getting him drunk, ensuring that the giant's cruelty is met with a fittingly violent end.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the concept of "Just Revenge" through the myth of Odysseus blinding the Cyclops Polyphemus. It argues that retribution is morally justified when it is delivered to someone who has committed acts of cruelty and hubris.</summary>

<keywords>Revenge, Justice, Odysseus, Polyphemus, Cyclops, Ithaca, Retribution, Greek Mythology</keywords>

<header>REVENGE.</header>
<sig>Fv</sig>

# Just revenge.
<note>original Latin: "Iusta vltio." This title suggests a form of vengeance that is morally or divinely sanctioned because it punishes a predator using its own aggression.</note>

<image-desc>An emblem illustration set within a highly decorative woodcut border. The border features Renaissance grotesque motifs, including snarling satyr-like masks on the sides and bottom, scrolled foliage, and winged putti in the upper corners flanking a central fanged mask. The central woodcut shows a raven perched on a gnarled, broken branch extending from a rocky cliffside. The bird holds a scorpion in its beak. The background depicts a landscape with a large tree trunk on the right and distant rolling hills under a cloudy sky.</image-desc>

*A raven was snatching a captured scorpion into the airy heights with its talons—*
*A reward won for its bold appetite.* <note>original: "audaci... gulæ," literally "for its daring throat/gluttony."</note>
*But the scorpion, slowly spreading poison through the bird’s limbs,*
*Acted as an avenger and drove its captor into the Stygian <term>Stygian waters</term>.* <note>original: "stygias... aquas." In Greek mythology, the Styx is the river of the underworld. To be driven into its waters is a poetic metaphor for dying.</note>
*O, what a laughable sight! He who was preparing death for others*
*Perishes himself, and has fallen by his own traps.* <note>original: "proprijs succubuitque dolis." This is a common moral in emblem literature: the "bitter bit," where the predator becomes the prey.</note>

<vocab>**Revenge** (Vindicta), **Just revenge** (Iusta vltio), **raven** (coruus), **Scorpion** (Scorpion), **poison** (ueneno), **Stygian** (Stygias), **traps or deceits** (dolis)</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the fable of a raven killed by the scorpion it intended to eat to illustrate the concept of "Just Revenge." It serves as a moral warning that those who plot the downfall of others often fall victim to their own schemes.</summary>
<keywords>revenge, justice, raven, scorpion, poetic justice, fables, Styx, morality</keywords>

## The guilt of the offender and the persuader is equal.
<note>original Latin: "Parem delinquentis, & suaforis culpam esse." This title sets the moral tone of the emblem, arguing that the person who incites an action is legally and morally inseparable from the one who performs it.</note>

> A victorious troop holds a herald captive in a foul prison,
> As he was blowing the signal-trumpet for battle. <note>original: "Præconem lituo perflantem classica." A <term>praeco</term> was a public crier or herald. The <term>lituus</term> was a curved bronze trumpet used by the Roman military, and <term>classica</term> refers to the trumpet-call used to summon soldiers to war.</note>
> To them, he offers this excuse: that he is not strong in weapons,
> Nor has he wounded any man’s side with a savage sword.
> To this they replied: "Nay, you are the greater sinner, you coward,
> Who rouses others to arms with the blare of brass." <note>original: "clangore... æris," literally the "noise of the bronze," referring to the metal of the trumpet. The soldiers argue that his music is what enabled the violence of others.</note>

<vocab>Vengeance, emblem, herald, trumpet, battle-signal, captive, prison, arms, you sin, persuader</vocab>

<summary>This page illustrates a moral lesson through the story of a captured herald who claims innocence because he did not fight. The captors reject his plea, establishing the principle that those who incite others to violence bear the same guilt as the combatants.</summary>
<keywords>vengeance, culpability, herald, trumpet, incitement, war, justice, military law</keywords>

<image-desc>The page is enclosed in a highly decorative woodcut border featuring architectural elements, scrolling foliage, and grotesque masks. # One person sins, another is punished.
<note>original Latin: "Alius peccat, alius plectitur." This title introduces a moral warning against misdirected anger or unfair scapegoating.</note>

Just as a puppy snatches up a stone and tires it out with his bite,
  Yet causes no matching harm to the one who threw it. <note>original: "percussori." This refers to the person who struck the dog or threw the stone. The dog attacks the object (the stone) rather than the person responsible for the pain.</note>
So many people allow their true enemies to slip away,
  And attack with their teeth those whom no guilt burdens. <note>original: "Et quos nulla grauat noxia: dente petunt." This warns that in their blind rage, people often hurt innocent bystanders or "instruments" while the real villain escapes justice.</note>

### Key Terms
| Latin Term | English Translation | Definition |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Vindicta** | Vengeance | The act of inflicting harm in return for an injury. |
| **Peccat** | Sins / Errs | To do wrong or commit a fault. |
| **Plectitur** | Is punished | To be beaten or suffer the consequences of a crime. |
| **Catulus** | Puppy / Whelp | <term>Catulus</term>: A young dog or any small animal offspring. |
| **Hosteis** | Enemies | Those who harbor active hatred or ill-will. |
| **Noxia** | Guilt / Offense | A harmful act or the state of being blameworthy. |
| **Dente** | With the tooth | Figuratively used here to mean "to attack" or "to bite back." |

<summary>This emblem uses the metaphor of a dog biting a stone thrown at it to criticize people who direct their anger toward innocent parties while letting the true culprits escape.</summary>

### The Madman's Sword.
<note>original Latin: "Insani gladius." This title refers to the weapon of the Greek hero Ajax during his famous fit of madness.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration contained within an elaborate architectural frame. The frame is composed of two caryatid columns shaped like bearded men on pedestals at the sides, supporting a decorative frieze with a central grotesque mask. The central image depicts the Greek hero Ajax in a state of madness. Ajax, dressed in Renaissance-style plate armor with a plumed helmet and cape, brandishes a sword in his raised right hand. He is shown in a landscape, attacking a flock of sheep and rams as if they were his enemies. In the background, there is a hilly terrain with a thatched-roof building. A small letter "V" is printed on the base of the right pedestal.</image-desc>

Sword-bearing Ajax stood in the midst of a bristly flock,
  Believing that in the slaughter of swine, he was slaying the sons of Tantalus. <note>original: "Tantalidas." This refers to Agamemnon and Menelaus, the leaders of the Greeks, who were descendants of Tantalus. After losing the competition for Achilles' armor to Odysseus, Ajax was struck with madness by the goddess Athena.</note>
Thus, the pig, acting as a substitute victim, paid the penalty
  In place of the son of Laertes, and for the sake of his cause. <note>original: "Laërtiade." This refers to Odysseus, the son of Laertes. In his delusion, Ajax believed the sheep he was killing were the rivals who had insulted his honor.</note>
Rage knows not how to strike its true enemies; its blow goes astray,
  And, lacking all reason, it rushes headlong into its own ruin. <note>The poem concludes with a moral warning: uncontrolled anger is ineffective against one's enemies and only leads to self-destruction.</note>

# PEACE.

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration set within a large, highly decorative architectural frame. The frame features a central grotesque mask at the top flanked by scrollwork and vases. On either side are statuesque herms: a male on the left and a female on the right, both emerging from square pedestals draped with cloth. The scene represents warlike power being turned to the service of peace.</image-desc>

With shoulders bearing towers, and a tusk of ivory,
  The elephant, <note>original: "barrus." An archaic Latin term for an elephant, often used in poetry.</note> who is accustomed to fiercely prevail in the wars of Mars, <note>original: "Martia bella." Mars was the Roman god of war; the phrase denotes the violence of combat.</note>
Has now placed its neck beneath the yoke; and, subdued by the goad,
  It draws the Imperial chariots <note>original: "Cæsareos currus." This refers to the triumphal chariots of an Emperor (Caesar), used here to symbolize state order.</note> toward the holy temples.
Even the wild beast recognizes that peoples everywhere are in harmony,
  And casting aside its weapons, it performs the duties of peace.

<vocab>Peace, Elephant, Wars of Mars, Imperial chariots, Peace, Emblem</vocab>

<summary>This page presents an emblem of Peace, using the metaphor of a war elephant being tamed to pull a ceremonial chariot. It illustrates how the instruments of war and animal ferocity are transformed into servants of civil order and religious devotion during times of harmony.</summary>

## Peace from war.

<image-desc>The page features a central woodcut illustration surrounded by an elaborate ornamental border. <summary>This emblem depicts a soldier's helmet transformed into a beehive, symbolizing the restoration of productivity and nature after a period of conflict. It argues that war is only a legitimate pursuit when it is the only remaining means to secure a lasting peace.</summary>

### Abundance from peace. <note>original: "Ex pace vbertas." This title links the state of political peace directly to economic and agricultural plenty.</note>

->Weave delicate garlands from the great ears of grain,<-
->Around which the vine-branch may wind its alternating shoots.<-
->Adorned by these, the Halcyons <note>original: "Hælciones." In Greek mythology, these birds (associated with kingfishers) were said to nest on the sea during a period of supernatural calm called "Halcyon Days."</note> on the waves of the tranquil, marble sea<-
->Build their nests; they cherish their chicks and those not yet ready to fly.<-
->The year will be joyful for Ceres <note>The Roman goddess of the harvest and grain.</note> and fertile for Bacchus <note>The Roman god of wine and the vine.</note> too,<-
->If the King is like this bird of the sea. <note>The poem argues that just as the halcyon bird requires a calm sea to raise its young, a kingdom requires a peaceful ruler for agriculture and society to flourish.</note><-

<vocab>**Peace** <note>original: "Pax"</note>, **abundance** <note>original: "vbertas"</note>, **Halcyons** <note>original: "Hælciones"</note>, **Ceres** <note>Goddess of grain</note>, **Bacchus** <note>God of wine</note>, **of the bird** <note>original: "alitis"</note></vocab>

<summary>This page uses the myth of the Halcyon bird, which nested on calm seas, as a metaphor for the prosperity that follows a peaceful reign. It suggests that when a ruler maintains tranquility, agriculture (represented by grain and wine) will thrive.</summary>
<keywords>Peace, abundance, Halcyon days, Ceres, Bacchus, prosperity, kingship, agriculture</keywords>

## That it is a sin for the learned to speak ill of the learned. <note>original: "Doctos doctis obloqui nefas esse." This title sets a moral boundary for intellectual discourse, suggesting that professional jealousy or public criticism among scholars is a violation of divine or natural law.</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration contained within a highly decorative architectural border. The top of the frame features a lion's head mask amid ornamental flourishes. The side borders are rendered as twisted Solomonic columns, each topped with a caryatid figure. The central scene depicts classical ruins, including broken arches and columns. A large bird's nest is balanced on top of a solitary standing pillar, and another bird is seen in flight in the distance. At the bottom of the border, a cartouche contains the initials "PV", identifying the artist as Pierre Vase (Peter Woeriot).</image-desc>

->Alas, cruel Progne, <note>In Greek mythology, Progne was a princess transformed into a swallow. In the context of this emblem, the swallow represents a talkative or predatory person.</note> why do you snatch the song-singing Cicada,<-
->And prepare such a terrible feast for your children?<-
->Should a chirping bird harm a chirping creature? A harbinger of spring harm another of spring?<-
->A guest harm a guest, <note>The cicada and the swallow were both considered "guests" of the summer/spring season in classical literature.</note> and a winged one harm a feathered bird?<-
->Therefore, cast aside this prey; for among those with musical hearts, it is the greatest<-
->Sin for one to perish by the bite of another.<-

<note>This emblem is from Andrea Alciato's "Book of Emblems." The "musical hearts" (musica pectora) refers to poets, scholars, and those dedicated to the Muses. The cicada was a common symbol for the poet in antiquity, prized for its tireless singing.</note>

<summary>This page uses the metaphor of a swallow (Progne) preying on a cicada to argue that scholars and poets—who share a common "musical" nature—should refrain from attacking or criticizing one another.</summary>
<keywords>Knowledge, Learning, Ethics, Alciato, Poetry, Scholars, Progne, Cicada, Pierre Vase</keywords>

### Eloquence is superior to Strength. <note>original: "Eloquentia Fortitudine præstantiior."</note>

He holds a bow in his left hand; his right hand carries a sturdy club,
And the skin of the Nemean lion <note>The Nemean lion was a monster slain by Hercules as his first labor; its pelt became his characteristic armor.</note> covers his naked body.
Is this, then, the face of Hercules? One thing does not seem right:
That he is old, and age has turned his temples gray.
And what of this? His tongue is pierced by delicate chains,
By which he easily draws along men with pierced ears?
Is it not because the Gauls say that Hercules <note>The text uses "Alciden," a name for Hercules referring to his grandfather Alcaeus.</note> was outstanding
Through his tongue, rather than his might, and thus gave laws to the nations?
"Arms yield to the toga," <note>original: "Cedunt arma togæ." A famous Roman proverb by Cicero, suggesting that military force is secondary to civil diplomacy and the rule of law.</note> and one powerful in eloquence
Draws even the hardest of hearts to his own will.

<summary>This page presents the emblem of the Gallic Hercules, an elderly figure who leads followers not by force, but by golden chains connecting his tongue to their ears. It serves as an allegory for the power of rhetoric and persuasion to govern society more effectively than physical violence.</summary>
<keywords>Eloquence, Hercules, Gallic Hercules, Rhetoric, Persuasion, Strength, Civil Law, Pierre Vase, Emblemata</keywords>

# Eloquence is difficult. <note>original: "Facundia difficilis."</note>

Fame has it that Mercury gave this to the man from Ithaca <note>Odysseus (Ulysses), the King of Ithaca and hero of the Odyssey.</note> 
as an antidote against the drugged cups of Aeaean Circe <note>Circe was a sorceress on the island of Aeaea who turned men into beasts; Mercury gave Odysseus a herb to remain human.</note>.
They call it Moly <note>A mythical herb with a black root and white flower.</note>; it is pulled from the earth with difficulty due to its black root,
But its flower is gleaming, and it is just like milk. <note>The Latin "purpureus" here suggests a bright or brilliant quality rather than just the color purple, as it is compared to the whiteness of milk.</note>
The brilliance of speech and eloquence attracts all people,
But for many, such a great thing is a work of much labor.

<image-desc>A small woodcut vignette depicting a horned satyr or demon head within a decorative banner.</image-desc>

## All the most ancient things are fictions. <note>original: "Antiquissima quæque commentitia."</note>

### A DEFENSE <note>original: "APOLOGESIS."</note>

O old man of Pellene <note>Pellene was a city in Greece; the poet addresses Proteus, the prophetic sea-god.</note>, Proteus, whose form is theatrical,
You who now bear the limbs of a man, and now the limbs of a wild beast,
Tell me, what reason or appearance turns you into all things,
So that no certain shape remains for you in your shifting?
"I display the signs of antiquity and of the earliest age:
About which everyone dreams according to their own judgment." <note>This suggests that ancient myths are like Proteus—constantly changing shape depending on how the interpreter "dreams" or views them.</note>

<vocab><term>Eloquence</term> (Facundia), <term>Mercury</term> (Mercurius), <term>The Ithacan</term> (Ithacus/Odysseus), <term>Moly</term> (a magical herb), <term>Circe</term> (the sorceress), <term>Proteus</term> (a shape-shifting sea god), <term>Apology</term> (Apologesis/Defense), <term>Knowledge</term> (Scientia), <term>Pellenean</term> (Pellenæe).</vocab>

<summary>This page explores the dual nature of knowledge and speech: first, it uses the herb Moly to symbolize how eloquence is a divine gift that is difficult to master; second, it uses the shape-shifter Proteus to explain how ancient myths are interpreted differently by every generation.</summary>

### The Insignia of Poets. <note>original: "Insignia Poëtarum."</note>

<image-desc>A woodcut emblem within an ornate architectural border. The border features two herms—figures with human torsos on rectangular pedestals—on the left and right, and a grotesque mask at the top center. ->There are those who carry the bird of Jupiter <note>The eagle, a symbol of power and empire.</note> on family shields,<-
->There are those for whom the Serpent or the Lion bears their signs.<-
->But let these dreadful animals flee the wax tablets <note>The "wax" (ceras) refers to the writing tablets used by ancient poets to draft their verses.</note> of the Bards <term>Vatum</term>,<-
->And let the beautiful Swan <term>Olor</term> uphold their learned lineage.<-
->This bird is sacred to Phoebus <note>Apollo, the god of music, poetry, and the sun.</note> and is a native of our region.<-
->Once a king <note>A reference to the myth of Cycnus, a king who was transformed into a swan while mourning the death of his friend Phaethon.</note>, he still preserves his ancient titles.<-

<summary>This page presents the swan as the ideal heraldic symbol for poets, contrasting it with the violent animals (eagles, lions, serpents) used by the nobility to represent military or political power. It links the poet’s craft to the divine through Apollo and the mythological history of King Cycnus.</summary>

### Music is a Care to the Gods. <note>original: "Musicam Dijs curæ esse."</note>

->A man of Locri dedicated a cicada to you, O Delphic Phoebus <note>Apollo, the god of music, often called "Delphic" after his primary sanctuary at Delphi.</note>,<-
->Eunomus <note>A legendary musician from the Greek colony of Locri in Italy.</note> gave this, the beautiful token of his victory.<-
->He was competing with his plectrum <note>A small tool used to pluck the strings of a lyre.</note> against a Spartan foe,<-
->And the strings, struck by his thumb, were giving forth their sound.<-
->When a worn string began to screech with a hoarse buzzing,<-
->And to ruin the harmony and the proper melody,<-
->Then the sweet, chirping "bird" <note>The Latin "ales" (winged one/bird) is used here poetically to describe the cicada.</note> of the Lyre brought itself there—<-
->The cicada, that it might fill the broken string with its voice.<-
->Attracted, it descended from the high forest glades,<-
->So that it might bring its talkative aid to us.<-
->Therefore, so that the honor of your cicada might remain firm, O holy one,<-
->The bronze musician itself sits here upon the lyre. <note>Eunomus dedicated a bronze statue of his lyre with a cicada on it to Apollo to commemorate the miracle.</note><-

<summary>This page illustrates the divine favor shown to musicians through the myth of Eunomus, whose broken lyre string was replaced by the song of a cicada during a competition. It serves as an emblem of how the gods (specifically Apollo) support and complete the efforts of the learned.</summary>
<keywords>Eunomus, Apollo, Phoebus, music, cicada, lyre, Locri, divine intervention, harmony, competition</keywords>

->The letter kills, but the spirit gives life. <note>original: "Litera occidit, spiritus viuificat." A quotation from 2 Corinthians 3:6, here applied to the dual nature of learning.</note><-

<image-desc>A central woodcut is enclosed within an elaborate Renaissance-style decorative frame featuring strapwork, scrolls, and a bearded grotesque mask at the top center. The main illustration depicts the mythological founding of Thebes by Cadmus. In the background, Cadmus is seen plowing a field with oxen and sowing the dragon's teeth. When Cadmus entrusted the viper’s teeth to the fields,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;And sowed those dreadful seeds in Aonian <note>Aonian: Relating to Aonia, a region in Boeotia, Greece, where Cadmus founded the city of Thebes.</note> soil:
A shield-bearing cohort of earth-born men arose,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Who fell by one another’s hostile hands.
Those who escaped did so at the warning of Tritonis <note>Tritonis: An epithet for the goddess Pallas Athena (Minerva), the goddess of wisdom.</note>;
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Casting aside their weapons, peace was granted and their right hands were joined.
The son of Agenor <note>Agenorides: Cadmus, the son of King Agenor of Tyre, whom Greek myth credits with introducing the Phoenician alphabet to the Greeks.</note> was the first to hand down the letters <note>elementa: literally "elements," referring here to the foundational letters of the alphabet.</note> and signs to teachers,

Many contradictions <note>original: "contraria plurima."</note> vex their disciples,
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Which cannot be resolved except by the help of Pallas <note>Pallas Athena (Minerva).</note>.

### The Sayings of the Seven Sages. <note>original: "Dicta septem sapientum." These were legendary figures from the 7th and 6th centuries BCE known for their practical wisdom.</note>

*Keep these in mind: if you wish to depict the sayings*
*Of the Seven Sages, and honor them with pictures.*
*Moderation is best in all things (as Cleobulus says);*
*The weighing of the scales, or the level, teaches this.*
*Chilon the Spartan commanded everyone to know himself;*
*Mirrors held in the hands, or glass, will provide this.*
*As Periander of Corinth said: "Add a bridle to your anger";*
*Pennyroyal <note>original: "Pulegium." A medicinal herb used in antiquity to soothe the spirit or clear the head.</note> held to the nostrils will effect this.*
*But Pittacus said, "Nothing in excess." Those who dissolve*
*Fennel flower <note>original: "gith." Also known as black cumin; its bitter taste caused a puckered or "contracted" mouth, symbolizing restraint.</note> in a puckered mouth say the same.*
*Solon commands us to look to the end. The final boundary stone*
*Of the fields <note>original: "Terminus." The Roman god of boundaries and the physical stones marking property; legend says Terminus refused to move his stone even to accommodate a temple for Jupiter.</note> would not yield itself even to great Jove.*
*Alas, how true is Bias: "There is a great abundance of evil";*
*Make a Sardinian rider sit upon a wild ram. <note>A "Sardinian rider" (sardus eques) on a "musmon" (wild sheep) was a classical proverb for a wretched or mismatched situation, illustrating the ubiquity of misfortune.</note>*
*“Do not be a surety,” <note>original: "Ne praes esto." A warning against acting as a financial guarantor for another person's debts.</note> said Thales; for thus, smeared with bird-lime,*
*The lapwing <note>original: "parra."</note> and the bee-eater <note>original: "merops."</note> draw their companion into the snares.*

# I G N O R A N C E.
## On driving away ignorance. <note>original: "Submouendam ignorantiam"</note>
->A reasoning<- <note>original Greek: "διαλόγισμ☉" (dialogismos). Refers to a logical consideration or a philosophical discourse.</note>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut illustration depicting a Sphinx. The creature has the head and chest of a woman, large feathered wings, and the lower body and legs of a lion. It stands in profile in a landscape filled with rocky terrain and ruins of classical architecture, including a broken stone arch and fallen masonry. The illustration is set within the primary decorative border of the page.</image-desc>

What monster is this? It is the Sphinx. Why does she have a maiden’s fair face,
The wings of birds, and the legs of a lion?
The ignorance of things has assumed this appearance: for of so great
An evil, there is indeed a triple cause and origin.
There are those whom a flighty mind, there are those whom seductive pleasure,
And there are those whom proud hearts make unrefined <note>original: "rudes." Lacking in education, wisdom, or culture.</note>.
But for those who know what the Delphic letter <term>Delphic letter: Likely a reference to the Greek letter 'E' inscribed at the Temple of Apollo in Delphi, which was a subject of much philosophical speculation (notably by Plutarch) regarding its hidden meaning of "Thou Art" or "Know Thyself."</term> can achieve:
They cut the terrible throat of the headlong monster.
For that same being is man: two-footed, three-footed, and four-footed alike <note>The Sphinx's famous riddle asked what walks on four legs in the morning, two at noon, and three in the evening; the answer is Man (crawling as a baby, walking as an adult, using a cane in old age).</note>,
And the first laurel of the wise is to know the man <note>original: "nosse uirum." To understand human nature and the self.</note>.

<vocab>Ignorance, Sphinx, monster, Delphic letter, to know the man, Alciato</vocab>

<summary>This page presents an emblem of the Sphinx as a personification of Ignorance, identifying its triple causes as flightiness, pleasure, and pride. It teaches that the solution to ignorance is self-knowledge, as symbolized by the Sphinx's riddle and the Delphic oracle.</summary>
<keywords>Sphinx, Ignorance, riddle of the Sphinx, Delphic letter, self-knowledge, moral philosophy, Andrea Alciato, emblems</keywords>

## The mind, not the form, holds more power. <note>original: "Mentem, non formam plus pollere."</note>

<image-desc>An emblem illustration set within a highly ornate woodcut border featuring architectural motifs, scrollwork, grotesque masks, and clusters of fruit. The central woodcut depicts a fox sitting in a rural landscape with a small house and mountains in the background. The fox holds a lifelike human theatrical mask or sculpted head between its paws, examining it closely. The scene symbolizes the idea that outward beauty or form is hollow without intelligence (mind).</image-desc>

A fox, having entered the workshop <note>original: "pergulam"</note> of a Theater Manager <term>Theater Manager: A "Choragus" was a wealthy citizen in ancient Greece who financed and organized the dramatic choruses for plays.</term>,
Found a human head skillfully polished,
Crafted so elegantly that only the breath <note>original: "spiritus"</note>
Was missing; in every other respect it seemed to come to life.
When she had taken it up into her hands, she said:
"What a fine head this is, but it has no brain."

<sig>K 4</sig>

**Vocabulary:**
Ignorance <note>original: "Ignorantia"</note>, The mind <note>original: "Mentem"</note>, Theater Manager <note>original: "Choragus"</note>, fox <note>original: "uulpes"</note>, head <note>original: "caput"</note>, brain <note>original: "cerebrum"</note>

<summary>This page uses the fable of the fox and the mask to illustrate that outward beauty and craftsmanship are worthless if the "mind" or "brain" is absent. It warns against being an impressive but hollow vessel of ignorance.</summary>
<keywords>Aesop, fox, mask, mind vs form, intelligence, appearance, ignorance, theater, Choragus</keywords>

### The Unlearned Rich Man <note>original: "Diues indoctus"</note>

## MARRIAGE.
### On wifely loyalty. <note>original: "In fidem vxoriam"</note>

<image-desc>A central woodcut illustration shows a man and a woman seated together beneath the shade of a tree. The man, on the left, wears a wide hat and a heavy cloak over puffed sleeves. The woman, on the right, is in a high-waisted dress. They are holding hands (the dexterarum iunctio). A small dog rests at the woman's feet. The background reveals a landscape with a river and a town featuring a church spire and ruins.</image-desc>

*Behold the maiden, who is joined to the man by her right hand.* <term>right hand: This refers to the "dextrarum iunctio," the ritual joining of right hands which was the central gesture of a Roman wedding, symbolizing union and contract.</term> *Behold*
*How she sits: and how the little dog* <note>original: "catulus" (a puppy or small dog). In Renaissance art, the dog is a common symbol of "Fides" or Fidelity.</note> *plays before her feet?*
*This is the very image of Faithfulness: but if the passion of Venus* <term>Venus: The goddess of love. The author suggests that while loyalty is the foundation, love and desire are also necessary for a fruitful marriage.</term> *should lead it forth:*
*A branch of apples in the left hand will not be out of place.*
*For apples are the fruit of Venus; thus Hippomenes defeated the daughter of Schoeneus* <term>daughter of Schoeneus: Atalanta, a mythical huntress who refused to marry unless a suitor could beat her in a race. Hippomenes won by dropping three golden apples, which she stopped to pick up.</term>,
*And thus did Galatea* <term>Galatea: A sea-nymph. In classical poetry, Galatea is described as throwing an apple at her lover to provoke a pursuit, using the fruit as a symbol of romantic invitation.</term> *seek her husband.*

**Vocabulary:**
Marriage, Wifely loyalty, little dog <note>original: "catulus"</note>, Venus, Apples <note>original: "Poma"</note>, Atalanta <note>original: "Schoneida"</note>, Hippomenes, Galatea.

<summary>This emblem illustrates the dual nature of marriage: the "joining of hands" and the dog symbolize legal and moral fidelity, while the mention of Venus's apples represents the role of attraction and desire in courtship.</summary>
<keywords>marriage, fidelity, Venus, Atalanta, Hippomenes, Galatea, symbolism, courtship</keywords>

-># M A R R I A G E <note>original: "MATRIMONIVM"</note><-

## That reverence is required in marriage.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration contained within an elaborate decorative border. The central image depicts a landscape with a river or coastal shore. In the foreground, a large gnarled tree trunk stands to the left. A snake (the viper) is coiled on the ground near the water's edge, while a sea eel (the murena) swims in the water towards it. In the background, a hilly landscape leads to a fortified castle atop a cliff. The ornate border features a winged face and floral swags at the top, two male telamones or caryatids on the sides, and a bottom panel showing two muscular men pulling ropes attached to a wheeled platform featuring a central leonine mask.</image-desc>

*When she burns with the fire of Love,* <term>Love: original "Venerem" (Venus), the goddess of love, here used to represent the natural instinct or desire to mate.</term> *the Viper*
*stations herself upon the shore of the sea: and vomits the deadly poison from her stomach.* <note>The author draws on a legend from ancient natural history (found in writers like Pliny and St. Ambrose) which claimed that a land viper would mate with a sea eel (murena). To do so safely, the viper was said to spit out its venom before the encounter.</note>
*Calling to the Sea-eel, she raises a mighty hissing,*
*and suddenly that creature seeks the embrace of her mate.*
*The greatest reverence is owed to the marriage bed;* <term>marriage bed: original "thalamo" (thalamus), referring to the inner bridal chamber or the spiritual union of the couple.</term> *a spouse*
*must also offer mutual devotion and compliance to their partner.* <note>original: "obsequium," a term implying a willing deference or the spirit of service toward one another.</note>

<vocab>marriage, reverence (respect), Viper, Sea-eel, Love (Venus), marriage bed, devotion (compliance)</vocab>

<summary>This page uses the legendary mating of a viper and a sea eel—where the viper supposedly discards its venom—as an allegory for how spouses should set aside their anger and harshness to respect the sanctity of marriage.</summary>
<keywords>marriage, reverence, respect, viper, sea eel, murena, mutual devotion, symbolism, folklore</keywords>

### On a fruitfulness <term>fruitfulness: original "fœcunditatem," referring to the ability to produce many offspring or much fruit.</term>
### harmful to itself.

> A mockery for boys throwing stones, a farmer's care
> placed me here, a walnut tree <note>original: "nucem"</note>, at the crossroads.
> Standing tall with mangled branches and stripped bark <note>original: "perstrictóque... libro"</note>,
> I am pelted from every side by eager slings.
> What could happen to a barren tree that is more foul? Alas,
> unhappy me: I bear fruit only for my own ruin <note>The poem is a variation on a theme from the "Nux" (The Walnut Tree), a work traditionally attributed to Ovid. It serves as an allegory for how success, wealth, or a large family can sometimes attract unwanted attention, envy, or physical exhaustion.</note>.

### The love of children. <note>original: "Amor filiorum"</note>

> *Before the spring day arrives, amidst northern chills, the grey wood-pigeon*
> *builds her nest, and warms her early eggs.*
> *So that her chicks may lie more softly, she plucks the feathers from her own wings;*
> *stripped bare by this, she herself succumbs to the winter frost.*
> *Does this not put you to shame, Medea <note>original: "Colchi," referring to Medea of Colchis, the mythological figure who murdered her own children to spite her husband.</note>, or you, wicked Procne <note>In Greek myth, Procne killed her son Itys to take revenge on her husband, Tereus.</note>? For this*
> *bird meets her death out of love for her own offspring.*

## The piety of children toward their parents. <note>original: "Pietas filiorum in parentes." The Latin "Pietas" implies more than just religious "piety"; it refers to the Roman virtue of duty, devotion, and loyalty to family, country, and the gods.</note>

# A woman’s reputation, not her beauty, ought to be widely known. <note>The author argues that a woman's "fame"—her character and good name—is more important than her physical appearance being "vulgatam" (common or public).</note>

### On the statue of modesty. <note>original: "In pudoris statuam." This poem refers to a legend about Penelope, the wife of Ulysses, who was forced to choose between her father and her husband.</note>

->Penelope, having been betrothed, desired to follow Ulysses,<-
->Unless her father Icarius preferred to keep her with himself.<-
->The one offers Ithaca, the other Sparta. The young woman remains anxious.<-
->On one side her father, on the other the mutual love for her husband urges her.<-
->Therefore, sitting down, she veils her face and covers her eyes.<-
->These were the signs of bashful modesty.<-
->By this, Icarius recognized that Ulysses was preferred to him,<-
->And he established an altar to Modesty <note>original: "pudori"</note> in this likeness.<-

### A bride married to a diseased man. <note>original: "Nupta contagioso." This poem uses a gruesome classical metaphor to criticize fathers who marry their daughters off for money to men who are physically or morally decaying.</note>

->May the gods grant better things to the pious! Why, Mezentius,<-
->do you drive me thus? Because a son-in-law was bought for you with a dowry—<-
->A man whom the French itch <note>original: "Gallica scabies." A common Early Modern term for syphilis, then known as the "French disease."</note>, and the dire mentagra <note>A skin disease, often affecting the face or chin, described by Roman writers.</note> burns.<-
->What else is this (tell me, cruel father)<-
->than joining dead bodies to living bodies,<-
->and renewing the savage deeds of the Etruscan leader? <note>The <term>Etruscan leader</term> refers to the tyrant Mezentius from Virgil's Aeneid, who famously executed people by binding a living person face-to-face with a rotting corpse until they died of the infection.</note><-

<summary>This page presents two poetic emblems on marriage: the first celebrates Penelope's modest choice of her husband over her father, while the second uses the myth of the tyrant Mezentius to condemn fathers who marry their daughters to diseased men for profit.</summary>

->## INSIGNIA.<-
->### To the illustrious Maximilian, Duke of Milan. On the emblem of the insignia of the Duchy of Milan. <note>The "insignia" refers to the coat of arms of the Sforza and Visconti families, known as the *Biscione*.</note><-

An infant leaping forth from the jaws of a winding snake,
Is the noble coat of arms of your family line.
We have seen that the King of Pella <note>original: "Pellaeum... regem" — a reference to Alexander the Great, born in Pella, Macedonia.</note> bore such coins,
And with them, he celebrated the fame of his race.
For he shows himself born of Ammon <note>Jupiter Ammon; legend claimed Alexander was fathered by the god in the form of a serpent.</note>, his mother having been deceived by the guise of a serpent,
Proving he is the offspring of divine seed.
The child emerges from the mouth; they say that some snakes give birth <note>original: "enitier"</note> in this way,
Or is it because Pallas <note>The goddess Athena.</note> was likewise born from the head of Jove?

<vocab><term>Insignia</term>: Heraldic symbols or emblems of office. <term>Maximilianus Dux Mediolanensis</term>: Maximilian Sforza (1493–1530), who was Duke of Milan briefly during the Italian Wars. <term>Mediolanum</term>: The Latin name for Milan. <term>Emblema</term>: An emblem, a combination of image and text used to convey a moral or political lesson. <term>Biscione</term>: The specific name for the man-eating serpent in Milanese heraldry. <term>Pellaeus rex</term>: "The King of Pella," referring to Alexander the Great. <term>Ammon</term>: An Egyptian and Libyan deity later identified with Zeus/Jupiter. <term>Pallas</term>: Another name for Athena, the goddess of wisdom. <term>Iouis</term>: Jove or Jupiter, the king of the gods.</vocab>

# Milan.
<note>original: "Mediolanum"</note>

->The ram gives a sign to the Bituriges, the Sow <note>original: "Sucula" — a small sow or piglet.</note> to the Haedui.<-
->To these peoples the origin of my fatherland is owed,<-
->Which the maidens called the Holy Land, Mediolanum:<-
->For this is how the ancient Gallic tongue sounds.<-
->Minerva was once worshipped here; now, where the deity has been<-
->changed, it is Saint Tecla’s, before the house of the Virgin Mother. <note>A reference to the site of the ancient temple of Minerva in Milan, which later became the site of the early Christian Basilica of Santa Tecla, located near the current Milan Cathedral (the Duomo).</note><-
->For this city, the symbol is a wool-bearing Sow, a creature of two forms:<-
->With sharp bristles on one side, and soft wool on the other. <note>The "Woolly Sow" (Latin: *Sus laniata*) is a legendary creature associated with the founding of Milan by the Gaul Bellovesus. The name "Mediolanum" was traditionally (though incorrectly) etymologized as *medio-lana* or "half-wool."</note><-

# Never procrastinate.
<note>original: "Nunquàm procrastinandum"</note>

->The elk <note>original: "alce" — a pun on the author's name, Alciato.</note> supports the insignia of the Alciato family,<-
->And in its hooves it carries the motto: "Never putting off." <note>original Greek: "μηδὲν ἀναβαλλόμενος" (*mēden anaballomenos*).</note><-
->It is well known that Alexander the Great replied thus to one asking<-
->How he had accomplished so many deeds in such a short time:<-
->"By never being willing to delay," he said. This is what the Elk indicates:<-
->So brave that one wonders if it might be even swifter.<-

# TREES.
<note>original: "ARBORES"</note>

## The Cypress.
<note>original: "Cupressus"</note>

> The shape of the turning-post <note>original: "metæ" — the conical pillars marking the turns in a Roman circus race-course.</note> and the name of the Cypress indicate
> That its own people should be treated with equal standing.

->ANOTHER ON THE SAME.<-

> The Cypress is a tree for the dead, a monument for the high-born,
> Just as Wild Celery <note>original: "Apium" — often used in antiquity for funeral wreaths for the common people.</note> is accustomed to adorning the common folk with its foliage.

->ANOTHER ON THE SAME.<-

> Its foliage is beautiful, and the leaves are arranged in a lovely order,
> But this beautiful foliage bears no fruit.

## The Oak.
<note>original: "Quercus"</note>

> The oak is pleasing to Jove, <note>Jupiter, the king of the gods.</note> who preserves and nourishes us;
> A crown of oak is given to one who saves a fellow citizen. <note>The "Civic Crown" (Corona Civica) was one of the highest military honors in Rome, awarded to a soldier who saved the life of a comrade in battle.</note>

->ANOTHER ON THE SAME.<-

> It nourished the ancients with acorns; now it is useful only for its shade.
> Even so, the tree of Jove remains dutiful in its service.

## The Laurel.
<note>original: "Laurus"</note>

> The laurel, possessing foreknowledge of what is to come, brings signs of health.
> Placed beneath a pillow, it makes dreams come true. <note>A reference to the ancient practice of "incubation," where sleeping on laurel leaves was believed to induce prophetic or truthful dreams.</note>

The laurel is owed to Charles, <note>Charles V (1500–1558), Holy Roman Emperor, whose military campaigns often drew comparisons to Roman triumphs.</note> now that the Carthaginians <note>original: "Poenis" — literally the Phoenicians/Carthaginians, here a poetic reference to the North African forces defeated by Charles V at Tunis in 1535.</note> have been overcome:

### The Fir-tree.
<note>original: "Abies"</note>

The fir-tree, suited for the seas, <note>Fir wood was prized in antiquity for shipbuilding, particularly for masts and hulls, due to its lightness and height.</note> is produced on high mountains:
It also offers the greatest convenience in adverse circumstances.

### The Pitch Pine.
<note>original: "Picea"</note>

But because the pitch pine sends forth no shoots from its stump,
It is the sign of a man who dies without offspring.

### The Quince.
<note>original: "Cotonea"</note>

The ancient Solon <note>A famous Athenian lawmaker (c. 638 – c. 558 BC) who introduced various social and political reforms.</note> is said to have decreed
That Cydonian apples <note>original: "Poma Cydonia" — quinces, which were native to Cydonia on the island of Crete.</note> ought to be given to new brides.
Since they are pleasing to the mouth and stomach, so that the breath from them
Is sweet, a charming grace remains in the mouth.

### The Holm Oak.
<note>original: "Ilex"</note>

Because the holm oak <note>An evergreen oak tree known for its very hard, brittle wood.</note> breaks itself due to excessive hardness,
It holds the symbols of civil sedition. <note>An allegory suggesting that rigid, uncompromising natures lead to internal social strife and fragmentation.</note>

### The Ivy.
<note>original: "Hedera"</note>

The little tree of ivy never withers; they say that Cissus
Gave it as a gift to the boy Bacchus: <note>In mythology, Cissus was a youth who transformed into ivy while dancing for the god of wine.</note>
Wandering and bold, tawny with golden clusters,
It is green on the outside, while paleness possesses the rest.
Hence, poets <note>original: "uates"</note> encircle their temples with suitable wreaths:
They grow pale from their studies, yet their lasting praise remains green.

<summary>This page explores the symbolic meanings of various trees and plants, linking the fir to seafaring, the quince to marriage laws, and ivy to the eternal fame of poets. It also includes a contemporary political reference to Charles V's victory in North Africa.</summary>
<keywords>Charles V, Tunis, Fir-tree, Pitch Pine, Quince, Solon, Holm Oak, Sedition, Ivy, Bacchus, Poets, Symbolism</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page concludes the botanical section of Andrea Alciato’s *Emblemata*. Following the previous entries on the Fir, Pine, and Quince, this page explores the symbolic and moral associations of the Boxwood, Citron, Poplar, Willow, Almond, and Mulberry trees, drawing heavily on classical mythology and Greek etymology.</meta>

<page-num>64</page-num>
<header>T R E E S.</header>

<image-desc>A rectangular woodcut border frames the text. The top section consists of a central cartouche surrounded by lush fruit and foliage. The sides feature muscular male figures (atlantes) stacked vertically, supporting the top. The bottom section depicts two reclining figures on either side of a central decorative bowl filled with fruit.</image-desc>

### The Box Tree.
<note>original: "Buxus"</note>

The box tree, ever green and with a curly crown,
From which a flute <note>original: "fistula" — a pipe or flute, often made of boxwood due to its density.</note> is made with varied notes,
Is a tree suited for tender delights and lovers.
It possesses a paleness, and every lover grows pale. <note>In classical poetry, such as Ovid, paleness was considered the characteristic color of a lover.</note>

### The Citron.
<note>original: "Malus medica" — literally the "Median Apple," identifying the citron, which was thought to have originated in Media (modern-day Iran).</note>

These are the golden apples of Venus: <note>The citron was sometimes identified with the golden apples of the Hesperides.</note> their pleasant bitterness
Points the way. To the Greeks, love is thus "bittersweet." <note>original: "γλυκυπιϰϱος" (glykypikros) — a term famously used by the poet Sappho to describe the dual nature of love.</note>

### The White Poplar.
<note>original: "Populus alba"</note>

Since the two-colored poplar adorns the hair of Hercules, <note>According to myth, Hercules wore a crown of poplar leaves when he descended to the Underworld; the side touching his forehead stayed light, while the outer side was darkened by the smoke of Hades.</note>
It alternates the turns of time: both night and day.

### The Willow.
<note>original: "Salix"</note>

Because Homer called the willow "fruit-destroying," <note>original: "frugiperdem" — Alciato refers to the *Odyssey* (10.510), where willows are called *olesikarpos* because they shed their seeds before they ripen.</note>
He likens it to men of Clitorian habits. <note>original: "Clitorijs" — a reference to the town of Clitor in Arcadia. In Greek lore, the waters there were said to cause a distaste for wine, but "Clitorian habits" here likely implies a sterile or unproductive lifestyle, matching the "fruit-dropping" willow.</note>

### The Almond Tree.
<note>original: "Amygdalus"</note>

Why, almond tree, do you send forth flowers before your leaves in such haste?
I dislike youths <note>original: "pupillos" — literally "wards" or "pupils," referring to children or students.</note> of a too precocious nature. <note>The almond is the first tree to bloom in spring, making it a symbol of haste or premature development.</note>

### The Mulberry.
<note>original: "Morus"</note>

But the mulberry is later, never budding until the frost has passed:
And being wise, it bears a false name. <note>A pun on the Greek word "mōros" (foolish). Though called "Morus," the tree is considered the wisest (*sapientissima*) because it waits for the safety of warm weather to bloom.</note>

->T H E   E N D.<-

<summary>This page concludes Alciato’s botanical emblems, using various trees to illustrate human conditions such as the "bittersweet" nature of love, the wisdom of patience, and the dangers of precocity.</summary>
<keywords>Boxwood, Citron, Poplar, Willow, Almond, Mulberry, Hercules, Venus, Homer, Bittersweet, Precociousness, Symbolism</keywords>

<meta>This title page marks the beginning of the primary text, succeeding the fragmentary notes of the preceding page. It introduces the Spanish translation of Andrea Alciato’s "Emblemata," the foundational work of the emblem book genre.</meta>

# THE EMBLEMS <note>The emblem book was a popular Renaissance literary genre that combined symbolic illustrations with short, moralizing poems and mottos to teach philosophical lessons.</note>

## OF ALCIATO <note>original: "Alciati," the Latin genitive form of the author's name, Andrea Alciato (1492–1550), the Italian jurist who created this genre.</note>

<summary>This is the title page for a Spanish edition of Andrea Alciato’s "Emblems," a highly influential collection of allegorical images and moral verses. The ornate, gold-tooled leather cover reflects the high value placed on this work by 16th and 17th-century collectors.</summary>

<keywords>Andrea Alciato, emblem book, Spanish translation, Renaissance humanism, allegory, bookbinding</keywords>
